The indents apply as they do without shapes, and the shapes are measured against the lines where they actually stand — with `-anchor top` the first baseline sits one ascender below `-at`, and a shape starting on that edge narrows the first line already. The shapes are not drawn — that is a separate call, and the text can just as well keep clear of something invisible. Where the shapes leave no line on the page at all, nothing is drawn and nothing is marked: with `-paginate` the text begins on the next page, with `-height max` the whole text comes back as `rest`; in a tagged document no empty paragraph element is written for such a page.

  **-avoidMargin** *d* holds the text off every avoided shape by that distance; a shape may state one of its own as a fourth element (`{circle {x y} r 7}`), which then wins. Without it the words touch the picture, which reads as a mistake however exact the geometry is.

  **-tag** *type* names what the text *is* in a tagged document — `P` unless said otherwise, `H1` for a heading, `Artifact` for what is outside the tree; **-expansion** *text* marks the string as an abbreviation and gives its expanded form. Both are described under "Structure and accessibility" below.

  **Soft hyphens are honoured.** U+00AD is not a character but a permission — this word may be broken here. Where the breaker takes the offer, a real hyphen is set at the end of the line; everywhere else the mark stays invisible, in the drawing and in the measurement alike, so the same string can be set in any width. What it costs is named below under **Hyphenation**: extracting such a line yields the hyphen as well, unless the document is tagged, and a standard face is not refused for carrying the mark.

  **-hyphenate** *0|1|tag* breaks the words the offers of the text do not cover, using the patterns of the language named. `0` is the default and means what it has always meant — no word is broken except by the character fallback; `1` uses the language the document declares with **language**; anything else is a language tag such as `de-AT`. Only the two literal values `0` and `1` are the switch, and every other value is a tag: `no` is a Tcl false *and* the RFC 3066 tag for Norwegian, so a boolean reading would have turned hyphenation off for the one language whose name says otherwise. The patterns have to be loaded first with **::tclpdf::hyphenate load**, and a language that is not loaded is refused by name — `TCLPDF HYPHENATE LANGUAGE` — rather than set unhyphenated in silence, because a document that is quietly not what was asked for is worse than one that stops. Accepted by **textLines** and **textHeight** as well, so a block is measured the way it will be set. A **table** takes it as a style key rather than as an option — `{hyphenate de-DE}` in **-style**, in a section style, in a column description or in a cell's own style — because a table can hold columns in different languages; see there.

  **-breakHyphen** *0|1* says that the `-` this line ends on is a break the caller made, not a character of the text — for whoever broke his lines himself and draws them one at a time. In a **tagged** document the hyphen is then shown inside a `Span` with an empty `ActualText`, exactly as **-width** does it for the lines it breaks itself, so extracting the line gives the word back whole (ISO 32000-1, 14.8.2.6); an untagged document is written as it always was. The default is `0`. It is refused where it cannot be true: together with **-width**, because there the line breaker decides per line and a caller could only say it for all of them, and on a string with no hyphen in it, because there is nothing to call a break. **textLines** and **textHeight** accept it and ignore it, so one option list still serves a measurement and a drawing. The line keeps the width **textWidth** measured for it: the kerning pairs that straddle the hyphen are put back where the split lost them, which a caller who cut the string himself and made two **text** calls could not do. A **table** sets it for its own cells and needs nothing said. Which lines of a paragraph carry such a hyphen is asked for with **textLines -hyphens 1**, which is the other half of this.

**Where a line breaks.** A line breaks at ASCII white space — the space, a tab, a carriage return; a tab or a stray CR is set as the space that joins two words — and after the spaces UAX #14 lets a line break at: the Ogham space mark U+1680, en quad to hair space (U+2000–U+2006, U+2008–U+200A), the medium mathematical space U+205F and the ideographic space U+3000 (class BA), and the zero width space U+200B (class ZW). Where a line ends on one of these the character is dropped like a space; inside a line it is a character of the text — measured and set with its own glyph width, refused by a face that has no glyph for it, the standard fourteen among them — and never stretched. The zero width space is the exception: like the soft hyphen it never reaches a font, so it breaks where it stands, shows nothing and is set by every face. The byte order mark U+FEFF is dropped the same way — nothing to draw, no glyph asked of the face, by the standard fourteen as by an embedded face — instead of being refused as an Arabic mark or as a character outside WinAnsiEncoding. The no-break spaces U+00A0, U+2007 and U+202F are glue (class GL): the breaker never breaks at them, so `12 €` stays on one line with its unit. Every other space of Unicode — U+2028, U+2029 and U+0085 among them — is a character of the word it stands in. Justification distributes the gap over the U+0020 characters of the line only, because that is what word spacing reaches (ISO 32000-1 9.3.3): in a justified line a no-break space keeps the width of a space and a thin space stays thin while the word spaces around them grow. The rest a height limit hands back is the tail of the string, so a thin space inside it is still a thin space. Example `01.10` sets the same amounts with a no-break and with a thin space side by side.

**Hyphenation.** Two things can offer a break inside a word: the soft hyphens the text already carries, and the patterns of `tclpdf::hyphenate` where **-hyphenate** asked for them. Where a word carries marks of its own those win outright — it is one source or the other, never both, because whoever wrote the marks knew that word and mixing the two would produce breaks neither of them asked for. The hyphen that appears at the break is a real one, U+002D, so the line ends the way a reader expects; in a **tagged** document it sits in a `Span` with an empty `ActualText`, which is how ISO 32000-1, 14.8.2.6 says to tell a reader that the character is not part of the text, so extracting the line gives the word back whole. An untagged document is written exactly as it was before, bracket and all left out. A hyphen the text brought itself — `E-Mail` — is never bracketed and never broken at.

**tclpdf ships no hyphenation patterns**, and that is a licence decision rather than an omission. Every published pattern set carries terms of its own — the German ones LGPL over LPPL, the American English ones BSD-style over the plain TeX table, others the LaTeX Project Public License — and this package is MIT; bundling them would make the smallest of those the licence every user has to read. The caller loads the file, from wherever that machine keeps it (`/usr/share/hyphen/` on most Linux systems, the LibreOffice dictionary extensions on macOS and Windows), and the licence stays with the file.

**The three commands below are package commands rather than document methods**, and they live in a module nothing loads by itself: a script that hyphenates needs `package require tclpdf::hyphenate` of its own. A document loads it too when a block asks for **-hyphenate**, but the patterns have to be loaded before that, so the require comes first either way.

**::tclpdf::hyphenate load** *tag* *path* ?**-left** *n*? ?**-right** *n*? ?**-min** *n*? ?**-exceptions** *words*?

: Reads a pattern file for a language. *tag* is RFC 3066, the same spelling **language** takes; *path* is a libhyphen `.dic` file — the format LibreOffice, Hunspell and the `hyphen` library install, `hyph_de_DE.dic` and its fifty siblings — which names its own encoding on its first line and its own minima in the lines after it. A file that carries several pattern tables separated by `NEXTLEVEL` is read as its **last** table: the earlier ones drive a compound algorithm of libhyphen's own, the last one is the plain Liang table, and Liang is what this is. Loading a tag again replaces it.

  **-left** and **-right** are how many letters must stay on either side of a break, and they are what keeps `Abend` from being set as `A-bend`. Without them what the file says is used, and without that the plain TeX defaults 2 and 3. **Pass `-left 2 -right 2` for German:** `hyph_de_DE.dic` states `COMPOUNDLEFTHYPHENMIN` and `COMPOUNDRIGHTHYPHENMIN` but neither of the two that matter here, and a right minimum of 3 refuses the two-letter endings German breaks off every day. **-min** is the shortest word broken at all, 5 unless said otherwise. **-exceptions** takes a list of words written with `-` at the breaks — `{Wachs-tu-be Ur-in-stinkt}` — which are looked up before the patterns and taken exactly as given, minima included; the `.dic` format has no exception block of its own, encoding its exceptions as whole-word patterns instead. A file that states a `LEFTHYPHENMIN` or `RIGHTHYPHENMIN` below 1 is **refused as it loads**, naming the file and the directive: a minimum of zero offers a break before the first letter, which is not a break at all, and the offer would otherwise reach the line breaker as a piece that consumes nothing of the word. Returns the tag.

**::tclpdf::hyphenate languages** ?*tag*?

: Without a tag, the languages that are loaded, in the spelling they were loaded under. With one, what is known about that language: `tag` — which may differ from the one asked for, see below —, `patterns`, `exceptions`, `left`, `right` and `min`. A tag that is not loaded raises `TCLPDF HYPHENATE LANGUAGE`, which is how a caller asks whether a language is available without guessing.

  A tag is looked up exactly first, then by its primary subtag: patterns loaded as `de` serve a document set in `de-AT`, and patterns loaded as `de-DE` serve one set in `de`. The tables of a language differ far less between its regions than they do between languages, and the alternative would be loading the same file under four names.

**::tclpdf::hyphenate word** *tag* *word*

: Where a word may be broken, as the list of pieces it falls into — `Sil ben tren nung`. For checking a pattern file and for showing what it does; the line breaker uses the same answer. The pieces always concatenate back to the word handed in, character for character, punctuation and quotes included, so they may be joined with a hyphen to print or used to cut the original string.

  **Decomposed text is hyphenated like composed text.** A word is canonically composed before the patterns see it and the breaks are mapped back onto the caller's characters, so `heru` + U+0308 + `ber` falls into `her über` exactly as `herüber` does — measured over the 12 561 words with umlauts in `hyph_de_DE.dic`, both spellings now break identically, where before a third of them differed. The minima count **letters**, and a combining mark is not one: a break never falls between a letter and its own mark. The limits of the table are worth knowing — Latin, Greek and Cyrillic compose, Latin Extended Additional (the Vietnamese tone marks) and polytonic Greek do not, and marks are read in the order they were written rather than sorted into canonical order first.

  A word that must **not** be broken comes back as a one-element list, which is deliberately the same answer as "no break was found": neither is a failure. Not broken are a word shorter than **-min**, a run of fewer letters than the minima allow, a word with a digit anywhere in it, a word written in capitals throughout, and anything shaped like a URL, an address or a file name — a word that still holds a dot, a colon, a slash or an at sign once the punctuation has been taken off its ends. A word that carries a hyphen of its own is broken **in** its parts but never **at** the hyphen it already has, or the line would end in two of them. And a word carrying a soft hyphen is handed back whole, because those marks win.

  Example `01.14` sets the same paragraph twice in the same column, with and without, and prints what each column's worst word space measured; `examples/assets/languages/README` says where the two pattern files came from and under which licences.

*doc* **leader** *left* *right* **-at** *{x y}* **-width** *w* ?**-fill** *"."*? ?**-gap** *d*? ?**-tag** *type*? ?*font options*?

: A row with two ends and a filled middle — a table of contents, a price list, a total:

  ~~~tcl
  $doc leader "3. Embedding fonts" "24" -at {20 100} -width 120
  # 3. Embedding fonts ........................... 24
  ~~~

  Both ends are measured and the space between them is filled with as many whole copies of **-fill** as fit — measured as the run is drawn, so `-spacing` and `-wordSpacing` are counted between the copies — holding **-gap** clear of each end (1 unit by default). The remainder stays in front of the right hand end, so the figures of several rows line up. **-fill** may be any string, and an empty one draws nothing at all — which is what a sum under a rule wants. Either end may be empty. Returns the y coordinate one line down, so rows stack without measuring again.

  **-direction rtl** turns the row round: the first argument is the *leading* end and belongs at the right edge, the second at the left, and the fill is measured from the other side. The alignment of each end follows, so a right-to-left row needs nothing but the option.

  It does not wrap: each end is one line. A left side too long for the width keeps its full length and the fill disappears, rather than moving the figure a reader is looking for. In a tagged document the row is **one** element and the fill is an artifact — a reader that spelled the dots out would say "dot dot dot dot" between every entry and its number. **-tag** names the element (`P` by default), and `-tag Artifact` — or the kind as a list, `-tag {Artifact Pagination Header}`, as `text` takes it — takes the whole row, ends and fill, out of the tree, which is what a running head is. **-width** is a positive number, **-gap** a distance of 0 or more.

*doc* **textPath** *string* **-segments** *{...}* ?**-align** *a*? ?**-offset** *d*? ?**-tag** *type*? ?*font options*?

: Sets one line of text along a path, cluster by cluster, each one turned by the direction the path takes at its own position. The segments are the ones **path** takes (`move`, `line`, `curve`, `close`), checked exactly as **path** checks them — an unknown kind, a first segment that is not a `move`, a wrong operand count or a non-number is refused with the same message; **-align** — `left`, `center` or `right`, and `centre` for `center` — places the string at the start, the middle or the end of the path, and **-offset** lifts the baseline off it — positive above, negative below (a number; anything else is refused). Returns the length of the path, which is what a caller measures a string against beforehand: glyphs that run past the end are dropped rather than piled up there. The path itself is not drawn.

  **A cluster, not a glyph.** A combining mark has no advance and no place of its own: it is set against the glyph it hangs on, and GPOS can only reach it while the two stand in the same run — handed to the path singly it loses its base and lands on the character after it. So a base and the marks that follow it travel together: they are turned by the tangent as **one** piece, the marks are drawn inside the base's own text object, and the path advances by the width of the **base**. Both spellings of a word therefore come out at the same place and the same size — measured on DejaVu Sans at 20 points on the curve of example `02.13-marks-on-a-path`, `Café Crème` spelled with U+00E9 and U+00E8 and the same words spelled with U+0301 and U+0300 are both 41.5444 mm wide on the same 179.5325 mm of path.

  **-spacing counts glyphs there, not clusters**, and that one is visible: the character spacing of PDF sits between every pair of glyphs, and a combining mark is a glyph, so the decomposed spelling gains one gap per mark — 49.3055 mm against 47.8944 mm at `-spacing 2` for the words above. `textWidth` counts it the same way, which is why **-align** stays exact for either spelling: what was measured and what is drawn are the same number.

  **-direction rtl** runs the glyphs backwards along the path and mirrors **-align** with them, exactly as it does on a straight baseline — including the numbers inside such a line, which keep their own order there too.

*doc* **pageNumbers -at** *{x y}* ?**-format** *"Page %n of %m"*? ?**-align** *a*? ?**-from** *n*? ?**-total** *n*? ?*font options*?

: Puts a page number on every page. `%n` is the number, `%m` the total. **-align** places the number on **-at** as `text` places a line: `left` (the default) starts there, `right` ends there, `center` is centred on it. It takes the *line* options as well as the font ones, so `-format "صفحة %n من %m" -direction rtl` sets a right-to-left page number with the two figures the right way round. The numbers are drawn when the document is written, not when the call is made — which is the only moment the total is known — so the call may come before the pages it numbers. **-from** leaves the leading pages unnumbered, **-total** states a total of its own for a document that is part of a larger set. **-total** is a whole number of 1 or more and **-align** one of `left`, `right`, `center`; both are refused at the call. Several calls are independent of each other: a number at the foot and a running title at the head are two of them. A character the face cannot set is refused at write time with the usual error code — its *position* then refers to the expanded label ("Page 3 of 12"), not to the format string, because the expanded label is what is being drawn.

*doc* **textWidth** *string* ?*font options*?

: The width of a string in the document unit. It takes the **font** options and nothing else: a layout option such as **-at**, **-width**, **-align**, **-tag** or **-hyphenate** is refused by name rather than accepted and ignored — a measurement that silently disregards half of what it was asked would be worse than one that stops, and the caller wanted **textLines** or **textHeight**.

*doc* **textHeight** *string* **-width** *w* ?*options*?

: The height a paragraph of that width would take — exactly what **text** advances by with the same options: the difference between the y it returns and the y it was given, indents, paragraph spacing, the ascender of `-anchor top` and the lines a shape pushes down included. It takes the options **text** takes, so one option list serves measuring and drawing; what only the drawing uses (`-align`, `-rotate`, `-tag`, `-expansion`) is accepted and changes nothing, and so are `-height`, `-paginate`, `-columns`, `-gutter` and `-balance` — a measurement has no page to fill; an unknown option is an error. With `-avoid` the call needs `-at`, because the shapes are page positions.

*doc* **textLines** *string* **-width** *w* ?*options*?

: The lines a paragraph would be broken into, with the same options and the same rules. Both calls also take the width as their first argument: `textLines $s 80` is the same as `textLines $s -width 80`.

  **-hyphens** *0|1* changes the answer: with `1` every element is a dictionary `{text … hyphen …}` instead — the line, and whether the `-` it ends on is a break the line breaker put there or a character of the text. A string cannot carry that difference and the caller cannot work it out afterwards: measured at one and the same width, `Die Betriebskoste-` ends a line on a hyphen made here and `die Nord-und Su-` holds one the text always had. It is the other half of **text -breakHyphen**, and the pair is what a caller needs who breaks his lines here and draws them one at a time — a break hyphen is not a character of the text (ISO 32000-1, 14.8.2.6) and has to be bracketed as such, or the line extracts as `Betriebskostenab-rechnung`:

  **-emergencyHyphen** *0|1* puts a hyphen on the break a word gets when it is longer than the column. Such a word has always been broken by character rather than let run past the edge; what this adds is the mark on the break, and the hyphen is measured **with** the piece rather than hung on after it, so the line stays inside the column — one character fewer, not one hyphen too wide. **Off by default**, and that is a decision rather than caution: the words that reach this fallback are as often a part number, a file path or a URL as a long word, and a hyphen inside one of those is not a typographic aid but a character the reader copies and a wrong value. A column too narrow to hold one letter *and* a hyphen drops the hyphen rather than the letter. `-hyphens 1` reports these breaks like any other, so **text -breakHyphen** can bracket them.

  ```
  foreach line [$doc textLines $body -width 60 -hyphenate de-DE -hyphens 1] {
      $doc text [dict get $line text] -at [list $x $y] -breakHyphen [dict get $line hyphen]
      set y [expr {$y + 5}]
  }
  ```

  The default is `0` and answers the list of strings it always did, so nothing that reads `foreach line [$doc textLines …]` changes. A dictionary rather than a two-element list because it says what it holds where it is read and can gain a key later without a caller noticing. **textHeight** takes the option and ignores it — one option list still serves the height and the lines — but refuses a value that is no boolean just as **textLines** does, under `TCLPDF TEXT HYPHENS`. **textWidth** refuses it by name like every other layout option, and **text** does not take it at all: it says what an answer looks like, and a drawing has no answer of that shape.

## Graphics

*doc* **line -from** *{x y}* **-to** *{x y}* ?**-stroke** *c*? ?**-width** *w*?

*doc* **rect -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-radius** *r*? ?**-fill** *c*? ?**-stroke** *c*?

*doc* **circle -at** *{x y}* **-radius** *r* ?**-fill** *c*?

*doc* **ellipse -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-fill** *c*?

*doc* **polygon -points** *{x y x y ...}* ?**-close** *1*? ?**-fill** *c*?

*doc* **curve -from** *{x y}* **-c1** *{x y}* **-c2** *{x y}* **-to** *{x y}*

*doc* **path -segments** *list* ?**-fill** *c*? ?**-stroke** *c*? ?**-rule** *evenodd*?

: The shapes. Common options are **-fill** and **-stroke** (a colour), **-width** (line width, 0 or more — 0 is the thinnest line the device can draw), **-dash** (a pattern: a list of lengths of 0 or more, not all of them zero; `none` or `solid` for an unbroken line), **-cap** (`butt`, `round` or `square`), **-join** (`miter`, `round` or `bevel`), **-miter** (the miter limit: how far a pointed join may reach before it is cut to a bevel; 1 or more, and 1 bevels every join), **-opacity** and **-blend**. **-radius** is a length of 0 or more; on a rectangle it rounds the corners and 0 leaves them square. **-rule** takes `nonzero` (the default) or `evenodd` and decides which parts of a self-intersecting path count as inside; anything else is refused. **-from**, **-to**, **-at**, **-c1**, **-c2** are exactly two numbers **-at** and **-size** are checked the way every other shape's are — two numbers each, and a third word is refused rather than silently dropped.. A segment of **-segments** is `{move x y}`, `{line x y}`, `{curve x1 y1 x2 y2 x y}` or `{close}`, in document coordinates, and a path begins with a `move`. **-close** joins the end of a `polygon`, a `curve` or a `path` back to its start; a polygon is closed by default, the other two are not. `circle` and `ellipse` are one command under two names: either takes **-radius** for a circle or **-size** *{w h}* for an ellipse, and **-at** is the centre. A shape that is refused — a point that is not a number, an unknown segment, an odd count of **-points** — leaves nothing behind in the page: the check comes before the first byte is written.

*doc* **clip -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-rule** *evenodd*? / *doc* **clip -segments** *list* ?**-rule** *evenodd*?

: Clips everything drawn afterwards to a rectangle or to an arbitrary path, until the graphics state is restored — so it wants a **save** around it. **-segments** takes the same list as **path**. The path itself is never drawn: it ends in `W n` instead of a painting operator and only decides what of the following output stays visible. **-rule** `evenodd` is what leaves the hole in a ring open; as on the shapes it takes `nonzero` or `evenodd` and nothing else. Give either **-at** with **-size** or **-segments**, not both.

*doc* **save** / *doc* **restore**

: Push and pop the graphics state (`q` and `Q`). A **restore** with no **save** open in the same stream is refused and writes nothing.

*doc* **transform** ?**-translate** *{dx dy}*? ?**-rotate** *deg*? ?**-scale** *s*? ?**-skew** *{a b}*? ?**-at** *{x y}*? ?**-matrix** *{a b c d e f}*?

: Multiplies the current transformation matrix. **-at** names a fixed point to turn, scale or skew about; **-translate** is a displacement. The two are different things and must not be confused. The parts compose as if called one after another — translate, then rotate, then skew, then scale — the way a sequence of `cm` operators does: a shape drawn afterwards is scaled first, skewed, turned, and displaced last, so the displacement is in unscaled, unturned document units, as it is in Canvas or SVG (`-translate {10 0} -rotate 90` moves 10 mm to the right, not 10 mm down). **-scale** is one factor or `{sx sy}`, none of them zero — a negative one mirrors.

  **-skew** shears by two angles in degrees: the first tilts vertically — y follows x — and the second horizontally, which is the slant an italic-looking stamp needs. **-matrix** takes the six numbers of a PDF matrix — exactly six, and not singular: a `cm` whose a·d − b·c is zero folds everything drawn after it onto a line, so it is refused before it is written — and multiplies them in as they stand, ignoring every other option: the values are the raw `cm` operands — points, origin at the bottom left, y upwards — for the caller who already has a matrix rather than wants one built.

*doc* **opacity** *value* ?*fill*|*stroke*|*both*?

: Fill and stroke opacity between 0 and 1. The second word limits it to one of the two — `both`, the default, sets fill and stroke alike. (PDF 1.4.)

*doc* **blend** *mode*

: The blend mode: how a colour is combined with what is already on the page. One of `Normal`, `Multiply`, `Screen`, `Overlay`, `Darken`, `Lighten`, `ColorDodge`, `ColorBurn`, `HardLight`, `SoftLight`, `Difference`, `Exclusion`, `Hue`, `Saturation`, `Color` or `Luminosity`. Like the alpha it is graphics state and holds until changed; the shapes take it per call as **-blend**, which keeps it inside their own save/restore. `Compatible` is refused — it has been deprecated since PDF 1.4 and means `Normal`. PDF/A parts 2 and 3 permit every mode listed. (PDF 1.4.)

*doc* **overprint** ?**-fill** *bool*? ?**-stroke** *bool*? ?**-mode** *0|1*?

: Whether what is painted from here on **knocks out** what lies under it on the other colourants, or leaves it standing (8.6.7). It is the one graphics state parameter that changes nothing a reader shows and everything a press prints, so nothing in this package decides it for the caller.

  Black text on a cyan panel is the standing case. Knocked out — the default — the cyan plate is punched where the letters go, and the sheet has to register to a tenth of a millimetre or a white edge shows around every letter. Overprinted, the cyan runs on underneath and a misregister is invisible. **And the trap**, which is why control strips exist: overprinting a *light* colour is a mistake — yellow over cyan, overprinted, is green. No viewer shows it and no validator reports it; the proof is where it is found.

  **-stroke** writes `/OP` and governs stroking (PDF 1.2), **-fill** writes `/op` and governs filling (PDF 1.3), and **-mode** writes `/OPM` (PDF 1.3): it says what a **zero** in a CMYK value means — `0` leaves that plate alone, `1` ("nonzero overprint") paints it at nought, which is how a caller sets one plate without disturbing the rest. The version asked for is the one the call actually writes, so a document that only sets the stroke is not raised for a key it never wrote. An option left out is not written; a call naming **none** of the three is refused (`TCLPDF GRAPHICS OVERPRINT NONE`) rather than writing a graphics state that changes nothing. The shapes take **-overprint** *bool* as well, which sets both sides at once inside the shape's own `q`/`Q` — a shape that is filled and stroked in one call has no way of saying which of the two it meant.

*doc* **style** ?*options*?

: Sets the drawing state — the same options the shapes take, in force until changed: a `-width`, `-dash`, `-cap`, `-join`, `-miter`, `-opacity`, `-blend`, `-fill` or `-stroke` set here holds for every shape that does not name its own. The colours are the part worth spelling out: after `style -fill red -stroke blue` a bare `rect` is filled red and stroked blue, a `rect -fill green` is filled green and still stroked blue — a shape paints the sides `style` coloured plus the ones it names itself — and a `line`, which has no fill, is stroked blue instead of the black it draws in on its own; a `text` between them leaves them in force: it brackets its own colour. The state is that of the *stream*: **save** and **restore** take it back with the `Q`, a form or a pattern being built neither sees the page's colours nor leaks its own, and a new page starts fresh.

## Colour

A colour is a name (`red`, `steelblue` — 148 of them, without Tk), a hexadecimal triplet (`#ffd700`, or `#fd7`), a grey value, `{r g b}` between 0 and 1, `{c m y k}`, a separation, a DeviceN colour, an ICC based colour `{icc alias components}` — the alias registered with **icc embed** below — or a Lab colour `{lab L a b}`. Three and four numbers are told apart by their count; the space may be named instead — `{gray 0.5}` (`grey` as well), `{rgb 1 0.84 0}`, `{cmyk 0 0.16 1 0}` — which is the unambiguous form. Fills may also name a pattern: `{pattern sky}` — and so may the colour of text, through **font -color** or a text call's **-color**, which fills the glyphs with the gradient or the tile. A name or hex triplet whose three components are equal — `black`, `white`, `gray`, `silver`, `#808080` — is written as DeviceGray, not as three equal RGB numbers: the picture is the same, but PDF/A allows DeviceRGB only under an RGB output intent (ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.3) and DeviceGray under any, so the achromatic names stay usable in a document whose intent is CMYK — and `pdfa` refuses at the write whatever does not fit the intent, naming the call. Where colours have to share one space, as the stops of a shading do, a grey stop is promoted to the space of the coloured ones — `{white steelblue}` is an RGB gradient. Components outside 0..1 are clamped to the range, not refused.

A separation is a spot colour — a varnish, a security ink, a Pantone shade: `{separation Name alternate ?tint?}`. *Name* is the plate as the press will know it — a separation needs a name, and `All` and `None`, the special colourants of ISO 32000-1 8.6.6.4, are written as they stand; *alternate* is an ordinary colour in one of the three device spaces or in Lab — grey, RGB, CMYK or `{lab L a b}`, never another separation or a pattern — what a reader that has no such ink shows instead; *tint* is the coverage from 0 to 1 and defaults to 1. `{separation Varnish {cmyk 0 0 0 0.2} 0.8}` paints 80 % of a plate called Varnish. The colour space object and its tint transform are written on first use, once per name, and fill and stroke may both use it; a name that appears again must carry the same alternate, since one plate is one ink. Under PDF/A the alternate follows the output intent like every other colour (ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.4): a CMYK alternate needs a CMYK profile, an RGB one an RGB profile, grey passes anywhere — see `pdfa` for the check.

A Lab colour is a colour as it was *measured* rather than as a device would make it: `{lab L a b}`, where *L\** runs from 0 to 100 and *a\** and *b\** over the range the space names — `{lab 54.29 80.81 69.88}` is the red of sRGB, said as a measurement. The space has two parameters, and both are options on the colour itself: **-whitePoint** *{Xw Yw Zw}* is the tristimulus value of the diffuse white point, `{0.9642 1.0 0.8249}` — D50, the illuminant of the ICC profile connection space and of every printed measurement — unless you say otherwise; ISO 32000-2, 8.6.5.4, Table 64 requires *Xw* and *Zw* positive and *Yw* exactly 1.0, and a white point that breaks either is refused, because a wrong white point is not a stray value but a wrong space. **-range** *{amin amax bmin bmax}* bounds *a\** and *b\**, `{-100 100 -100 100}` by default, which is the standard's own default; the wider `{-128 127 -128 127}` of the example in 8.6.5.4 is the usual choice in prepress and one option away. Components outside those bounds are **clamped, not refused** — the standard prescribes it: "Component values falling outside the specified range shall be adjusted to the nearest valid value without error indication" — so sRGB blue, whose *b\** is -112.05, comes out at -100 unless **-range** admits it. The three numbers are not 0..1 like every other space here; a Lab colour clamped to that range would come out black. The colour space object is written on first use and stands in the document once per distinct white point and range, however many colours and separations use it. **The point of the space is the spot colour**: `{separation "Spot Red" {lab 48.3 68.5 47.3} 0.6}` gives a plate the measurement behind its name instead of a CMYK guess, and tint 0 there is *L\** 100 — paper, not ink. Lab is device independent like an ICC based colour, so `pdfa` admits it under *every* output intent — measured with veraPDF: a Lab fill, a Lab stroke and a Lab separation alternate pass PDF/A-3B under an sRGB, a grey and a CMYK intent alike, where the same colours as `{rgb …}` are refused under the CMYK one. A gradient cannot take one, for the same reason an ICC based colour cannot: a shading names its space by family in its dictionary, and a Lab space is an array. `Lab`, and `Lab` followed by eight hexadecimal digits, are the names this writer gives its Lab colour spaces and may not be used for a separation or an **icc embed** alias. (PDF 1.1.)

A DeviceN colour paints several named plates at once: `{devicen {{Name colour} …} ?{tint …}?}` — a list of colourants, each written exactly the way a separation is (*Name* the plate as the press will know it, *colour* what that plate paints at full tint), and one tint per name in the same order, all of them 1 unless said otherwise. `{devicen {{Orange {cmyk 0 0.45 1 0}} {Green {cmyk 0.8 0 0.7 0.1}}} {0.7 0.3}}` paints 70 % of an orange plate over 30 % of a green one. This is the space of high-fidelity printing — PANTONE Hexachrome is CMYK plus orange and green — and of duotones, and it is not a separation with several names: the space carries **one function that turns n tints into the alternate space**, so what it describes is how the inks look *together*, where a separation describes one ink alone (ISO 32000-2, 8.6.6.5). All the colourants therefore paint into one alternate space — a grey one is promoted to the space of the coloured ones, as a grey shading stop is, and mixing RGB with CMYK is refused; Lab and ICC based colourants are refused too, although the standard would admit them, because combining two measured appearances needs a colour engine this package has not got — paint such a plate on its own with `{separation Name {lab L a b} tint}`. `All`, which a separation may be, is refused here: 8.6.6.5 rules it out, since every plate at once cannot be one component among n. `None` is the opposite case and is written `{None}`, without a colour: it names a component that is never painted, it is the one name that may be repeated, and this writer keeps it out of the fallback colour as well. A space whose colourants are all `None` paints nothing and names no alternate at all, and is refused rather than written. At most 32 colourants (Annex C.2). A colourant and a separation of the same name are the same plate: they share one colour space object, and a colourant that contradicts an existing separation's alternate is refused, naming the colour that stands.

The tint transform of that space is a **type 4 function — PostScript calculator code — because a type 2 or type 3 function takes one input by definition** (7.10.3, 7.10.4) and this one needs n; the standard points at type 4 for this very case, a sampled function being prohibitive at 4·m⁶ samples for a six-colourant space (7.10.5.1). In a CMYK alternate the ink amounts add and the function's own `/Range` clips the sum, which is what a press does when two solids overprint; in an RGB or grey alternate, where the numbers count light rather than ink, they multiply instead, so no ink at all leaves paper rather than black. Both are exact where a caller can check them — no ink, and one plate alone: the same plate painted as a separation and as one component of a DeviceN space comes out the same colour, measured with pdftoppm. The space also carries an attributes dictionary with `/Subtype /DeviceN` and `/Colorants`, which holds each plate on its own — the one thing a combined transform cannot say — and under PDF/A that dictionary is **required**, not optional: ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.4 wants an entry for every spot colourant of a DeviceN space, and veraPDF fails a document without it. `/Process` and `/MixingHints` are not written: the first belongs to the NChannel subtype, the second wants measured ink solidities and dot gain that this package has no source for. Because every entry of that dictionary is PDF 1.6, a DeviceN colour needs a 1.6 document although the space itself is 1.3 — said at the call, with the number to raise the document to. Under PDF/A the **alternate** space is what the output intent judges, exactly as for a separation (6.2.4.4). A gradient cannot take a DeviceN colour, for the same reason it cannot take a Lab or an ICC based one: a shading names its space by family in its dictionary, and a DeviceN space is an array. `DeviceN` followed by eight hexadecimal digits is the name this writer gives its DeviceN colour spaces and may not be used for a separation or an **icc embed** alias. (PDF 1.6.)

*doc* **icc embed** *alias* *path* / *doc* **icc names**

: Registers an ICC profile under an alias; **icc names** lists the aliases. `{icc alias components}` is then a colour in that profile's space wherever a colour is taken — fill, stroke, text — with as many components as the profile has: one for a grey profile, three for RGB, four for CMYK; a wrong count is refused naming the right one, and an unregistered alias is refused before anything is recorded. The profile has to describe a device space — grey, RGB or CMYK — and be a profile at all; anything else is refused at the **icc embed** call. The colour space object — a stream holding the profile, with `/N` for the component count (ISO 32000-1, 8.6.5.5) — is written on first use, and a profile stands in the document **once** however many roads it arrives by: as a registered colour space, as a picture's embedded profile (see *Images*), or as the PDF/A output intent — `pdfa` with the same file as **-profile** points its intent at the same stream. An ICC based colour is anchored to its profile rather than to the device, so `pdfa` admits it under *every* output intent — measured with veraPDF: `{icc srgb 0.2 0.45 0.75}` passes PDF/A-3B under a CMYK press intent where the bare `{rgb 0.2 0.45 0.75}` is refused, because ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.3 judges device colours only. A gradient cannot take one — a shading names its space by family in its dictionary; give the stops in grey, RGB or CMYK. (PDF 1.3.)

## Images

*doc* **image embed** *alias* ?*path*? ?**-data** *bytes*? ?**-type** *auto|jpeg|png|tiff*? ?**-icc** *bool*? ?**-stencil** *bool*? ?**-mask** *alias*? ?**-invert** *bool*? ?**-interpolate** *bool*?

: Reads a JPEG or PNG and prepares it for placing. JPEG data passes through untouched as `/DCTDecode`; a PNG without alpha passes through as `/FlateDecode`. A PNG whose transparency is a single colour — a greyscale or truecolor file with a `tRNS` chunk, or a palette whose entries are either opaque or fully transparent and whose transparent entries form one contiguous run of indices — keeps that transparency as a `/Mask` colour-key array on the same pass-through, at the file's own bit depth; the reader keys the colour out, nothing is decoded. The one exception is a 16-bit greyscale or truecolor file: a `/Mask` array at sixteen bits is what the format asks for, but readers were measured to ignore it — poppler compares the ranges as if the image were 8-bit, and CoreGraphics renders the picture opaque too — so there the key is decoded once into an `/SMask` of 0 and 255; the picture itself still passes through at 16 bits, only the mask is computed. Only a PNG with an alpha channel, a palette with partially transparent entries or with transparent entries in more than one run, or such a 16-bit key is decoded to build an `/SMask`. (A PNG with a transparent colour needs PDF 1.3, one with an alpha channel or a computed soft mask 1.4, sixteen bits per component 1.5.) A progressive JPEG and an interlaced (Adam7) PNG are refused with the reason: JPEG data is embedded as it is and only baseline and extended-sequential files are taken — re-save as baseline; an interlaced PNG re-save without interlacing.

: An ICC profile embedded in the file — JPEG carries one in `APP2` `ICC_PROFILE` segments, PNG in an `iCCP` chunk — becomes the picture's `/ICCBased` colour space instead of the bare device name, so the picture is anchored to its profile and `pdfa` admits it under every output intent (see *Colour*); for a palette PNG it becomes the base of the `/Indexed` space, and a soft mask stays DeviceGray — the mask is coverage, not colour. The profile stream is shared with **icc embed** and the output intent: one profile, one copy per document. A kept profile has to describe the space of the samples (`/N`, ISO 32000-1, 8.6.5.5) — a grey picture with an RGB profile, or an `APP2`/`iCCP` payload that is no profile, is refused at the embed with **-icc** *0* named as the way out: `-icc 0` embeds the picture without its profile. (A picture with a profile needs PDF 1.3.)

: **-data** takes the bytes instead of a file name — for an image that never was a file: a canvas posted from a browser, a plot from a subprocess, a value out of a database. The format is decided by the leading bytes in both cases, so nothing else changes; `image info` then reports an empty `path`. **A PNG with an alpha channel is by far the most expensive way in** — the channel has to be split out in pure Tcl, measured at about a hundred times the cost of the pass-through. Where transparency is not needed, JPEG or a PNG without alpha is the cheaper choice, and for a browser canvas that means `toDataURL("image/jpeg")`.

: **-stencil** *1* turns a one-bit PNG into a **stencil mask** (`/ImageMask true`, ISO 32000-2, 8.9.6.2). Such a picture has no colour of its own — Table 87 forbids it a `ColorSpace` entirely — and its samples "designate places on the page that should either be marked with the current colour or masked out": every placement paints the fill colour then in force, the one **style -fill** last set, through the bits of the file. That is the way in for a single-colour logo or a stamp that has to take the colour of the document it lands in: one embedding, one object, any number of placements in any number of colours. Which of the two bit values is the ink is read out of the file rather than left to be guessed — in a greyscale picture 0 is black and paints, in a palette picture the darker of the two entries paints, unless a `tRNS` chunk makes exactly one of them fully transparent, in which case the other one does. **-invert** *1* reverses whatever that decided (the `/Decode` array becomes `[1 0]`). A stencil is a pass-through like any other PNG, never a decode, and the `tRNS` chunk it may carry reaches the file as nothing at all, because with `ImageMask true` the `Mask` entry shall not be present. It has to be one bit per sample: a JPEG is refused, because a `DCTDecode` filter always delivers 8-bit samples, and so is a PNG of any other depth or with more than one sample per pixel. Under **pdfa** a stencil is admitted whatever the output intent says — it brings no colour space to be judged, and what is judged is the fill colour it lets through, where that colour is set.

: **-mask** *alias* names a **second embedded picture** as this one's mask, which until now only a PNG's own alpha channel could produce. What it becomes in the file depends on what the named picture is, and the difference is not one of spelling: a picture embedded with **-stencil** *1* is all-or-nothing and becomes the `/Mask` of explicit masking (8.9.6.3) — unmasked areas show the base picture, masked areas show nothing — while any other picture is coverage and becomes the `/SMask` of a soft mask (11.6.5.2), so the base picture fades rather than ends. The mask has to be embedded before the picture that wears it, it gets an object of its own and no page resource, and two pictures may share one mask, which then travels once. The two need not be the same size: all images are defined on the unit square, so their boundaries on the page coincide. Table 143 fixes what a soft-mask image may be, and each of its conditions is a refusal here — a mask that is not greyscale, one carrying an ICC profile (**-icc** *0* is the way out) and one with transparency of its own. So is a picture that already brings its own mask, because an `SMask` entry overrides the image's `Mask` entry and a second mask would silently replace the first, and so is a stencil asked to wear a mask, which with `ImageMask true` may have none.

: **-interpolate** *1* sets `/Interpolate true` (8.9.5.3), the declaration that a picture "might render better if interpolation is used" where it is rendered well below the device's resolution. It is a hint, and the standard says outright that a processor may ignore it; it is written only where it is asked for, since Table 87 gives it the default `false`. On a stencil the effect, where it is honoured, is to smooth the edges of the **mask** rather than the colour it lets through. **Under PDF/A it is refused, on both roads**: ISO 19005-2, 6.2.8 says the key "shall not be present, or shall have a value of false", and veraPDF reports it — smoothing is a decision the archive format takes away from the reader on purpose, so that the same file looks the same in twenty years. Whichever comes first in the script is what refuses: a picture placed under a standing declaration is turned away as it goes down **An inline image binds the version in both directions:** a 1.x file that carries one cannot be raised to PDF 2.0 afterwards, because `/L` is required there and the bytes are already on the page; and a 2.0 file that wrote `/L` cannot be lowered., and a **pdfa** call after the fact is turned away naming the pictures already in the file. The error code is `TCLPDF IMAGE INTERPOLATE PDFA` either way.

: A TIFF is read too. A TIFF is a directory of tags and a list of **strips**, each a band of the picture compressed on its own, and every compression that occurs has a PDF filter that undoes it — Deflate is `/FlateDecode`, PackBits is `/RunLengthDecode`, CCITT Group 3 and 4 are `/CCITTFaxDecode`, TIFF 6.0 Technote 2 JPEG is `/DCTDecode` — so the strips are passed through, Group 4 fax data included. Five things are not the file's own bytes. LZW is unpacked and written out again as Flate, always, because ISO 19005-3, 6.1.7.2 forbids `/LZWDecode` and a picture's stream is built when it is first placed while **pdfa** may be declared afterwards. An uncompressed file is deflated on the way out. A `FillOrder 2` fax has the bits of every byte turned round, which PDF has no parameter to ask a filter for. A 16-bit file is unpacked where its samples are little-endian, since PDF reads a 16-bit sample high byte first (8.9.5.2), and where it carries TIFF Predictor 2, whose per-component difference no reader was measured to undo correctly at that depth; the difference is resolved here, the samples are swapped into PDF's order, and what travels is plain deflated samples with no predictor. And a JPEG-in-TIFF is assembled rather than copied: TIFF 6.0 Technote 2 puts one bare JPEG stream in every strip and the tables they share in a tag of their own, so neither half is a file a decoder would take, and each strip goes out as its own stream with an `APP14` Adobe segment and those tables put in front of it. PackBits is passed through, and only walked: its no-op byte is PDF's end-of-data, so the runs are stepped over to drop those and to append the end-of-data marker TIFF does not write — measured, no file in the corpus holds one, so the walk copies the strip byte for byte and only proves that it may. A greyscale, RGB, palette or CMYK picture at 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 bits is taken, an embedded ICC profile becomes the `/ICCBased` colour space as it does for the other two formats, a palette becomes an `/Indexed` space over DeviceRGB, and a `WhiteIsZero` picture reaches the file with the `/Decode` array that reconciles it with DeviceGray. Several IFDs are not several pages: the primary image is the one embedded, and the others are passed over in silence — **image info** says nothing about them, and there is no way to ask for one. A multi-page fax reaches the page as its first page. Refused by name, each with what the file is and what to re-save it as: BigTIFF, a tiled file, separate colour planes, an alpha channel (`ExtraSamples` — PDF carries no alpha inside an image and none is split out), YCbCr outside a JPEG stream, and any bit depth, sample format or predictor PDF cannot be told about.

: **A TIFF whose compression carries state from one row to the next becomes several image XObjects, one per strip**, because such a stream begins afresh in every strip and the strips therefore cannot be joined. Nothing about the calls changes — the picture is embedded, sized, placed, rotated and made transparent as any other, and it counts as one Figure or one artifact in a tagged document — but **image info** answers `strips`, the file carries that many image objects, and a placement writes that many `cm`/`Do` pairs inside its one `q` … `Q`. Uncompressed, PackBits and LZW files are one image however many strips they have, since those carry no state and are joined into a single stream. Two things such a stack cannot be, and both are refused with `TCLPDF TIFF STACKED`: another picture's mask, and the wearer of one — `/Mask` and `/SMask` name a single image XObject, and a mask laid over a stack would be drawn over each part in turn instead of over the picture. Above 256 parts the picture is refused with `TCLPDF TIFF STRIPS` rather than stacked. The way out of all three is the same and is named in the message: re-save the file with a `RowsPerStrip` that holds the whole picture. **-stencil** *1* stays a PNG option — a bilevel TIFF is placed as the picture it is.

*doc* **image place** *alias* **-at** *{x y}* ?**-width** *w*? ?**-height** *h*? ?**-size** *{w h}*? ?**-scale** *s*? ?**-fit** *{w h}*? ?**-fitMode** *contain|cover*? ?**-align** *left|center|right*? ?**-valign** *top|middle|bottom*? ?**-dpi** *n*? ?**-rotate** *deg*? ?**-opacity** *o*? ?**-inline** *bool*? ?**-alt** *text*? ?**-artifact** *bool*?

: Places an embedded image. Giving only one of width and height keeps the aspect ratio; **-size** sets both extents at once and keeps nothing. Without any of them the natural size applies, and **-dpi** decides it: a pixel is 1/dpi of an inch. The default is **-dpi auto**, which is the resolution the file itself states — a PNG `pHYs` chunk, a JPEG JFIF density, an Exif resolution, a TIFF `XResolution` with its `ResolutionUnit` — and **72 only where the file states none**, which makes one pixel one point as before. A file that gives a pixel aspect ratio without a resolution has that ratio applied to the 72. `-dpi 300` overrides whatever the file says and places a scan at the size it was scanned from. The distinction matters because a scan is not a screenshot: 1728 pixels at the 200 dpi of a fax line are 219 mm of paper, and read as 72 dpi the same picture comes out 610 mm wide, off the sheet in any format. **-scale** multiplies that natural size and yields to any explicit width, height or size. **-width**, **-height**, the two lengths of **-size**, **-scale** and **-dpi** are numbers above zero — zero would fold the picture onto a line and a negative one place it mirrored beyond its corner, so both are refused; **image size** refuses the same values. **-at** is exactly two numbers, **-rotate** a number of degrees. The same image placed five times is stored once.

  **-fit** *{w h}* is the box the picture goes into with its proportions kept, and it is the one sizing option that cannot be worked out from the natural size alone: the box decides the factor, and **-fitMode** says which of its two edges decides it. `contain` (the default) takes the smaller factor, so the whole picture is inside the box and a band is left on the axis that had room to spare; `cover` takes the larger, so the whole box is covered and what hangs over it is **clipped away** — the box is what the caller asked to see, and the overhang would otherwise be drawn across whatever stands beside it. A covering placement declares the **box** as its bounding box in a tagged document, not the placement: ISO 32000-2, 14.8.5.4.3 asks for the rectangle that encloses the *visible* content, and three quarters of a covering picture is not visible. **-fit** and **-size**, **-width**, **-height** or **-scale** contradict each other and are refused together — both say how large the picture comes out, and one would have to win in silence. **-dpi** is not among them: it says how large a pixel is, which is what the proportions are made of, and those are what **-fit** keeps.

  **-align** and **-valign** say where in that box the picture sits — the words this package already uses for the two axes, `left`, `center` (or `centre`) and `right` across, `top`, `middle` and `bottom` down. **-at** names the top left corner of the box, exactly as it names the top left corner of a `rect`. **Without -fit the box has no size, and the same two options are the plain anchor:** a logo whose right edge is to sit on the right margin is `-at {190 20} -align right`, and nothing has to be measured first. They are deliberately not called `-anchor`: **text** has an **-anchor** and it means `baseline` or `top`, one *axis* rather than a corner. **-rotate** turns the placement about the point **-at** names, so it cannot be combined with **-fit** or with an **-align**/**-valign** that moves the corner (`TCLPDF FIT ROTATE`): the turned picture leaves its upright box, and a covered box would be cut by a rectangle standing at an angle to it. Where both are wanted, **image size** takes **-fit** and answers the fitted size, and **-size** takes that.

  **-inline** *1* writes the picture **into the content stream** instead of beside it (ISO 32000-2, 8.9.7): `BI`, an abbreviated dictionary, `ID`, the bytes, `EI` — no object number, no entry in the page's resources, no line in the cross-reference table, and no way of using it twice, so a picture placed inline five times travels five times. That is its whole case and its whole cost, and it is why it is *asked* for rather than decided by size here: whether a picture occurs once is something the caller knows and the writer does not. The classic use is a one-bit stamp of a few dozen bytes placed with **-stencil**, painted in the fill colour then in force. At most **4096 bytes** of image data — the line the standard itself names twice — and a larger picture is **refused** rather than quietly written as an XObject. Five things an inline picture cannot be, each refused by name: masked, transparent of its own accord (an alpha channel, a colour key, a partly transparent palette), ICC-tagged (8.9.7 admits no CIE-based colour space), a stack (a striped TIFF is several images and `BI … EI` is one), or over the line. The dictionary is written in the abbreviations of Table 92 — `/W`, `/H`, `/BPC`, `/CS /G`, `/F /Fl` — and `/L` only in a 2.0 file, where the standard requires it. Measured with veraPDF: PDF/A-2B, PDF/A-3B and PDF/UA-1 all take an inline picture, and `pdfimages -list` reports it with `[inline]` where the object number would be.


*doc* **image draw** ?*path*? ?**-data** *bytes*? **-at** *{x y}* ?*options*? ?**-alt** *text*? ?**-artifact** *bool*?

: Embeds and places in one call, for an image used once. It takes **-data** as well; with no file name to key the cache on, the bytes themselves are the key, so the same picture drawn twice still travels once. The options are those of **image place**, **-alt** and **-artifact** among them — what they do is described under *Structure and accessibility*.

*doc* **image info** *alias* / *doc* **image size** *alias* / *doc* **image names**

: What the file is, its size, and the aliases embedded so far. **image info** answers `type`, `path`, `bytes`, `width`, `height`, `bitDepth` and `icc` — the size in bytes of the ICC profile that will travel with the picture, 0 when the file carries none or `-icc 0` left it behind — for all three formats, and with them `xResolution`, `yResolution`, `resolution` and `pixelAspect`. The two resolutions are in dpi and are **empty when the file states none** — empty is not 72: 72 is what a placement falls back on, while empty is the file saying nothing, and the two must not be confused by a caller that lays out around a picture. `resolution` names where the number came from: `pHYs`, `JFIF`, `Exif`, `XResolution` or `none`. **A JPEG may state a resolution twice and state it differently, and then Exif wins** — measured, a file whose JFIF header says 72 dpi and whose Exif block says 300 is placed at 300, and `resolution` answers `Exif`. The recurring shape in real files is exactly that one: an encoder's default left standing in the JFIF header beside a figure somebody meant. A segment that gives only a ratio between the axes never displaces one that gives a measure, whichever of the two it is; `resolution` says which segment the answer came from, so a caller can see what was preferred. `pixelAspect` carries a ratio for the files that give one without a measure; a PNG adds `colorType`, `alpha` and `transparency` — how the picture's transparency reaches the file: `none`, `colourKey` (a `/Mask` array, the picture still passed through) or `softMask` (an `/SMask`: an alpha channel, a palette with partial entries or with transparent entries in more than one run, or a 16-bit colour key), a JPEG adds `components` (1 grey, 3 RGB, 4 CMYK) and `alpha` 0. A TIFF adds `components`, `alpha` 0, `compression` (the name of its strips' compression: `none`, `packBits`, `lzw`, `deflate`, `jpeg`, `ccittRle`, `ccittG3`, `ccittG4`), `strips`, `rowsPerStrip` and `space` (`DeviceGray`, `DeviceRGB`, `DeviceCMYK` or `Indexed`) — the two that decide how the picture reaches the file, answered before it has been placed. `resolution` answers `XResolution` where the file states one; `ResolutionUnit` 1 is a bare ratio between the axes, so the two dpi fields stay empty and only `pixelAspect` carries it. A picture embedded as a mask or wearing one adds `stencil` (1 for an image mask), `mask` (the alias named with **-mask**, or empty) and `interpolate`; for a stencil, `transparency` answers `stencil` — its bits *are* the transparency, so neither a `/Mask` array nor an `/SMask` is written for it. **image size** is the size a placement would come out at, in the document unit, and takes the same sizing options as **place** — what a caller needs to lay out around a picture. **image names** lists the aliases.

## Tables

*doc* **table -at** *{x y}* ?**-width** *w*? ?**-head** *rows*? **-body** *rows* ?**-foot** *rows*? ?*options*?

: Draws a table and returns the y coordinate below it. **-width** is the width of the table in the document unit; without it the table takes the page width less twice the larger of its left edge and 10 — 170 mm for a table at `-at {20 y}` on A4. A row is a list of cells; a cell is a string, or a dictionary with `text` and any of `colSpan`, `rowSpan`, `align`, `valign`, `direction` and style keys. `align`, `valign` and `direction` may equally be written inside `style`; on the cell itself they win over column, section and theme.

  `direction` is `ltr` or `rtl` and says which way that cell's text runs — a property of the cell rather than of the table, because an invoice has a right-to-left description column and a left-to-right amount column on the same row. Under `rtl` the cell's `align` is mirrored with it, so the default `left` sets the text flush with the right hand edge of the cell. See "Writing systems" for what a right-to-left line does and does not do.

  `hyphenate` breaks the words of a cell where the patterns of a language allow it, and it is a style key for the same reason `direction` is: a table can carry a German description column beside an English note, and a language said once for the whole table could not say that. `0` is the default and means what it has always meant — a word wider than what is left of its line is split between two characters and no hyphen is set; `1` is the language the document declares with **language**; anything else is a language tag such as `de-AT`. Only the two literal values `0` and `1` are the switch, so `no` is the tag for Norwegian rather than a Tcl false, exactly as under **text**. The patterns have to be loaded first with **::tclpdf::hyphenate load** — none are shipped with the package, which is a licence decision and not an oversight — and a language nobody loaded refuses the whole table by name, `TCLPDF HYPHENATE LANGUAGE`, before a cell is drawn.

  **What it is worth**, measured: the sentence *Die Betriebskostenabrechnung wird nach der Wohnflaeche umgelegt.* in a 40 mm column of 9 pt Helvetica takes four lines without it, the second of them broken as `Betriebskostenabrechnun` / `g` because the compound is wider than the rest of that line, and three lines with it — 13.95 mm of row instead of 17.61. Read the other way round, those same three lines fit a column of 36.5 mm where 46.5 mm was needed before: ten millimetres of table width, for one word in the column description.

  A broken cell brackets its break hyphen exactly as a **text -width** paragraph does: in a **tagged** document the hyphen sits in a `Span` with an empty `ActualText` (ISO 32000-1, 14.8.2.6), so extracting the cell gives `Betriebskostenabrechnung` and not `Betriebskostenab-rechnung`. The cell is still measured first and set line by line from what was measured — the line is not cut up here, it is handed to **text** whole with **-breakHyphen**, and the cutting happens inside one text object after the position of the whole line has been computed. So the drawn line begins at the same coordinate and is as wide as the measured one, for every alignment; a table that cut its own strings would lose the kerning across the break and set a line wider than the column it was measured for. An untagged table is written byte for byte as it was before.

  A row is as tall as its tallest cell. Where one cell carries running text over several lines and its neighbours hold a single line each, `valign` says where in the row those single lines sit — `top` by default, or `middle` or `bottom`. In a table whose rows are all one line it makes no difference, which is why the default is the one that leaves such a table alone.

  **Unknown keys are an error**, in a cell, in a style, and in a column description — as they already were among the `-options`. A dictionary written over defaults otherwise refuses nothing: a mistyped key is carried along, read by nobody, and the caller sees the default and no message. The values are checked as well: `align`, `valign` and `border` outside the lists below, a negative `padding` or `lineWidth`, a `leading` of 0 are refused, as is a `colSpan` reaching over a column no row has a cell in (unless **-columns** describes it) and a `rowSpan` reaching past the last row of its section.

  A cell is read as a dictionary when its **first** word is one of the cell keys and it has an even word count; anything else is the cell's text. Tcl draws no line between a string and a dictionary, so this is a decision rather than a detection, and it leaves one ambiguous case: a plain string that begins with `text`, `align`, `colSpan`, `rowSpan`, `valign` or `style` and happens to have an even word count is read as a dictionary. It then names the offending key rather than silently keeping a fragment of the sentence. Write such a string as a cell dictionary — `{text "text is set here"}` — and it is unambiguous.

  Column widths come in three kinds, resolved in that order: fixed (`{width 34}`), weighted (`{weight 1}`) and automatic — the rest is shared according to how wide the content actually is. Both keys take a number; there is no percent width, a share of the table is what `weight` is for. Fixed widths that add up to more than the table are refused, and so are fixed widths that use the table up while a further column has no width of its own — that column would come out zero wide.

  **-columns** describes the columns, **-theme** picks `striped` — **the default, so a table drawn without a theme is a striped one** — or `grid` or `plain` — their lines and grey fills are single DeviceGray values, the one space every PDF/A output intent allows (a grey given as an RGB triplet was the one failed check of a PDF/A file with a CMYK intent); the only colour a theme brings is the blue head of `striped`, an RGB value that is right in every plain PDF and under the default sRGB intent, and that a document with a CMYK or grey output intent (**pdfa -profile**) overrides with **-headStyle** `{fill {cmyk …}}`, or the write is refused under `pdfa`, naming a `rect` on that page (the table draws its cells through `rect`) — and **-style**, **-headStyle**, **-bodyStyle** and **-footStyle** set fonts, colours and padding. **-alternateFill** colours every second body row — the stripe the `striped` theme brings, replaceable with any colour; the other themes have none. **-minRowHeight** is the least height a row may take, for rows whose content alone would leave them shallower. The `border` style key takes `none`, `all`, `horizontal`, `vertical` or `outer`; `outer` frames the block once per page instead of ruling every cell. **-repeatHead** and **-repeatFoot** carry those sections onto each page: the head is repeated (`1`) and the foot is not (`0`) unless said otherwise, because a head names the columns the reader is looking at while a foot is usually a total that belongs under the last row only. **-horizontalBreak** deals a table too wide for the page over further pages, with **-repeatColumns** keeping the leading columns on each.

  **-top** and **-bottom** are the type area a breaking table works within, which is not the same thing as where it sits. **-at** says where this table starts on its first page; **-top** says where it resumes on every page after that, and **-bottom** how far down it may run. Both default to the type area of the page the table is on (**page typeArea**) — with **-typeArea** the margins the document was given, without it five percent of the page height, 14.85 mm and 282.15 mm on A4 — read again on every page it continues on, so a table that starts on a landscape page and runs on to portrait ones keeps each page's own margins; and neither is taken from **-at**, because where a table happens to begin says nothing about where the page ends. Set **-top** to clear a running head, and **-bottom** to clear a footer. **-top** and **-bottom** are numbers; **-bottom** has to lie on the page and below **-top**, or the call is refused. A table is broken between rows and only there: a head, a row, or the rows a `rowSpan` ties together that — with the head and foot drawn around them on a page — do not fit between **-top** and **-bottom** are refused before anything is drawn, naming the heights and the band. A table whose head and first row (or head and foot, without body rows) do not fit below **-at** starts on the next page instead of leaving the head alone at the foot of the page, and the foot after the last row is kept inside **-bottom** the same way. **-minRowHeight** takes a height of 0 or more, **-repeatColumns** a whole number of 0 or more.

  `-align decimal` lines the decimal separators of a column up under each other, measured across head, body and foot together; **-decimal** picks the separator, `.` by default. A cell without the separator is aligned as if it ended at the separator, so integers line up with the integer parts of the other cells — `100` stands under the `1234` of `1234.56`, which is where a column of figures wants it — and a cell that is not a number at all sits there too.

  Rows tied together by a `rowSpan` are never split across a page break: the whole group moves.

  **Style keys.** A style is a dictionary of the keys below, and the same keys are read at every level: **-style** for the whole table, **-headStyle**, **-bodyStyle** and **-footStyle** for a section, a column description, and the `style` of a cell — laid over each other in that order, so the most specific wins. A theme is nothing but a set of these.

  **The defaults in the table below are the base a theme is laid over, not what a table comes out as** — a theme is read before any of the five style options and changes some of them. The second table says what the three themes make of it, measured on a drawn table; where a theme is silent the base value below stands.

  | key | values | default |
  |---|---|---|
  | `family` | what **font -family** takes | `helvetica` |
  | `fontStyle` | what **font -style** takes: `bold`, `italic`, both, or empty | empty |
  | `size` | the font size, in points | `9` |
  | `leading` | line spacing as a factor of the size | `1.15` |
  | `padding` | space inside the cell, in the document unit | `1.5` |
  | `fill` | the cell background — a colour, or empty for none | empty |
  | `color` | the text colour | `black` |
  | `align` | `left`, `right`, `center` or `decimal` | `left` |
  | `valign` | `top`, `middle` or `bottom` | `top` |
  | `direction` | `ltr` or `rtl` | `ltr` |
  | `hyphenate` | `0`, `1`, or a language tag such as `de-DE` | `0` |
  | `border` | `none`, `all`, `horizontal`, `vertical` or `outer` | `horizontal` |
  | `lineColor` | the colour of the rules | `{0.6 0.6 0.6}` |
  | `lineWidth` | the width of the rules, in the document unit | `0.1` |

  **What the themes make of that.** Every key not named here keeps the value above.

  | key | `striped` (the default) | `grid` | `plain` |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `border`, everywhere | `horizontal`, but `none` in the head | `all` | `none` |
  | `lineColor` | `0.76` | `0.47` | `{0.6 0.6 0.6}` |
  | `lineWidth` | `0.1` | `0.15` | `0.1` |
  | head `fill` / `color` | `{0.22 0.28 0.40}` / `1` | `0.86` / `black` | none / `black` |
  | head `fontStyle` | `bold` | `bold` | `bold` |
  | foot `fill` | `0.91` | `0.86` | none |
  | foot `fontStyle` | `bold` | `bold` | `bold` |
  | **-alternateFill** | `0.965` | none | none |

  A **column** description in **-columns** takes the same keys plus `width` and `weight`, described above — `{width 20 align decimal}` is a fixed column of amounts. A **cell** dictionary takes `text`, `colSpan`, `rowSpan`, `align`, `valign`, `direction` and `style`, the last holding any of the keys above.

  Four hooks are called: **-didParseCell** once per cell before it is measured, **-willDrawCell** and **-didDrawCell** around drawing it, and **-didDrawPage** after each page. Each receives a dictionary and the document, in that order; returning 0 from `willDrawCell` skips that cell, and a dictionary returned from `didParseCell` replaces the cell.

  The dictionary a cell hook receives is the cell as the table holds it: the cell keys above, filled in — `text`, `colSpan`, `rowSpan`, `align`, `valign`, `direction`, `style` — plus `row`, `column` and `section` (`head`, `body` or `foot`), which is what `didParseCell` sees. `willDrawCell` and `didDrawCell` see the measured cell on top of that: `lines` (the text as broken), `width`, `height`, `leading`, `resolved` (the assembled style), `x` and `y` (the top left corner on the page), `spanHeight` for a cell spanning rows, and `decimal` and `tail` for the separator and the room its tail takes. `didDrawPage` receives `page`, the index of the page just finished, and `y`, where the table stopped on it — except after a page turn that a horizontal break forced, where only `page` is known.

*doc* **table layout** ?*same options*?

: Measures without drawing — what a caller needs to decide whether a table still fits.

*doc* **table themes**

: The names of the built-in themes: `plain`, `striped`, `grid`.

## Gradients and patterns

*doc* **shading axial -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* **-colors** *list* ?**-angle** *deg*? ?**-from** *{x y}*? ?**-to** *{x y}*?

*doc* **shading radial -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* **-colors** *list* ?**-center** *{x y}*? ?**-radius** *r*? ?**-innerRadius** *r*? ?**-focus** *{x y}*?

: Draws a gradient directly — shading types 2 and 3, clipped to the rectangle of **-at** and **-size**, which both forms require. More than two colours are stitched together. (PDF 1.3.) **-size** takes two numbers above zero: a shading is an area, and a rectangle without one either clips everything away or leaves the shading's own matrix singular, which paints nothing and says nothing.

  For an axial gradient, **-angle** turns the run: 0 is left to right, counting clockwise, so 90 runs top to bottom; the end points are derived from the rectangle. **-from** and **-to** name the two end points directly instead and win over **-angle** — for a run that starts or ends inside the rectangle, or one that does not pass through its centre.

  A radial gradient runs from an inner to an outer circle. **-center** and **-radius** describe the outer one — by default the middle of the rectangle and half its longer side. **-innerRadius** (default 0) is the radius of the inner circle and **-focus** its centre, the same as **-center** unless given; moved off it, the highlight of a sphere sits away from the middle. Both radii are lengths of 0 or more.

  **-stops** positions the colours: one value per colour, between 0 and 1 and strictly ascending — two equal stops are refused, as is a count that does not match **-colors**. Only the inner values have an effect — the first and the last colour sit at the ends regardless — so it matters from three colours on. Without it the colours are spaced evenly.

  **-extend** is a pair of booleans, `{1 1}` by default: whether the first and the last colour continue beyond the ends of the gradient or stop there. **-at**, **-size**, **-from**, **-to**, **-center**, **-focus** are exactly two numbers and **-extend** two booleans, checked before the shading is written.

  **-matrix** is accepted by both direct forms as well, and there it changes only how the coordinates are read: with it, **-from**, **-to**, **-center**, **-focus** and the radii are taken as they stand, in the space the matrix maps from, rather than converted from document coordinates — the gradient itself is painted in whatever transformation is active. Writing the matrix into the file is what the pattern form below does. The value is checked like every **-matrix** — six numbers, not singular — even though it is not written.

*doc* **shading function -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* **-space** *gray|rgb|cmyk* **-expression** *body* ?**-domain** *{x0 x1 y0 y1}*?

: Shading type 1: the colour at a point is what a **function** returns for that point, rather than a run between stops. **-expression** is the body of a PostScript calculator function (ISO 32000-2, 7.10.5) **without its outer braces**, which are written here — x and y arrive on the stack in domain coordinates and what is left when it ends is the colour, one number per component. **-space** says how many that is, and is the same three families a gradient's stops may be in. **-domain** is the rectangle the calculation is written over, `{0 1 0 1}` unless given, with x0 below x1 and y0 below y1; **-at** and **-size** say where that rectangle goes, and the `/Matrix` is derived from the two rather than asked for a second time — the domain's y therefore runs **up** the page, the way a calculation expects it to. (PDF 1.3.)

  Every word of the expression is held to Table 42 — a misspelt operator is refused naming it, and so is a brace that does not close, because a reader answers either by painting nothing at all. What cannot be decided by reading is whether the right *number* of values is left on the stack: `if`, `ifelse` and `roll` make that depend on the values. A function that leaves too few is a valid file and an empty rectangle; poppler reports `Stack underflow in PostScript function` and draws nothing.

*doc* **shading triangles** ?**-at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}*? **-vertices** *list*

*doc* **shading lattice** ?**-at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}*? **-perRow** *n* **-vertices** *list*

*doc* **shading coons** ?**-at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}*? **-patches** *list*

*doc* **shading tensor** ?**-at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}*? **-patches** *list*

: The four mesh shadings, types 4 to 7 — the ones that carry their colours as points rather than deriving them. **A mesh bounds itself**: it paints only where its triangles or patches are, so unlike a gradient it needs no rectangle, and **-at** with **-size** is a clip where one is wanted. Half of a rectangle is refused. (PDF 1.3.)

  A **vertex** is `{x y colour}` with an optional fourth word, the edge flag. **triangles** is the free-form mesh: flag 0 begins a triangle and shall be followed by two more vertices with flag 0, while 1 and 2 each add a *single* vertex to the triangle before them — 1 keeps its second and third corner, which makes a strip, and 2 keeps its first and third, which makes a fan. The flags are checked: four vertices with the flags left at 0 is the ordinary mistake, and it is the one no tool reports — poppler draws the first triangle and drops the fourth vertex, qpdf and veraPDF say nothing. **lattice** is the same vertices without flags, read as rows of **-perRow** each, two or more of them and two or more rows; a flag written on a lattice vertex is refused rather than ignored, because the type cannot express it.

  **The same gradient written twice costs one object, not two.** Every PDF function this package writes goes through one place, and a function whose bytes are identical to one already in the file is referenced again instead of repeated — the device the ICC profiles already use. A table with the same sheen on twenty cells therefore carries one function rather than twenty, and two gradients that differ anywhere, down to the fifth decimal of a stop, keep their own.
