What a reader sees is unchanged; what changes is the number of objects, and with it every object number after the first repeat.

  A **patch** is a dictionary `{points {…} colors {…}}`. **coons** takes twelve control points, **tensor** sixteen, and both take four colours, one per corner. The points run round the boundary from the first corner — four cubic Bezier edges with the corners shared — and the corner colours belong to points 1, 4, 7 and 10 in the order the boundary passes them. A tensor patch adds its four interior points last; left where a Coons patch would put them they change nothing, and pulled away from there they shape the inside of the patch rather than letting it be interpolated. Only complete patches are written: the standard's flags 1 to 3, which let a patch borrow an edge and two colours from the one before it, save bytes in the file and nothing in the drawing, and a caller who repeats the shared edge gets the same picture.

  **How a mesh reaches the file.** The points are packed as a binary stream, sixteen bits per coordinate over the bounding box of the mesh itself and eight per colour component — every field a whole number of bytes. A vertex is eight bytes in RGB, a Coons patch sixty-one, a tensor patch seventy-seven, and the stream is deflated with everything else under **-compress**. The `/Decode` range is computed from the points that are there rather than from the page, because that is what the encoding resolution is spent on. All the colours of one mesh share **one** space, exactly as the stops of a gradient do: a grey corner among coloured ones is promoted, and mixing RGB with CMYK is refused. A shading's space counts for PDF/A like a painted colour (ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.3) — measured with veraPDF: a document carrying all five of these types passes PDF/A-2B and PDF/A-3B with no failed check, and the same mesh under a CMYK output intent is refused at the write, naming the call.

*doc* **shading pattern** *name* *type* ?*options*? ?**-matrix** *{a b c d e f}*?

: Registers a shading as a pattern, usable afterwards as `{pattern name}` in any fill. *type* is any of the seven — `axial`, `radial`, `function`, `triangles`, `lattice`, `coons`, `tensor` — and the options are the ones above.

  **-matrix** maps the gradient into the page. A pattern is bound to the default space of the content stream that carries it — the page here, a form’s own space inside a form — and ignores whatever transformation is active when the shape is painted (ISO 32000-2, 8.7.2) — filled under a transform, the shape lands in the right place and the gradient inside it somewhere else. A caller drawing under a transform passes that transform here — six numbers, not singular, checked before the shading itself is written; the coordinates are then read in the space the matrix maps from. A gradient placed without **-matrix** therefore belongs to the stream it was placed in: using it in another one — placed on the page, filled inside a `form create` script, or the other way round — is refused, because the right matrix would depend on where the form is placed and a form may be placed more than once, which is the one thing PDF does not carry over (the note beside 8.7.2). Define the gradient where it is used, or pass **-matrix** and say which space the numbers are in.

*doc* **shading names**

: The gradients registered so far, by name.

*doc* **pattern create** *name* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-step** *{sx sy}*? ?**-unit** *u*? ?**-matrix** *{a b c d e f}*? ?**-origin** *{x y}*? **-script** *body*

: A tiling pattern. Inside the script the tile is drawn like a small page, and every shape command works unchanged. (PDF 1.2.)

  **-step** is how far apart the tiles sit, the tile size unless given: equal, they touch; larger, and the background shows through between them — a sparse watermark rather than a hatch. Neither the size nor a step may be zero (ISO 32000-1 Table 75). **-unit** reads **-size** and **-step** in another unit than the document's.

  Pattern space hangs off the **content stream**, not off the shape being filled: a tiling pattern is bound to the default space of the stream that carries it — the page, or a form’s own space inside a form — and ignores whatever transformation is active when the shape is painted (ISO 32000-2, 8.7.2). Without a matrix the tiles are laid from the page's bottom left corner, and a rectangle anywhere else gets them cut at whatever phase falls on its edge. **-origin** anchors the tile grid at a point in document coordinates — the unit, y from the top, like **-at** — so a tile begins flush at that point; give the corner of the shape and the first tile sits square in it. **-matrix** takes the six numbers of a PDF matrix — exactly six, and not singular — and writes them as they stand: the raw pattern matrix (Table 75), points, origin at the bottom left, y upwards, for turning or scaling the tile or for a caller who already has the matrix. Both together are refused — a matrix carries its own translation. The matrix belongs to the pattern **object**, which is written once: the same hatch anchored at two corners is two patterns under two names. A tile anchored with **-origin** belongs to the stream it was created in and is refused in another one, the same way a gradient is; a tile with neither **-origin** nor **-matrix** has no place of its own and crosses freely — it lays from the corner of whatever space it lands in, which is what a hatch is for. **pattern size** answers the tile in pattern space, unscaled — a matrix that doubles the tile does not double the answer.

*doc* **pattern names** / *doc* **pattern size** *name*

: The registered tiling patterns, and the size of one of them as `{w h}` in the document unit, before any **-matrix**.

## Reusable content

*doc* **form create** *name* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-unit** *u*? **-script** *body*

: Defines a form XObject — a drawing stored once and placed as often as wanted. Inside the script the origin is the form's own **top left** corner and y counts downwards, the same way it does on a page. **-unit** reads **-size** in another unit than the document's. **-size** is two lengths above zero, checked before the script runs.

*doc* **form place** *name* **-at** *{x y}* ?**-scale** *s*? ?**-fit** *{w h}*? ?**-fitMode** *contain|cover*? ?**-align** *left|center|right*? ?**-valign** *top|middle|bottom*? ?**-rotate** *deg*? ?**-opacity** *o*? ?**-alt** *text*? ?**-artifact** *bool*?

: Places it. **-scale** is a factor above zero, **-rotate** an angle in degrees, **-at** exactly two numbers; each is checked before anything is written. **-fit** *{w h}* names a box instead of a factor and works out the factor from it, keeping the form's proportions — `contain` (the default) fits the whole form inside the box, `cover` covers the whole box and clips what hangs over, and **-align**/**-valign** say where in the box the form sits (`left` and `top` by default). **Without -fit the box has no size, and the same two options are the plain anchor** — exactly as for a picture, and measured against it: a form whose right edge is to sit on the right margin is `-at {190 20} -align right`. One factor, never two: a form scaled unevenly is a distorted drawing, and neither mode asks for that. **-fit** and **-scale** contradict each other and are refused together, as are a fitted box and a **-rotate** that is not zero — a turned placement leaves the upright box it was fitted into. It is the same arithmetic **image place** uses and the same error codes, so a form and a picture given the same box and the same words come out in the same place. Placing is a transformation, not a redraw: the object stays one object in the file. Every form is written as an isolated transparency group (ISO 32000-1, 11.6.6), so **-opacity** fades the placement as one object: where shapes inside the form overlap, the one on top hides the one below at the given opacity, rather than each shape being faded on its own and the overlap coming out darker; and an opacity set inside the form applies within the group, so a translucent element there is faded by the placement too. The group names no colour space of its own: it composites in the colour space of the page it is placed on, which under PDF/A is the one the output intent describes. A group declaring `DeviceRGB` would count as a use of DeviceRGB, which ISO 19005-2 (6.2.4.3) allows only under an RGB output intent — so a document with a CMYK or grey intent (**pdfa -profile**) stays valid whatever profile it carries, and it makes no difference whether **pdfa** is called before or after **form create**. Documents set to a **-version** before `1.4` have no groups, and -opacity then applies per object.

*doc* **form names** / *doc* **form size** *name*

: The names of the forms this document holds, and the size of one of them in the document unit — the created and the imported alike.

## Layers

*doc* **layer create** *name* ?**-title** *text*? ?**-visible** *bool*? ?**-intent** *View|Design*?

: Declares an optional content group (ISO 32000-2, 8.11) — what readers call a layer. **-title** is the name shown in a reader's layer panel; without it the alias stands in, because a row a user cannot tell from the next is worse than a long name. **-visible** *0* starts the group off; the state belongs to the configuration rather than to the group, so **layer state** can change it after the drawing. **-intent** is `View`, `Design` or both (Table 96) — note that the default configuration considers only groups whose intent it shares, and its intent is `View`, so a group created with `Design` alone is not switched by it, which is what the standard prescribes. Optional content is PDF 1.5: a document set to an older **-version** is refused, naming the version it has.

*doc* **layer draw** *name* **-script** *body*

: Puts what the script draws into the group, bracketing it with `/OC /name BDC … EMC`. Layers nest, and so do their brackets; in a tagged document the layer bracket and the structure marks nest cleanly rather than crossing (14.6.1), and the bracket consumes no marked-content identifier of its own. The script runs in the frame that called it, so a document held in a local variable is in reach, and the bracket is closed even when the script fails — the error is then raised as it stands. An empty script writes nothing at all rather than an empty bracket. **A bracket opens and closes in one content stream:** a script that adds a page, or that leaves the stream it began in, is refused by name, and what it drew before is still closed properly. The script may draw anything, **form place** included — placing a form inside a bracket is how a reusable block is made optional.

*doc* **layer names** / *doc* **layer state** *name* ?*bool*? / *doc* **layer radio** *{name name …}*

: The layers this document holds, in the order they were declared; whether one of them starts out visible, read with no value and set with one; and a set of layers that behaves like radio buttons, at most one of them on at a time (`/RBGroups`, Table 99) — two or more names, each a layer of this document, each named once. One language per layer is the usual case.

*doc* **layer configure** ?**-title** *text*? ?**-listMode** *AllPages|VisiblePages*?

: The default configuration's own settings: the name a reader may show for it, and whether its panel lists every group or only those a visible page uses. Read with no arguments. The name may not be empty — ISO 19005-2/-3, 6.9 requires one on every optional content configuration dictionary, and an empty one fails veraPDF's check 6.9-1.

  **What is written** is `/OCProperties` in the catalogue, with `/OCGs` listing every group in the file and a default configuration `/D` carrying `/Name`, `/Order`, `/ON`, and `/OFF`, `/RBGroups` and `/ListMode` where they apply. `/AS` and `/BaseState` are never written. `/Order` lists every group, which is what ISO 19005-2/-3, 6.9 asks of it — so a PDF/A document with layers stays conforming, measured with veraPDF 1.30: a PDF/A-3B document with three layers, one of them off, and a radio group over all three comes out `isCompliant` with 0 failed checks. A page taken over with **pdf import** that brought layers of its own keeps them: its groups are merged into the same entry and appear in `/OCGs` and `/Order`, however many imports and writes follow. **That holds for a document whose only layers came in with an import** — the configuration it gets is written the same way, `/Name` and `/Order` included, because a configuration dictionary without a name is what veraPDF's check 6.9-1 fails and a group missing from `/Order` is one no reader offers to switch.

  **What is not offered** is an `/OC` entry on an XObject or on an annotation. Bracketing the placement does the same for a form or a picture, and says it per placement rather than once for all of them; an annotation, though, cannot be put into a layer with this package — a link is not hidden by one. Alternate configurations (`/Configs`) and usage dictionaries are not written either: one default configuration is what a document written from a script needs, and `/AS` is forbidden under PDF/A anyway.

## Importing a page from an existing PDF

*doc* **pdf import** *alias path* ?**-page** *n*?

: Takes over one page of an existing PDF file as a form: the page is registered under *alias* exactly as **form create** registers one, and **form place** puts it down — as often as wanted, scaled, rotated, faded — while the page is stored once. Taking over a letterhead is one **pdf import** and one **form place** per page; `examples/06.01-pdf-import.tcl` shows it end to end. **-page** counts from 1; without it the first page comes.

  **What is read.** Both cross-reference flavours of ISO 32000: the classic table (7.5.4) and, since PDF 1.5, cross-reference streams with object streams (7.5.7–7.5.8) — measured on a stock of real invoices, 29 of 32 use the stream flavour — hybrid files included, and incremental updates followed over `/Prev`, newest section first. The page's inheritable attributes (`/Resources`, `/MediaBox`, `/CropBox`, `/Rotate`) are taken from the page tree where the page itself is silent. The form's extent is the CropBox where one exists, intersected with the MediaBox (ISO 32000-2, 14.11.2.2) — that is what a viewer shows — and the MediaBox otherwise; a CropBox that does not intersect the MediaBox at all is refused, since nothing of the page would be visible; a `/Rotate` of 90, 180 or 270 becomes the form's matrix, so the placement shows the page upright, the way a viewer shows it, with width and height swapped accordingly in **form size**.

  **What travels, and how.** Everything reachable from the page's resources is copied object by object and renumbered — fonts, images, ICC profiles, extended graphics states — with their bytes and their `/Filter` entries untouched: the import never decodes what it does not have to, so an exotic image filter is no obstacle. The copy walks PARSED objects rather than substituting text, so a literal string that happens to contain `5 0 R` stays a string. Only the page's CONTENT streams are decoded — they become one stream inside the form, written behind this document's own **-compress** — which is why their filters must be ones this package can read: FlateDecode, LZWDecode, CCITTFaxDecode, ASCII85Decode, ASCIIHexDecode, or none; anything else is refused naming the filter. LZW was the general-purpose filter before PDF 1.2 and is read since 2026-08-21, `/EarlyChange` among its parameters — the same decoder reads an LZW-compressed scan, which is where it came from. `CCITTFaxDecode` is read since 2026-08-24 — Group 4 and both flavours of Group 3, with every parameter of Table 11 taken from the stream's `/DecodeParms`: `/Columns`, `/Rows`, `/K`, `/BlackIs1`, `/EncodedByteAlign`, `/EndOfLine`, `/EndOfBlock` and `/DamagedRowsBeforeError`, and `/Height` standing in where `/Rows` is silent. The parameters decide everything, because the geometry is not in the fax data: a wrong `/Columns` does not fail but decodes rows of the wrong width, and `/BlackIs1` is the one whose default is a trap — false means a 0 bit is **black**, so a stream read the other way round gives a perfectly well-formed negative of the page. Refused by name rather than guessed at: a two-dimensional extension code, a stream that ends before its rows are full, a row that codes more colour changes than its width can hold, and a code that is none — the last three tolerated up to `/DamagedRowsBeforeError` rows, after which the refusal names the row. Measured over 25 232 files on this machine: 1681 name the filter and all 11 398 fax images in them are Group 4, so that is the case the decoder was checked hardest on — 406 foreign streams, 570 megapixels, not one pixel different from libtiff and poppler. What this does not change is the writing side: a TIFF whose strips are Group 3 or Group 4 still travels into `/CCITTFaxDecode` untouched, since decoding a scan in order to re-encode it would cost the picture and gain nothing. An optional-content resource (`/Properties`) gets its catalog counterpart written as `/OCProperties`, so the layers stay configurable; groups from several imports are merged into that one entry, and a membership dictionary (OCMD) contributes the groups behind it. The imported page carries the same transparency group a form built with **form create** carries, so **form place -opacity** fades it as a whole.

  **A refused import leaves the document exactly as it was** — every question that can turn a page away is asked before the first object number is handed out, so a `pdf import` the caller caught does not make the document unwritable later on. **What is refused, naming the file:** encrypted files (this package carries no decryption), a page number beyond the count (the message says how many pages the file has), a broken or truncated cross reference, content filters the package cannot decode, a stream whose `/Length` does not end at the `endstream` keyword — a wrong length would silently corrupt the imported content —, a `/Rotate` that is not a multiple of 90 (an integer-valued real such as `90.0`, found in defective files, is read as its integer), a page that has no `/MediaBox`, its own or inherited, since nothing then says how large the page is, and a `/Contents` entry pointing at an object that is not a stream, which is a page description that does not exist. Refusing beats guessing — a letterhead that is silently wrong reaches the recipient.

  **What the import does not judge.** The imported page is taken as it is: **pdfa** and **ua** of this document vouch for what this document draws, not for what the foreign page contains. A document that claims conformance and imports foreign content is the caller's responsibility — validate the result, exactly as `make check` does for the examples.

## Continuing an existing file

**::tclpdf::update open** *path*

: Answers a handle on a finished PDF file, to be continued by an **incremental update** (ISO 32000-2, 7.5.6): the changes are appended to the end of the file and every byte already in it stays exactly where it is. **add**, **reserve**/**put** and **addStream** write objects the file does not have yet, numbering upwards from its `/Size`; **replace** and **replaceStream** write a second copy of an object the file already carries, whose current body **body** hands back as the file states it; **trailer** sets an entry of the added trailer, **id** pins the file identifier, and **write** puts the original bytes and the appendix behind each other — without a path, continuing the file in place. The added cross-reference section carries entries only for what this update wrote, the added trailer carries every entry of the previous one except `Prev` plus a `Prev` of its own, and it ends in its own `%%EOF`.

  **The handle is a package command rather than a document method**, and nothing on the way to a document pulls its module in: `package require tclpdf::update` first. Continuing a file is not something a document being written ever does. The one road that loads the module by itself is **::tclpdf::sign add**, which is built on it — where a second signature has already been put on, the handle is there.

  **Why this exists** is the second signature. A signature covers the bytes of the file it sits in, so signing an already signed document means appending rather than rewriting — there is no other way that leaves the first signature intact. Everything else the handle can do follows from that one purpose; this is not a PDF editor, and it cannot become one: the object graph of a foreign file is not in memory, and what the objects mean is exactly the knowledge a reader does not have.

  **The handle speaks the same vocabulary as the writer**, deliberately: `add`, `reserve`, `put`, `addStream`, `stream`, `ref`, `body` and `count` mean here what they mean while a document is being written. A builder written against the one works on the other unchanged, which is why a signature does not need a second implementation of the objects it builds. `release` is among them: it gives a reserved number back after a refusal, filling it with the null object rather than handing it out again — whoever already points at it is the caller's business, and ISO 32000-1, 7.3.9 makes a reference to a missing object and one to null the same thing. An update session takes nothing back from the file it appends to, but it hands out numbers of its own, and a refusal between `reserve` and `put` would otherwise leave its cross-reference naming an object that is not there.

  **The update is always written as a classic cross-reference table**, also onto a file whose own cross-reference is a stream — and that is the common case, since most foreign files use one. The standard does not say what flavour a section must have given its predecessor's, and the sentence that forbids `xref` and `trailer` (7.5.8.1) applies to files that use streams *entirely*, which a file with an appended table is no longer — a justification that is circular enough not to be leaned on. What the decision rests on is measurement: qpdf, pdfinfo, pdftotext and pyHanko all read such a file, a replaced object overrides even a compressed one inside an object stream, and a PDF/A document stays conformant. It also takes nothing away, where appending a stream would: a reader that cannot read cross-reference streams could not open the original either.

  **Refused by name rather than written wrong:** an encrypted file, whose strings and streams would have to be encrypted with its own key; an object at a generation other than 0; an object stream or a cross-reference stream as the target of **replace**, because the objects compressed inside the one would keep pointing at the copy no longer read and the other is the file's own bookkeeping; and **deleting** an object — 7.5.6 provides for it, but nothing in an update says what still points at the object being dropped, and a dangling reference produces a file that opens with pieces missing.

  **`/Info` and the metadata stream are never rewritten**, so a PDF/A or ZUGFeRD claim in the XMP survives an update untouched — measured on foreign tools, an update that writes fresh metadata without the `pdfaid` properties turns an archivable invoice into an ordinary PDF file. Of `/ID`, the permanent first string is kept and the changing second one is recomputed from what the update appends (14.4), which makes the same update repeatable byte for byte.

## Reading a finished file

**::tclpdf::pdf info** *path*

: What a finished PDF says about itself, as a dictionary — the questions `pdfinfo` answers, out of this package's own parser. It is the reader **pdf import** uses, so nothing new is read and nothing outside has to be installed: no foreign program, no exit status to interpret, no output format to parse. A **package command** rather than a document method, beside **::tclpdf::update open** and **::tclpdf::sign digest**, and for the same reason: the subject is a finished file made by anyone, addressed by its path, while a document object is a file this package is about to write. **The five `::tclpdf::pdf` commands live in the module `tclpdf::importInfo`** — that is the name to hand `package require`, and it is not the name of the ensemble, because the ensemble is the reader's public face while the module is one of several the reader is built from. Nothing else loads it, so a script that only reads files begins with `package require tclpdf` and `package require tclpdf::importInfo`; `examples/06.03-pdf-info.tcl` does exactly that. Every key is always present, with an empty value where the file says nothing, so a field can be read without first asking whether it exists: `path` and `bytes`; `version` and `catalogVersion`; `xref` (`table` or `stream`); `sections` and `revisions`; `id`; `encrypted` and `encryption`; `pages` and `size`; `info` with the document information dictionary; `xmp`, `pdfa` and `pdfua`; `tagged` and `form` (`none`, `AcroForm` or `XFA`, where **pdf fields** answers what stands in the fields); `outputIntents`; `attachments`; `signatures`.

  **`revisions` is not the number of sections.** An incremental update appends its cross-reference section, so its offset is *higher* than the one its `/Prev` names; a linearized file carries a second section at the *front*, chaining forwards to the main one — two sections written in one go. So `revisions` is `sections` minus every step of the chain that runs forwards. Measured over 166 files, that forward step sits exactly where `qpdf --check` reports "File is linearized" and nowhere else. Whether a file *is* linearized is deliberately not claimed: that would need the hint tables checked against what they promise.

  **An encrypted file is answered by *this* command, not refused** — the one place in this package where it is, and it is this command alone: **pdf pages**, **pdf fonts**, **pdf metadata** and **pdf fields** refuse an encrypted file by name, as **pdf import** and **update open** do, because each of the four would have to read cipher text. **pdf import** and **update open** have to refuse one, since they would build a document out of cipher text; an inventory is the caller for which "encrypted, standard handler, revision 6, AES-256" *is* the answer, and it is more than `pdfinfo` gives, which stops at "Command Line Error: Incorrect password". What comes back is what the trailer and the cross-reference say — size, version, revisions, `/ID`, and `encryption` with `filter`, `subfilter`, `v`, `r`, `length`, `p` and `method`, where `method` is the crypt filter's `/CFM` and `subfilter` is the handler's `/SubFilter` — the syntax a handler of its own defines for the rest of the dictionary (Table 20), which the standard handler has none of, so it is empty for every file this package writes and for most it reads. Every content-derived key stays empty, because strings and streams are cipher text without the key, and `/P` is handed on as the number it is rather than decoded: from revision 6 on the authoritative copy of the permissions is the encrypted `/Perms` string, so decoded bits would state as fact what only decryption can confirm.

**::tclpdf::pdf pages** *path* / **::tclpdf::pdf fonts** *path* / **::tclpdf::pdf metadata** *path*

: The three answers that cost more than the summary does, which is why they are separate calls — measured on a 62 MB catalogue of 220 pages, **info** takes 52 ms and **fonts** 1.6 seconds, because the latter walks every page and everything its resources reach.

  **pages** answers one dictionary per page: `number`, `mediabox`, `cropbox`, `rotate`, and the `width` and `height` a viewer shows — the CropBox intersected with the MediaBox that bounds it (14.11.2.2), with the rotation applied, so a page turned by 90 is as wide as its box is high. `pdfinfo` prints the CropBox of page one and states the rotation separately; both numbers are here, with the raw boxes beside them.

  **fonts** answers one dictionary per face: `object`, `subtype`, `cid`, `basefont`, `subset`, `embedded`, `file` (which of `FontFile`, `FontFile2`, `FontFile3` or `CharProcs` carries the glyphs), `encoding`, `differences` and `pages`. The walk covers every page, the forms and patterns its resources reach, **and the appearance streams of annotations** — a face used only by a filled form field or a stamp is in the file just the same. Measured against `pdffonts` on a foreign form: it passes over the `ZapfDingbats` that all 35 checkbox appearances name, and that face is unembedded, so it is as missing on another machine as one in the page content would be. A face without `/BaseFont`, which a Type 3 font has none of, is named by its descriptor's `/FontName`.

  **metadata** answers the XMP packet as it stands in the file, or the empty string. It is handed out raw rather than parsed: reading XML properly means tdom, which this package requires only where a document builds a packet of its own. **info** scans the same packet for `pdfaid:part`, `pdfaid:conformance` and `pdfuaid:part`. Note what that is: a **claim**, not a conformance — veraPDF decides the latter over hundreds of rules.

  **Refused by name:** an encrypted file — **info** is the only one of the five that answers one — a file that is not there, one that carries no `startxref`, a broken or circular cross-reference chain, a stream whose filter this package cannot decode, a page that has no `/MediaBox` anywhere up its tree, and a page whose `/Rotate` is not a multiple of 90 — the same refusals the import makes, for the same reason. **Deliberately not answered**, because an honest answer would need more than reading: the text, words, images and colours of a page (that is rendering — `pdftotext` does it); whether a signature is *valid* (cryptography against a trust store — `pdfsig` does it, and what is reported here is the field, its claims, and whether `/ByteRange` reaches the last byte); whether the file really *is* PDF/A; and annotations other than the form widgets **pdf fields** reports, outlines, page labels and the structure tree, each a tree of its own.

**::tclpdf::pdf fields** *path* ?**-xfa** *bool*?

: What stands in an interactive form (12.7), as a list of dictionaries — one per field, in the order the file lists them, a parent field before the children beneath it. It is the reading half of **field**, and it answers the one question a filled-in form is ever asked: what did the sender type. Until it existed that answer needed a second program — `qpdf --json --json-key=acroform`, or PDFBox behind a Java runtime — for a walk this reader was making anyway, since **info** reports the signatures out of the same tree. A file with no `/AcroForm` answers the empty list rather than an error.

  **`type` carries the words of the writing side**, not the raw `/FT` names: `text`, `check`, `radio`, `button`, `listbox`, `combo` and `signature`, so what comes back as `combo` is what **field combo** writes and the two halves of the package speak one language. `/FT` alone does not say it in any case — three of those words are one `/FT` told apart by a bit of `/Ff` (Tables 229 and 233), which is why the flags are read before the type and not after it. A `/FT` this package does not build travels in the file's own spelling; the words above are lower case, so a raw `Tx`, `Btn` or `Ch` is recognisable as one. A node that names no type at all, which 12.7.4.2 allows for one that only groups names, answers the empty string. `signature` is the one word with no subcommand behind it: such a field is written by **sign**, and what its value claims is answered by **info** under `signatures`.

  Every key is always present, with an empty value where the file says nothing, as everywhere in this reader: `name`, the fully qualified name (12.7.4.2) — the partial names of the chain joined with periods; `partial`, the `/T` of this node alone; `object`, the object number of the field dictionary, empty where the field stands in the array rather than as a reference; `type`; `value`; `default`, which is `/DV` read exactly as `value` is; `flags`; `options`; `selected`; `tooltip`, the `/TU` that 14.9.3 makes the accessible name of the field; `readonly` and `required`, the two flags a caller asks after without wanting the whole list; `pages`; `widgets`; and `appearance`.

  **`/V` is a different data type in every field type**, and a reader that treats them alike answers nonsense — Table 226 says only "(various)" and the types are in the sections below it. A text field holds a string (12.7.5.3). A check box and a radio set hold a **name**, the on state or the `/Off` that 12.7.5.2.3 reserves, which comes back without its slash and is the name the widget's `/AS` points at. A choice field holds the **displayed** text, or an array of them where several are selected. A signature field holds a dictionary (12.7.5.5), which answers `signed`. A push button holds nothing at all — "this type of field retains no permanent value" (12.7.5.2.2) — so a value a file writes there anyway is not passed on. A check box with no `/V` answers empty rather than `Off`: the two mean the same thing to a reader, and every other key is empty where the file is silent.

  **The export value of a choice field is not in `/V`** — it is the first half of the matching `/Opt` entry and stands nowhere else in the field. `options` hands out `/Opt` as *{export display}* pairs, one per option, whether the file wrote the entry as one string or as the two-element array of Table 234; where it wrote one string the two halves are that string, which is what the standard means by it. `selected` answers the export values actually in force: out of `/I`, "the zero-based indices in the Opt array of the currently selected option items", where the file writes one, and otherwise by matching `/V` against the display halves. `/I` wins where it is there, because it is the entry the standard invented for the case the other road cannot decide — two options showing the same text. A value matching no option travels unchanged: an editable combo box may hold whatever its user typed, and that *is* the value. For a check box and a radio button `selected` is `/V` itself without the reserved `/Off`, so an unticked box answers the empty list whether the file writes `/Off` or nothing.

  **`flags` is a list of names rather than a number** — the set bits of `/Ff` in bit order, counted from one as the standard counts them: `ReadOnly`, `Required` and `NoExport` (Table 227), and the ones each type has of its own (Tables 229, 231 and 233). It is not the writing side's table turned round, and the reason is bit 26: it is `RichText` on a text field and `RadiosInUnison` on a button, so one number has two names and only the field type says which. A bit no table names is reported as `bit26` and the like rather than dropped — a foreign file that sets one is saying something.

  **A field is not a widget, and `/T` alone tells them apart.** "A field dictionary that does not have a partial field name (T entry) of its own shall not be considered a field but simply a Widget annotation" (12.7.4.2) — so a radio set of four buttons is one record with four `widgets`, and a node that carries only a name is a record with none. `/FT`, `/Ff`, `/V` and `/DV` are inheritable down `/Kids` (Table 226) and are read as inherited, a parent naming the type while the kids carry the widgets. `widgets` holds one dictionary per widget annotation, in the order the file lists them: `page`, `rect`, `state` — its `/AS` — and `appearance`, whether it carries `/AP /N`. `rect` is the file's own `/Rect`, in PDF units from the bottom left corner of the page, not the top-left **-rect** the writing side counts in: nothing here converts, the file being anyone's. `pages` collects the pages those widgets sit on, ascending and without repeats, counted from 1 as **pages** counts them and not from 0 as **-page** does; a widget no page lists has no page and says so. `appearance` is 1 where **every** widget carries `/AP /N`, 0 where one does not, and empty where the field has no widget at all — a widget without one is drawn by nobody unless the file asks the reader to draw, which is what `/NeedAppearances` does and 12.7.3 deprecates. An invisible signature field answers 0 without anything being wrong with it, its rectangle being degenerate in both axes (12.5.2). Text comes back as Tcl characters either way it is written, PDFDocEncoding or UTF-16BE behind a byte-order mark (7.9.2.2), which is what a reader filling in a form writes.

  **Measured against two other readers** over 65 documents and 703 fields — 56 foreign forms plus this package's own examples: `name`, `type`, `value`, `tooltip` and the widget count agree with Apache PDFBox on every field but one kind, and that one is deliberate — where a check box or a radio set carries no `/V`, PDFBox answers `Off` and this answers empty; `selected` is empty in both readings. `qpdf --json --json-key=acroform` agrees on name, type and flags and differs in what it **counts**: it enumerates widgets, so a radio set of three buttons is three rows there and one record here; it therefore omits a field that has no widget, which this lists as PDFBox does; and its `alternativename` is read off the widget and falls back to the field's name, so the `/TU` of a radio *set* is lost there and answered here.

  **Refused by name:** an **XFA** form (Table 224, deprecated in PDF 2.0) unless **-xfa 1** asks for it. Its data is an XML stream in a format defined outside ISO 32000, and the `/AcroForm` fields beneath it are a shadow copy that need not agree with it — so the file is refused rather than half answered, and the option says "the shadow copy is what I want"; **info** reports the form as `XFA` either way. And an encrypted file, as every read but **info** refuses one: a field name and a text value are strings, and strings are the one thing every security handler encrypts (7.6.2). Measured on a 40-bit RC4 form, `/FT`, `/Ff` and `/AS` come through — names and numbers are not encrypted — and every `/T` and `/V` is binary rubbish. Half an answer with no way to tell which half is why the refusal stands. `examples/09.03-form-read.tcl` reads a form it filled in itself, or any file named on its command line.

## SVG

*doc* **svg** *path* **-at** *{x y}* ?**-width** *w*? ?**-height** *h*? ?**-size** *{w h}*? ?**-scale** *s*? ?**-fit** *{w h}*? ?**-fitMode** *contain|cover*? ?**-align** *left|center|right*? ?**-valign** *top|middle|bottom*? ?**-opacity** *o*? ?**-alt** *text*? ?**-artifact** *bool*?

*doc* **svg -data** *markup* **-at** *{x y}* ?*same options*?

: Draws an SVG as **real vectors** — paths, shapes, groups, transforms, `use`, text and gradients become PDF operators, not a picture. **The shapes follow the geometry rules of the format, not the numbers as they stand:** a corner radius larger than half the side is capped to half (SVG 1.1, 9.2), `rx` and `ry` stand in for one another where only one is given, a radius of zero means a square corner and a `circle` of radius zero draws nothing at all (9.3), and a negative width, height or radius — which 9.2 and 9.3 call an error — is reported through **svg info** rather than drawn as its absolute value. Since a `clipPath` is made of the same shapes, the clipping region follows the same rules. Returns `{x y width height}` of what is **visible** — the rectangle a reader sees, which is what a caption goes under. Fitted rather than stretched, the drawing is smaller than the rectangle asked for and sits inside it; under `slice` or **-fitMode** *cover* it is larger and the rest is clipped away, and then the answer is the box it was cut back to. That is the same rectangle the `Figure` gets as its `/BBox` (ISO 32000-2, 14.8.5.4.3 asks for the rectangle enclosing the visible content), and the same one **image place** and **form place** answer for the same call. Without a size the drawing's own dimensions apply; with one it is fitted, keeping the aspect ratio. **-size** gives both extents at once, **-scale** multiplies the drawing's own size, **-fit** *{w h}* names a box and fits the drawing into it — `contain` (the default) puts the whole drawing inside, `cover` covers the whole box and clips what hangs over — and **-opacity** applies to the drawing as a whole. **-width**, **-height**, **-size** and **-scale** take values above zero; **-at** is exactly two numbers. `opacity`, `fill-opacity` and `stroke-opacity` are honoured; on a shape, `opacity` multiplies with the per-side values. A group element (`g`, `svg`, `a`, `switch`, `use`) with `opacity` below one becomes a **transparency group**: its children composite among themselves first and the alpha applies once to the result, so overlapping children stay exactly as light as a single one. That needs PDF 1.4; below it the alpha is refused with the version in the message, and it is PDF/A-clean under every output intent.

  **-align** and **-valign** say where in a **-fit** box the drawing sits. They default to `center` and `middle`, and that is not an arbitrary choice: it is what a drawing has always done — `xMidYMid meet`, the default of `preserveAspectRatio` — written out, so a script from before the option writes the same bytes. Only a caller who names an edge moves anything.

  **`preserveAspectRatio` is read**, all ten alignments with `meet` and `slice`, and `none`. `meet` fits the drawing inside the rectangle, `slice` covers the rectangle and is clipped back to it, `none` stretches — the one spelling that turns a circle into an ellipse, and it does so because the file asked. The alignment says which edge is kept: `Min` the near one, `Max` the far one, `Mid` splits what is left over. A value in error is the default, as SVG 1.1 7.8 requires, and is reported through **svg info** — a drawing whose fitting differs from what it says is what reading the attribute prevents. **The caller's own options win over the file's:** a script that says **-fitMode** *cover* has asked for a covered box in this document, and a drawing carrying `meet` does not undo it. `defer` is accepted and passed over, since it applies to a `use` pointing at an `image`, which this package does not draw.

  **`clip-rule` is inherited**, as SVG 1.1 6.2 makes it: a value on the `clipPath` itself, or on any element above it, is the default its shapes start from, and a shape's own value beats it. **`clip-path` and `mask` are honoured**, each through the device PDF has for it. A `clipPath` becomes a **clipping path**: its shapes are written as subpaths of one path and clipped with `W` — or `W*` where `clip-rule` says `evenodd` — so several shapes make their **union** the window, which two clipping operators could not (a second one narrows the region, and PDF has no operator that widens one). A `mask` becomes a **luminosity soft mask**: the mask is drawn in greys and the grey value *is* the alpha, so a gradient in the mask fades the shape — something no single opacity value can do. Neither is an approximation and nothing is rasterised. That needs PDF 1.4 for the mask, and the same mask used twice is one resource. `userSpaceOnUse` only: `clipPathUnits` or `maskUnits` saying `objectBoundingBox`, a reference to nothing, a transform on a clipping shape, a shape the package cannot draw, and a mask that draws nothing are each **reported through svg info and the element is then drawn whole** — visible and complete beats invisible or wrongly cut, and the report says which happened. **Filters are not drawn** and are reported the same way.

  **-data** takes the markup from a Tcl variable instead of a file, which is what a generator wants: whatever produces the SVG hands it over directly, with no temporary file in between. Everything else is the same, including **-alt** and **-artifact** — with a description the drawing becomes a `Figure` carrying it, without one an artifact, and **-artifact 1** says that this is what was meant.

  ~~~tcl
  set markup "<svg xmlns=\"http://www.w3.org/2000/svg\"\
      width=\"40\" height=\"40\">\
      <circle cx=\"20\" cy=\"20\" r=\"18\" fill=\"$colour\"/></svg>"
  $doc svg -data $markup -at {20 20} -width 12 -alt "Status: $state"
  ~~~

  Note that **svg size** below takes a file name only; the size of markup in a variable is what drawing it returns.

  A `fill` naming a gradient works on text as it does on a shape; the gradient is measured against the line's em box — the width it was measured at, by the face's ascent and descent — rather than the outlines of the letters, which the text road does not hand back. **Text in a drawing uses the same faces as `text` does.** `font-family` is a comma-separated wish list and the first name that resolves wins; an **embedded** face resolves under the alias it was embedded as, so `font-family="house"` finds it after `font embed house …`. That settles three things at once: the drawing may use any character the face has rather than the 224 positions of WinAnsi — `Łódź` and `Москва` are ordinary labels, not errors —, it can be set in the house face, and it can be part of an **archivable** document, which embeds every font it finds. Kerning and ligatures apply as they do elsewhere.

  A list nobody can satisfy ends at `helvetica` rather than failing, which is what a drawing wants; in a PDF/A document that face is not embedded and `write` then refuses the document, so it is worth naming a face the document has. **`font-weight` is read**: `bold`, `bolder`, or a number, where 600 and up is bold — the line CSS 2.1 15.5 draws. A family with no bold cut of its own, which is every **embedded** one since an alias carries a single cut, is **reported through svg info** rather than quietly coming out light; the way to a bold embedded face is to embed it under an alias and name that. The document's own weight is never inherited by a drawing — a drawing must not come out bold because the running text around it happens to be. `font-style` is not read.

*doc* **svg info**

: What the LAST drawing skipped — the elements the module does not draw. It reports on the document, not on a file: anything written after **info** is accepted and ignored, so `svg info some.svg` says nothing about `some.svg`.

*doc* **svg size** *path*

: The natural size of an SVG file.

### Barcodes

tclpdf has no barcode encoder and does not need one. **tzint**, the Tcl binding for libzint, encodes into SVG, and `svg -data` draws that as real vectors — a MaxiCode keeps its hexagons and rings, and the digits under an EAN stay characters that can be copied. No temporary file is involved: the markup goes from one variable into the drawing.

tzint is **not a dependency**. It is a C extension and therefore platform bound; nothing in tclpdf requires it, and a document that never draws a barcode never notices it.

~~~tcl
package require tzint
::tzint::Encode svg markup "1234567890128" -barcode ean13
$doc svg -data $markup -at {20 20} -height 16 -alt "EAN-13 1234567890128"
~~~

Two things about the encoder are worth knowing, because neither is obvious and both cost an afternoon:

**The status is three-valued.** `0` means silence, **`1` to `4` are warnings with a perfectly good symbol**, and only `5` and up mean nothing was produced. Code that tests for "not zero" throws usable barcodes away — and on an error the target variable is not cleared but **left as it was**, so code that looks at the variable instead of the status quietly draws the previous barcode again. Both were measured: a Euro sign is a warning for `qrcode` and an error for `code128`.

~~~tcl
set rc [::tzint::Encode svg markup $data -barcode qrcode -stat info]
if {$rc >= 5} {
    error "no barcode: [dict get $info error]"
}
~~~

**An EPC-QR (GiroCode) needs `-eci 26`.** The dataset states its own character set in line 3, and without the option the encoder picks one itself — the symbol scans, but its encoding is not the one the data claims. `-security 2` is the error correction level the specification asks for.

For an **archivable** document the clear text line needs an embedded face, and the encoder names the one it wants: tzint writes `font-family="OCRB, monospace"` into its markup, so embedding a face under the alias `OCRB` is enough — the digits are then set in real OCR-B, stay text rather than becoming picture, and the markup is not touched. Where that face is not to hand, `-notext 1` leaves the line out and `text` sets it.

## Attachments, links and bookmarks

*doc* **attach** *path* ?**-name** *n*? ?**-mime** *m*? ?**-description** *d*? ?**-relationship** *r*? ?**-date** *d*? ?**-compress** *1*?

*doc* **attach -data** *bytes* **-name** *n* ?*same options*?

: Attaches a file. `-relationship` is the `/AFRelationship` value — `Alternative`, `Data`, `Source`, `Supplement` or `Unspecified`. (PDF 1.3.)

  **-mime** is the media type written as `/Subtype`, `application/octet-stream` unless given, and takes only `type/subtype`; **-description** goes into `/Desc`; **-date** is the modification date in `/Params`, a PDF date like `info CreationDate` takes; **-compress** is on by default. **-name** may not contain `/` or `\`; a name outside printable ASCII is written to `/UF` as text and to `/F` with such characters replaced by one `_` per code point — one, not one per UTF-16 unit, so the fallback bytes are identical under Tcl 8.6 and 9. **-data** may be empty; it must be bytes (encode text first).

  **-data** takes the bytes instead of a file — for an attachment that never was one. **-name** is then required, because there is no file name to fall back on; without it the call is refused.

*doc* **attachments**

: What has been attached.

*doc* **link -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* ?**-url** *u*? ?**-page** *n*? ?**-structure** *name*? ?**-to** *{x y}*? ?**-zoom** *z*? ?**-tooltip** *t*?

: A link rectangle over an area of the page — to a URL, to a page of this document, or to a named structure element. It is drawn as nothing: the visible text is a separate call. (PDF 1.1.)

  **-page** is the page index, counted from 0 as `page current` counts — not the number printed on the page. The page need not exist yet: a link on the first page may point at a contents page added last, and the target is resolved when the document is written — a page that never came is then reported, naming the link. A negative index is refused at once. `bookmark -page` works the same way.

  **-url** is written in 7-bit ASCII as the format demands: whatever is outside it — an umlaut, a space — becomes `%XX` from its UTF-8 bytes, and a `%` already in the address is left alone, so an encoded URL is not encoded twice.

  For a page destination, **-to** names the point to land on and **-zoom** the magnification the reader applies there (`1` is 100 %); without **-zoom** the reader keeps the one it has, and without **-to** the whole page is fitted and **-zoom** does not apply.

  **-structure** takes the **-name** of a `structure` element and writes a structure destination (12.3.2.3), which names the element rather than a place on a page and therefore still lands on the right thing after the content above it has moved. PDF/UA-2 asks for internal targets to be written that way. It is written as a GoTo action carrying both the structure destination (/SD) and a page destination (/D) to the element's first page — /XYZ at the top of its first content, or /Fit when no position is known — so a reader that does not understand structure destinations still lands on the right page. It needs a tagged document and a 2.0 file (`ua -part 2` gives both). `bookmark` takes the same option.

  **-tooltip** becomes the annotation's `Contents`, which PDF/UA requires on every link (7.18.5): it is what a reader announces instead of just saying "link". Under PDF/UA the link annotation must also sit inside a `structure Link` (part 2: `Link` or `Reference`).

*doc* **bookmark** *title* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-at** *{x y}*? ?**-parent** *id*? ?**-open** *1*? ?**-structure** *name*?

: Adds an outline entry and returns its id, which can be the `-parent` of further entries. **-page** counts from 0, as `page current` counts, and defaults to the current page; as with `link -page`, the page need not exist yet — the target is resolved when the document is written, and a page that never came is then reported, naming the bookmark; a negative index is refused at once. Bookmarks are turned into objects when the document is written. **-open** decides whether the entry shows its children unfolded, and is on by default; `-open 0` collapses a branch until the reader asks for it. Each entry's `/Count` is the number of descendants a reader shows for it, not the number of its children, and the root counts everything visible at all levels (ISO 32000-1 12.3.3). The catalog then carries `/PageMode /UseOutlines`, so a reader opens the outline pane — unless a `catalogEntry PageMode` was set already.

*doc* **bookmarks**

: The outline built so far.

*doc* **destination** *page* ?**-to** *{x y}*? ?**-zoom** *z*?

: A destination in PDF syntax (ISO 32000-1 12.3.2) — where a link or a bookmark points. `link -page` and `bookmark` build theirs through it; calling it directly is for a place the package does not write itself, and **catalogEntry** is the standing one: `catalogEntry OpenAction [$doc destination 1]` makes a reader open the document on the second page. *page* counts from 0 as `page current` counts — not the number printed on the page.

  **-to** and **-zoom** are `link`'s two options, because they end up here: without **-to** the whole page is fitted (`/Fit`) and **-zoom** does not apply; with **-to** *{x y}* — in the document unit, from the top left corner as every coordinate here — the reader lands on that point (`/XYZ`), and **-zoom** is the magnification it applies there, `1` being 100 %; without **-zoom** the reader keeps the one it has.

  For a page that exists the call returns the destination array itself; a *page* beyond the last is allowed and returns a reference to an object filled when the document is written — a link on the first page may point at a contents page added last. A page that never came is then reported — as the `link` or the `bookmark` that asked for it, and as `a destination` when this method was called directly. A negative or non-numeric *page* is refused at once.

### Vertical writing

**The CMap is the smaller half.** `-direction ttb` writes `/Identity-V` where a horizontal line writes `/Identity-H`, as a second Type 0 font over the same descendant, the same descriptor and one embedded font file — the writing mode is a property of the CMap and not a second copy of the face. What actually decides whether the page is right is that **the metric is a different one**: horizontally a glyph advances by its width out of `hmtx`, vertically it advances by its **height** and hangs from a vertical origin, and neither number is in `hmtx`. They come from `vhea`/`vmtx`, or from `VORG` where an OpenType/CFF face carries one, and PDF wants them restated in the CID font as `/W2` and `/DW2`. Measured in Noto Sans JP: U+3031, the vertical kana repeat mark, is 1000 units wide and **2000 tall** — a column built from the horizontal advances puts it in half the room it needs, the glyph below overlaps it, and qpdf, veraPDF and `pdftotext` all call the file clean.

**`/DW2` is written at the standard's own default, `[880 -1000]`, and `/W2` only for the glyphs that differ from it.** That is not a saving of a few bytes: in Noto Sans JP 16 522 of 17 103 glyphs sit at the default origin and 17 096 advance the default height, so a document of ordinary Japanese writes no `/W2` at all. An entry, where one is needed, is three numbers — the vertical displacement and the position vector, whose x is half the horizontal advance. Neither is written for a face nothing was set vertically with, so a document that never asks for a column is unchanged.

**A face with no vertical metric is not refused.** ISO 32000-2 defines the case and gives it a default of one em per glyph, so DejaVu Sans sets a column of square cells — the only defined answer — and the file says `/DW2` and no `/W2`.

**Some of the glyphs are other glyphs.** A bracket, the ideographic comma and full stop and the ellipsis have vertical forms — the comma moves from the bottom left of its square to the top right, the brackets turn — and a face names them in the GSUB feature `vert`. They are a lookup and not a rotation: the horizontal glyph turned by ninety degrees gives a bracket leaning the wrong way. tclpdf applies `vert`, falling back to `vrt2` for a face that has no `vert`. That inverts the preference the OpenType specification states, and it is measured rather than assumed: in Noto Sans JP `vert` holds 383 substitution rules and `vrt2` holds 378, the 378 are the same rules with the same outputs, and the five only `vert` has include the fullwidth colon. The substitution does not reach the `ToUnicode` map — both the horizontal glyph and its vertical form map back to the character that was written, so a column extracts in the order it was set.

**The columns are broken here, and placed by the caller.** `textLines $text -width $columnHeight -direction ttb` breaks a vertical text into columns and returns them in reading order — **-width** is the column *height* for such a text, because what the breaker asks of every candidate is "how far does this reach" and that is answered along whichever axis the text writes. Placing them is a loop of one **text** call per column at its own **-at**, moving leftwards, which is also what decides that Japanese columns run right to left:

~~~tcl
set x 180
foreach column [$doc textLines $text -width 120 -direction ttb] {
    $doc text $column -at [list $x 30] -direction ttb
    set x [expr {$x - 8}]
}
~~~

**What is deliberately not built is the placing.** `text -width -direction ttb` is refused, and the message names the two lines above as the way through. A horizontal block steps its lines *down* the page by the leading; a vertical one has to step its columns *left* by it — and the height limit with its remainder, the column balance, the flow around shapes and the table are all written along that one axis. Turning them is the second half of this subject rather than a flag, and half of it built would be worse than the loop. There is still **no vertical table**.

## Notes, stamps and marked-up text

An annotation is a remark laid over a page rather than drawn into it. The link of the previous section and the widget of a form field are annotations too; this section holds the kinds that say something *about* a page — a sticky note, a rubber stamp, the four ways of marking a passage of text, the five geometry annotations, and a file clipped to the place it belongs. Each is a subcommand of **annot**, and each returns the object number of the annotation it wrote.

Who draws the picture decides everything else. A note, and a stamp given **-name**, are drawn by the *reader* from a fixed set of symbols — which is what makes a note look like the reader's other notes, and which is also why a document claiming PDF/A refuses one: ISO 32000-2, Table 166 requires an appearance stream of every annotation, and veraPDF fails such a file under clause 6.3.3. The way out is **-appearance**, which hands the annotation a form built with `form create`; then it looks the same everywhere. The four text markups need none of that — the package draws their appearance itself, always and without being asked, because the marked rectangles and the colour are the whole picture.

*doc* **annot note -at** *{x y}* **-contents** *text* ?**-icon** *n*? ?**-title** *t*? ?**-colour** *c*? ?**-open** *1*? ?**-size** *{w h}*? ?**-appearance** *form*? ?**-opacity** *o*? ?**-date** *seconds*?

: A closed sticky note: a symbol on the page, and the text behind it. **-contents** is required — it *is* the note, and under PDF/UA it is the description a reader announces as well. **-icon** picks the symbol: `Comment`, `Key`, `Note` (the default), `Help`, `NewParagraph`, `Paragraph` or `Insert` (ISO 32000-2, Table 174); a name outside them is refused, because no reader draws one. **-open 1** shows the note unfolded when the document is opened. **-size** defaults to the 20 by 20 points a reader draws its symbol in — the symbol does not scale with the rectangle, so the number decides only where it sits. The annotation carries `/F 28`: printed, and neither zoomed nor rotated with the page. (PDF 1.1.)

*doc* **annot stamp -at** *{x y}* **-name** *n* **-size** *{w h}* ?*shared options*?

*doc* **annot stamp -at** *{x y}* **-appearance** *form* ?**-size** *{w h}*? ?*shared options*?

: A rubber stamp. **-name** is one of the fourteen a reader draws itself — `Approved`, `Experimental`, `NotApproved`, `AsIs`, `Expired`, `NotForPublicRelease`, `Confidential`, `Final`, `Sold`, `Departmental`, `ForComment`, `TopSecret`, `Draft`, `ForPublicRelease` (ISO 32000-1, Table 181) — and **-size** is then required, since there is nothing to derive one from. **-appearance** names a form built with `form create`, and its own size is used where **-size** is left out, so the numbers are not written in two places and cannot come to disagree. The two are exclusive: with an appearance present a reader never looks at `/Name`. (PDF 1.3.)

*doc* **annot highlight** | **underline** | **strikeout** | **squiggly** *…*

: Marks a passage that is already on the page — highlighted, underlined, struck through, or underlined with a squiggle. What such an annotation consists of is its `/QuadPoints`, the rectangles of the marked text, and there are three ways to say where those are; one of them is required. (`underline` and `strikeout` are PDF 1.3; `squiggly` is PDF 1.4 because the standard says so, and `highlight` because its band is drawn under the `Multiply` blend mode, which is.)

  **-text** *string* **-at** *{x y}* marks ONE line and repeats what the `text` call that drew it said — the same string and the same point, plus **-align** and **-anchor** where those were given. The width is `textWidth`'s, which is the width that was drawn; the height is the face's ascender above the baseline plus its descender below it, so the band covers the capitals and the descenders and nothing else.

  **-lines** *{…}* **-at** *{x y}* marks a PARAGRAPH, and takes what `textLines` answered for it — the plain list, or the dictionaries of `-hyphens 1`; both are read. One quadrilateral per line, one leading apart, and *one* annotation for the whole passage, which is what `/QuadPoints` is an array for and what makes a reader announce the remark once rather than once per line. The leading is the text state's own, which is what the block used; **-leading** overrides it for a block broken some other way.

  **-quads** *{{x y w h} …}* takes the rectangles directly, counted from the top left corner as `-at` is everywhere else. It is the road for a passage this package did not set, for a table cell whose geometry the caller already has, for a justified block — whose lines were stretched after they were measured — and for text in a Type 3 face, which carries no descender to measure with. Each of those three refusals names this option.

  The colour is `/C` and defaults to yellow for `highlight` and red for the other three; the appearance is drawn in the same colour, the highlight under the `Multiply` blend mode so that the words show through the band rather than disappearing under it.

  Shared with the note and the stamp: **-contents**, the description; **-title**, the owner of the remark (`/T`, which a reader shows in the note's title bar); **-colour** (or **-color**), which takes a grey, an RGB or a CMYK colour, those being the three `/C` has a spelling for; **-opacity** *0…1*, which fades the whole annotation, appearance and all (`/CA`, PDF 1.4); **-date** *seconds*, a time as `clock seconds` answers, written to `/M` and `/CreationDate` — without it an annotation carries no date at all, so that the same script writes the same bytes twice; and **-appearance**, which puts a form of one's own in place of the picture that would otherwise be drawn.

*doc* **annot line -from** *{x y}* **-to** *{x y}* / **annot square** | **circle -at** *{x y}* **-size** *{w h}* / **annot polygon** | **polyline -points** *{{x y} …}* ?**-colour** *c*? ?**-fill** *c*? ?**-width** *w*? ?*shared options*?

: The geometry annotations (12.5.6.7 to 12.5.6.9): a line, a rectangle, an ellipse and the two poly kinds, laid over the page as remarks. **The difference from drawing the same shape is not the picture but what it is**: a line drawn with **line** is content — it prints, and a reader can neither switch it off nor ask who put it there — while the same line as an annotation carries an author, a date and a description, and a reader lists it beside the other remarks. That is why the two exist side by side rather than one replacing the other.

  The coordinates are the caller's own, counted from the top left as everywhere else in this API; `/L`, `/Vertices` and `/Rect` count from the bottom left and the conversion happens where the numbers are written. **-colour** is the outline (`/C`, red by default, since a remark in the text colour does not read as a remark) and **-fill** the inside (`/IC`) — the two words the drawing methods already use. A line and a polyline have no inside, so an **-fill** on them writes no `/IC`. **-width** is the thickness, and `/Rect` is grown by half of it on every side: a line is drawn *on* its path, so half its thickness lies outside the points, and a reader that clips to `/Rect` would cut that half away.

  `circle` is the standard's word and it means an **ellipse** inscribed in the rectangle, not a circle — worth saying once, because the name misleads. **The appearance is drawn without being asked for**, unlike a note's: the picture follows entirely from the geometry, the colours and the width, so there is nothing left for a reader to decide and nothing to refuse under a PDF/A claim. Square, circle and line are PDF 1.3; polygon and polyline are 1.5, and the version guard says so.

*doc* **annot attachment -at** *{x y}* **-name** *n* ?**-icon** *Graph|Paperclip|PushPin|Tag*? ?**-appearance** *form*? ?**-size** *{w h}*? ?*shared options*?

: A file that travels with the document, sitting at a point on the page (12.5.6.15). **-name** is the name an **attach** call gave the file, not a path — the annotation points at a file that is already embedded rather than embedding a second copy, and a name nothing was attached under is refused. What it adds over the document's attachment list is *where*: an attachment reached through the list is a file somebody has to know is there, while this one is a paperclip at the paragraph it belongs to. **Under ZUGFeRD it is neither** — the invoice XML is reached through `/AF` and belongs to no page.

  Without **-appearance** the picture is the reader's icon, exactly as it is for **annot note**, and under a claim that requires an appearance the annotation is refused for the same reason a note is. **-appearance** names a form built with **form create** and is the way through; `/Name` is then not written at all, since `/AP` wins over it (Table 166) and a `/Name` nothing draws is a statement with no reader.

Under PDF/UA the caller does one thing and the package does the rest. **-contents** is required on every annotation and refused without it, naming the call — it is what a reader announces, and "annotation" is what it says otherwise (ISO 14289-1, 7.18.1). The structure element is the package's: one `Annot` element per annotation, opened inside whatever element is open at the call, which is what puts it in reading order, with `/StructParent` in the annotation and the `/OBJR` back to it in the element. veraPDF checks both by name. An annotation written before `tagged 1` stands outside the tree, and that is reported when the document is written.

## Interactive form fields

A form field is one call: `$doc field text customer -rect {20 40 80 8} -value "Erika Mustermann"`. What makes the result a *form* rather than a drawing is written by the package — an `/AcroForm` entry in the catalogue listing the fields, one widget annotation per field on the page it sits on, and an appearance stream per widget, which is the piece that decides whether a form is worth anything, since it is what a reader actually shows. Six types are provided, each a subcommand of **field**: `text`, `check`, `radio`, `button`, `listbox` and `combo`. Every one of them takes **-rect** *{x y w h}*, counted from the **top left** corner of the page in the unit of the document — as `link -at` with `link -size` and `sign -rect` count — and **-page** *n*, the page index counted from 0 as `page current` counts, defaulting to the page being drawn on. The page need not exist yet, but it has to exist when the document is written and is reported by name if it never came. A rectangle of no area is refused at the call; that it is four numbers a PDF can hold is checked there too. **Where it lands is a question for the page**, and therefore for the write: a rectangle sticking out over the page edge is allowed, one lying entirely beside the page is refused when the document is written, with both rectangles in the message — that is where a *y* counted from the bottom, and a **-rect** in millimetres on a document set in points, show up. (PDF 1.2.)

*name* is the partial field name (`/T`, ISO 32000-2, 12.7.4.2): the word an exported form, a script inside a reader and a filled document all address the field by, so it is the caller's and not a generated one. It may hold no period — the fully qualified name is joined out of the partial names with periods between them, so a period inside one names a parent field that does not exist — and it has to be free: a name already taken, by another field or by `sign -field`, is refused, since two fields under one name resolve to one and nothing reports the one that becomes unreachable. `field list` answers the names in the order they were declared, which is the order they go into `/Fields`.

**/NeedAppearances is never written**, not even as `false`. It is the flag that asks the reader to draw the fields instead, and ISO 32000-2, Table 224 marks it deprecated in PDF 2.0. Three reasons, and the first is enough: the standard says what the ingredients are (`/DA`, `/DR`, `/MK`, `/Q`) and nowhere how a reader is to combine them, so the result is unpredictable; a reader that is not interactive does not draw at all; and the flag fires field events on opening, which on a signed document is the last thing anyone wants. Every `/AP` is drawn by the package instead — which is also the only way a form passes PDF/A, Table 166 requiring an appearance dictionary on every annotation whose subtype is `Widget` and veraPDF failing a file without one in every part (clause 6.3.3). An absent key means false, and that is what a reader is told either way: `qpdf --json --json-key=acroform` over a file written here answers `"needappearances": false` with the key nowhere in it.

*doc* **field text** *name* **-rect** *{x y w h}* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-value** *text*? ?**-default** *text*? ?**-maxlen** *n*? ?**-align** *left|center|right*? ?**-multiline** *bool*? ?**-readonly** *bool*? ?**-required** *bool*? ?**-noexport** *bool*? ?**-password** *bool*? ?**-family** *f*? ?**-style** *s*? ?**-size** *n*? ?**-color** *c*? ?**-border** *c*? ?**-borderWidth** *n*? ?**-background** *c*? ?**-tooltip** *text*? ?**-contents** *text*? ?**-label** *script*?

: A field to type into (`/FT /Tx`, 12.7.5.3). Returns the name, as every field type does. Field and widget are written as a single object, which 12.5.6.19 permits wherever a field has exactly one widget and which every type here but the radio set therefore uses. **-value** is what stands in it (`/V`) and **-default** what a reset button puts back (`/DV`); a line break in either needs **-multiline 1**, a single-line field showing one line, and it goes into the file as the carriage return the standard prescribes rather than as the line feed a Tcl script hands in. **-maxlen** is the greatest number of characters the field takes (`/MaxLen`), 1 or more, and a value longer than it is refused rather than left for a reader to truncate at the first keystroke. **-align** is `/Q` — `left`, `center` or `right`; the default is left and is not written, a key that may only ever hold its own default being one to leave out. **-readonly**, **-required** and **-noexport** are the three flags of Table 227 that every type has; **-multiline** and **-password** two of Table 231.

  **-password refuses -value and -default.** Table 231 says of the Password flag that a PDF processor shall "never store the value of the text field in the PDF file", and `/V` is plain text in a file anyone can open with a text editor — so the value is refused rather than quietly dropped, a package whose refusals cannot be trusted being worse than one that says no. **-password** and **-multiline** cannot both be set either: the same table forbids the pair, a field that echoes nothing having no second line to echo it on.

  **-family**, **-style**, **-size** and **-color** are the font the field is set in — the same four names `font` takes, falling back on **field default** where they are left out. They go into the field's default appearance string (`/DA`, 12.7.4.3) **and** into the appearance the package draws, settled at the call so that the two cannot disagree; drawing them at the write instead read the document's text state, and a field came out in the courier of an unrelated code block three paragraphs further down the page. Naming the font also registers it, which is what makes the `/DA` resolvable against `/DR` and an embedded face usable in a field — a `/DA` naming a font `/DR` does not have passes `qpdf --check` without a word and draws as nothing in a reader. **-size** is a size in points above zero: a size of 0 is legal and means auto-size, which a package that draws the appearance itself cannot predict a reader's redrawing of. The colour is grey, RGB or CMYK — `/DA` is read outside any content stream, so the name of a colour space resource would resolve against nothing.

  **-border**, **-borderWidth** and **-background** are the frame and the fill. They are drawn into the appearance **and** written as `/MK /BC` and `/BG` (Table 192), with a `/BS` beside them: the stream is what a reader shows, `/MK` is what it rebuilds one from the moment a user types in the field, and without it a field with a drawn frame loses the frame at the first keystroke. A colour there is grey, RGB or CMYK for a second reason — Table 192 holds components rather than an operator and tells the space apart by counting them. **-borderWidth 0** is a field with no frame. The value is drawn inside a `/Tx BMC` … `EMC` bracket and the frame outside it, which is not cosmetic: a reader setting a new value replaces the stream from that mark to the matching `EMC`, and the frame is not the value.

*doc* **field check** *name* **-rect** *{x y w h}* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-checked** *bool*? ?**-default** *bool*? ?**-export** *name*? ?**-mark** *check|cross|circle|square*? ?**-markSize** *n*? ?**-color** *c*? ?**-border** *c*? ?**-borderWidth** *n*? ?**-background** *c*? ?**-readonly** *bool*? ?**-required** *bool*? ?**-noexport** *bool*? ?**-tooltip** *text*? ?**-contents** *text*? ?**-label** *script*?

: A check box — `/FT /Btn` with neither the Radio nor the Pushbutton flag set (12.7.5.2.3). **-checked** is the state it starts in, **-default** the state a reset puts it back into, and **-export** the name of the **on** state, `Yes` unless given. That name is what an exported form carries as the value of the field, so it is the caller's word: a form whose boxes all export `Yes` says nothing about which box it was. `Off` is the one name the standard reserves, for the off state, and is refused as an export value. The value of a button is a name object rather than a text string, and `/V` and `/AS` are written from one source here, so the two cannot drift apart — a reader given two that disagree silently prefers `/AS`.

  **Both states are drawn**, always. `/AP /N` of a check box is a subdictionary with one entry per state (12.5.5, Table 170), and leaving the off entry out is what makes the frame of an unticked box disappear: a reader "shall also attempt to provide reasonable behaviour (such as displaying nothing)" for a state it has no appearance for, and displaying nothing is exactly what happens. With both in the file a reader toggling the box switches `/AS` between two streams that are already there and regenerates nothing.

  **-mark** is the shape drawn inside the box — `check`, `cross`, `circle` or `square`, `check` unless given — and **-markSize** its size in the unit of the document, fitted to the box where it is left out. The four are **paths, not glyphs**: the examples in 12.7.5.2.3 set the tick in ZapfDingbats, which would put a font into the file for four vector strokes, tie the mark to the glyph repertoire of one face and make a check box depend on the text topic. No `/MK /CA` is written for a check box or a radio button, for the same reason — it is the caption a reader builds a *dynamic* appearance from, and neither of the two ever needs one built.

*doc* **field radio** *name* **-buttons** *{{value {x y w h} ?description? ?-page n? ?-label script? ?-contents text?} …}* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-value** *name*? ?**-default** *name*? ?**-unison** *bool*? ?**-notoggle** *bool*? ?**-mark** *check|cross|circle|square*? ?**-markSize** *n*? ?**-color** *c*? ?**-border** *c*? ?**-borderWidth** *n*? ?**-background** *c*? ?**-readonly** *bool*? ?**-required** *bool*? ?**-noexport** *bool*? ?**-tooltip** *text*?

: A set of radio buttons, which is **one field with several widgets**: the field carries `/Kids` and each button is a widget annotation beneath it (12.7.5.2.4). It cannot be built any other way — "a field dictionary that does not have a partial field name (T entry) of its own shall not be considered a field but simply a Widget annotation" (12.7.4.2) — so four radio buttons are four widgets and one field, the whole set is declared in one call, and `field list` and `field state` show one field however many buttons it has. A single switch that is on or off is **field check**.

  **-buttons** is the set: one *{value {x y w h} ?description? ?-page n? ?-label script? ?-contents text?}* entry per button, in the order they are to stand in `/Kids`. The value is the name of that button's **on** state and what an exported form carries when it is the one selected; the rectangle counts from the top left corner of the page like every other **-rect**; the third word is that button's own accessible description, written as `/Contents` on its widget. After it the entry goes on in *-name value* pairs, the form every call of this package takes: **-page** *n* is the page **that button** sits on, the field's own **-page** where it is left out; **-label** *script* draws that button's visible caption; **-contents** *text* is the description by name. The dash is what tells an option from the description, so a description that itself begins with one — `-- none of them --` — is given as **-contents**, and saying it both ways is refused, both being the one `/Contents` of that widget. **-value** and **-default** name one of those values — `/V` of a radio field is "a name object corresponding to the appearance state of whichever child field is currently in the on state" — and a value no button answers to is refused, naming the ones there are. It is called **-buttons** rather than **-options** because in this package "options" means the *-name value* pairs of a call, and because 12.7.5.2.4 calls them buttons.
