  **-unison** is the RadiosInUnison flag (Table 229, PDF 1.5), which makes buttons sharing an on-state name switch together; two buttons under one name **without** it are refused, being a set in which two buttons light up at once and nothing says why. **-notoggle** is NoToggleToOff — "exactly one radio button shall be selected at all times" — and it needs **-value**: a set written with the flag and nothing selected opens in the one state its own flag forbids, and the way out would be the click that turns a button off. The default **-mark** here is `circle`, and a circular mark gets a round frame, a dot in a square box being a check box drawn wrong.

  **A set may span pages.** Each button carries its own **-page**, so a form that asks the same question on every sheet collects all the answers in **one** field: `/Kids` holds "an array of widget annotations representing the individual buttons in the set" and nothing there says they share a page. Buttons that repeat one question carry one on-state name and **-unison 1** with it, so that ticking the box on one sheet ticks it on the rest. Measured with `qpdf --json --json-key=acroform`: one field, every button with a `parent` and an empty `partialname` — 12.7.4.2, a dictionary without a `/T` of its own "shall not be considered a field but simply a Widget annotation" — and `pageposfrom1` counting the sheet each one landed on.

  **A radio field takes no -contents and no -label; its buttons take both.** Each describes ONE widget and the field is the whole set — a `/TU` on a field with four widgets describes none of the four (ISO 14289-2, 8.10.2.4) — so a button's description is the third word of its entry and its label is that entry's **-label**. **A -label script that draws nothing is not a label:** the element is asked afterwards whether anything reached it, and an empty one counts as a missing description rather than passing a claim the recipient's validator then refuses. The label is the caption seen beside the button, and it goes where 8.10.2.2 puts it: into a `Lbl` inside the `Form` structure element of **that** button and of no other, before the object reference, because the order of `/K` is the reading order. The script runs in the caller's scope and draws with the caller's own variables. A label for the **group** is a third thing: 8.10.2.2 wants that one "within the parent structure element that also contains … the Form structure element for each widget", which is the element the caller opened, so it is drawn beside the field in a `structure Lbl` of its own.

  **A button's label needs PDF 2.0, and so therefore does a labelled set.** ISO 32000-1, Table 340 let a `Form` element hold only the object reference, and veraPDF holds a 1.7 file to it (rule 7.18.4-2); ISO 14289-1, 6.1 makes PDF/UA-1 a PDF 1.7 format, so **ua -part 1** refuses a 2.0 document before a validator ever sees it. A UA-1 form therefore describes its buttons with the third word and a UA-2 form may label them: measured, the same labelled bytes pass `verapdf -f ua2` with no findings and fail `-f ua1` under 7.18.4-2. Take the label away again and UA-2 answers 8.10.2.3-1, "If a label for a widget annotation is not present, a Contents entry shall be provided to supply description and context for the widget".

*doc* **field button** *name* **-rect** *{x y w h}* **-caption** *text* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-action** *reset*? ?**-fields** *{name …}*? ?**-family** *f*? ?**-style** *s*? ?**-size** *n*? ?**-color** *c*? ?**-border** *c*? ?**-borderWidth** *n*? ?**-background** *c*? ?**-readonly** *bool*? ?**-tooltip** *text*? ?**-contents** *text*? ?**-label** *script*?

: A push button (the Pushbutton flag, 12.7.5.2.2). It has **no -value, no -default and no -checked**: such a field "shall not use the V and DV entries in the field dictionary", "because this type of field retains no permanent value" — and no **-required** and no **-noexport** either, both of those speaking about a value it does not have. **-caption** is therefore the whole of what the button says and is required. It is drawn into the appearance **and** written as `/MK /CA`, with a `/DA` beside it, because a reader may well rebuild the appearance of a button and those two are what it has to work from. The four font options are the caption's and are named as **field text** names them. `/AP /N` is a subdictionary here too, although a push button has one appearance and 12.5.5 would take the stream directly: veraPDF fails rule 6.4.1-1 over a widget whose `/FT` is `Btn` and whose `/N` is a stream, so the one entry is written under `/Off` and `/AS` goes with it.

  **-action reset** is the one form action this package writes (`/ResetForm`, 12.7.6.3). It is the one that is purely declarative — it puts `/V` back to `/DV` and removes `/V` where there is no `/DV` — and needs neither a script nor a server. **-fields** names the fields it resets and leaves the rest alone; without **-action** it is refused, there being no action for the list to belong to.

  **Under a PDF/A claim the reset action is refused, and at the write.** The profile admits no action on a widget annotation at all — veraPDF 6.3.3-3, "A Widget annotation dictionary shall not contain the A or AA keys" — and names `ResetForm` among the actions it forbids outright (6.5.1-1). The check falls at the write because a document declares its profile and its fields in either order, and it falls in both orders. An archived document is a record, and a record does not reset itself: the way out is a copy written without the button, which `examples/09.02-form-application.tcl` shows by asking for both on purpose, catching the refusal and printing it. A refused write leaves no file behind, so the second attempt may use the same name.

*doc* **field listbox** *name* **-rect** *{x y w h}* **-options** *list* ?**-page** *n*? ?**-value** *v*? ?**-default** *v*? ?**-index** *{i …}*? ?**-multi** *bool*? ?**-top** *n*? ?**-highlight** *c*? ?**-sort** *bool*? ?**-commit** *bool*? ?**-align** *left|center|right*? ?**-readonly** *bool*? ?**-required** *bool*? ?**-noexport** *bool*? ?*font, frame and description options*?

*doc* **field combo** *name* **-rect** *{x y w h}* **-options** *list* ?**-editable** *bool*? ?**-spellcheck** *bool*? ?*and the same otherwise, without* **-multi**, **-top** *and* **-highlight**?

: The two shapes of a choice field (`/FT /Ch`, 12.7.5.4): a list box shows its options in rows, a combo box shows one line and opens a list. They are one dictionary with one flag bit between them — Table 233, bit 18 — which is why everything below is shared but for five options: **-multi**, **-top** and **-highlight** belong to the list box and **-editable** and **-spellcheck** to the combo box, and each is refused on the other, an option that exists and does nothing being worse than one that is refused. The subcommand is not called `choice`, although that is the family name in the standard, because it would name the family and deliver one of its two members; `list` was taken by the core.

  **-options is the `/Opt` array of Table 234, written in Tcl.** An entry of one word is an option whose export value is the text it shows; an entry of two is *{export display}*. So a display text that is itself two words has to say so the way every nested list does, with a brace pair of its own: `-options {{{very good}} good poor}` offers three options, the first of them shown as "very good", while `-options {{1 {very good}} {2 good}}` offers two with export values in front. Counting the words is the only rule that lets both forms stand in one list, and it is the rule the standard's own array uses. At least one option is required — a choice field without `/Opt` is one where "no choices should be presented to the user" — and a field the user types free text into is **field text**.

  **`/V` holds the DISPLAYED text, not the export value.** 12.7.5.4: `V` is "a text string representing the selected item, as given in the field dictionary's Opt array … (For items represented in the Opt array by a two-element array, the name string is the second of the two array elements.)" The export value is the *first* element and appears nowhere else in the field dictionary. **-value** and **-default** name an option by whichever of the two the caller has — the export value is tried first, being the option's identity — and what lands in `/V` is the display text either way. A word that answers for two options is refused, because the field dictionary could not say which of them was meant either.

  **-index** names the selection by position instead, counting the options from zero, and is `/I`. It cannot stand with **-value**, both of them naming the selection, nor with **-sort**, which reorders the options before they are written so that a position counted in **-options** is not the position in the file. `/I` itself is written **only where it says something `/V` cannot** — a multiple selection, or two options that show the same text, where `/V` names both and neither — and left out where it would repeat what `/V` already says.

  **-sort really sorts.** Table 233 addresses bit 20 to the writer in so many words: "This flag is intended for use by PDF writers, not by PDF readers. PDF readers shall display the options in the order in which they occur in the Opt array." So **-sort 1** sorts the array by display text and sets the flag afterwards; the flag set over an unsorted array achieves nothing at all, in any reader.

  **-multi** is MultiSelect, a list box flag, and it is the one option that makes **-value** read its argument as a *list*: everywhere else one value is one option, whole, so "very good" is the option of that name and not the two options "very" and "good". **-top** is `/TI`, the index of the first visible option, and **-highlight** the colour of the bar behind a selected row — a light blue unless given, which is what a reader paints. **-editable** is the Edit flag and the one thing that lets **-value** name a value the options do not offer; **-spellcheck 0** is DoNotSpellCheck, which Table 233 admits only on an editable combo box, there being nothing to spell-check in a field the user cannot type in. **-commit** is CommitOnSelChange. The fonts, the frame, **-align** and the three flags of Table 227 are as **field text** takes them.

*doc* **field list** / *doc* **field names** / *doc* **field state** *name* / *doc* **field default** ?**-family** *f*? ?**-style** *s*? ?**-size** *n*? ?**-color** *c*?

: The names of the fields this document holds, in the order they were declared; what was declared for one of them; and the font a field is set in when it names none. `list` and `names` are the same word twice. **field state** answers a dictionary — `name`, `type`, `page`, `rect`, `tooltip`, `flags`, `contents`, `label` and the type's own `data` — without the object numbers and the write-time methods, which are a writer's business and nothing a caller can act on. A field with several widgets carries `widgets` in place of `rect`, `contents` and `label`: one entry per button, each with its `index`, its own `rect`, `page`, `contents`, `label` and whatever the type put in `entry`. An unknown name is refused, naming the fields there are. **All three speak about the document being written.** What stands in a *finished* form — one this package wrote, or one somebody filled in and sent back — is answered by **::tclpdf::pdf fields**, which reads the file by its path and names the type in the same words **field** takes; see "Reading a finished file".

  **field default** read with no arguments answers the current values: Helvetica at 10 points in black unless it was set, one of the fourteen standard faces, so that a document which declares a field embeds nothing it did not ask for. Set, it is both the fallback for every field that names no font and the `/DA` of the `/AcroForm` itself, which is inheritable — one call rather than one per field, since a form whose fields disagree about their type looks assembled from parts. The four values are checked at the call, by building the string they will produce: a family that does not resolve and a colour with no `/DA` spelling are refused there rather than inside a catalogue entry at the write.

**The document-level `/DA` and `/DR` are written only where a field needs them.** By 12.7.4.3 that is a field with variable text — a text field or a choice field — and nothing else. It is not cosmetic: the string names a font, naming a font registers it, and a form of nothing but buttons therefore used to put Helvetica into a document that had asked for no font at all — which under a PDF/A declaration is not a finding at the validator but a refusal at the write, for a font the caller never named. **Under PDF/A a field's font is embedded like any other**, so a document claiming a profile gives its fields an embedded face with **field default -family**; `examples/09.02-form-application.tcl` writes the same form twice, once in Helvetica and once, as PDF/A-3B, in an embedded face.

**Accessibility costs one call.** `$doc tagged 1` is the whole of what the caller does: every widget gets its own `Form` structure element, made at the call that declares the field and therefore standing where the field stands in reading order, with an object reference (`/OBJR`) to the annotation inside it and `/StructParent` on the annotation pointing back through the parent tree (14.7.5.4). It is one element per **widget** and not per field — ISO 14289-2, 8.10.1: "A Form structure element shall enclose at most one widget annotation … a Form structure element does not semantically equate to a form field" — so a radio set of four buttons is four elements and one field, which is also what veraPDF checks by name. `/Tabs /S` stands on every page that carries annotations. A field declared **before** `tagged 1` gets none of it, and `ua` reports it by name; there is no other way for a widget to end up outside the tree.

**-tooltip is not a tooltip under a UA claim; it is the name of the field for someone who cannot see it.** It is written as `/TU`, which ISO 32000-2, 14.9.3 makes the alternative name that "shall be used in place of the actual field name", and PDF/UA requires it: `ua` refuses a document whose fields have none and names them, rather than filling in the field name — a form whose fields announce themselves as "iban" and "plz2" is the reason the entry exists at all. **-contents** describes ONE widget and is written as `/Contents` on the annotation; PDF/UA-2 asks for a label or a description on every widget (8.10.2.3) and says why the field's `/TU` does not stand in once there are several (8.10.2.4), which is why the buttons of a radio set take theirs from **-buttons**. Under PDF/UA-1 the field's `/TU` is enough, measured rather than assumed: veraPDF 1.30 passes a UA-1 form whose widgets carry neither.

**-label** *script* draws the widget's label into the widget's own `Form` element, as the `Lbl` element that ISO 14289-2, 8.10.2.2 puts there — "which shall in turn be a direct descendent of a Form structure element that also includes the object reference to the widget annotation". The script runs in the scope of the `field` call, so the caller's own variables are in reach, and it runs **before** the object reference is written, the order of `/K` being the reading order and a label read out after the control it names arriving too late. **It needs a PDF 2.0 file** and is refused below one: ISO 32000-1, Table 340 gave a `Form` "only one child: an object reference identifying the widget annotation", and veraPDF still holds a 1.7 file to it (rule 7.18.4-2). The Role attribute of the PrintField class is not the way round that — 14.8.5.6 is explicit that those attributes are for *non-interactive* forms, so writing one to quiet a validator would be a false statement about the document. A PDF/UA-1 document describes its widgets with **-contents** instead, and a label for a whole radio group is a `structure Lbl` beside the field either way.

**What is deliberately not built**, named rather than half written: XFA (Table 224, deprecated in PDF 2.0, and forbidden outright by ISO 14289-2, 8.10.1) and with it `/DS`, `/RV` and the RichText flag, all three defined in the XFA format rather than in the PDF standard; ECMAScript actions, whose contents 12.6.4.17 defines in ISO/DIS 21757-1, another standard entirely — and therefore calculated fields, whose `/C` action is an ECMAScript action by Table 199; the submit-form and import-data actions (12.7.6.2, 12.7.6.4), which need a server and a reader's file dialogue; the icon entries of Table 192 (`/I`, `/RI`, `/IX`, `/IF`, `/TP`) and the rollover and down captions `/RC` and `/AC`; and `/NeedAppearances`. A value that has to be computed is computed in the script and written as `/V` with its `/AP` drawn — which is what a document produced by a script can do, and what a script inside the document cannot be measured doing here.

**A further field type is a module of its own.** The six shipped types attach to the core through a contract written out at the head of `field.tcl` in the source distribution: register a subcommand, declare the field, answer at write time the dictionary pairs that are the type's own — the list is exhaustive and a key outside it is refused by name — and draw the appearance into a canvas the size of the widget, where `{0 0}` is its top left corner and every drawing method works as it does on a page. `/AcroForm`, `/Fields`, `/SigFlags`, `/DA`, `/DR`, the page's `/Annots`, every object number, the version floor and the whole structure tree stay the core's, which is why a field type gets accessibility by declaring its field and nothing else. `examples/09.01-form-fields.tcl` shows the text field on its own and `examples/09.02-form-application.tcl` a whole form of all six types.

## Metadata

*doc* **info** *key* ?*value*?

: Reads or sets an entry of the information dictionary: the text keys `Title`, `Author`, `Subject`, `Keywords`, `Creator`, `Producer`; the two dates `CreationDate` and `ModDate`, which take a PDF date such as `D:20260818120000+02'00'` (ISO 32000-1, 7.9.4) and are refused otherwise — **a date without a time zone keeps the day and loses the time** in the XMP packet, because 7.9.4 makes the offset optional while XMP (Part 1, 8.2.1.4) has no form for a time without one; writing `Z` would claim UTC and the local offset would claim the machine that made the run, and both are inventions; and `Trapped`, a name — `True`, `False` or `Unknown` (PDF 1.3). Each of the text keys goes into the XMP packet as well, under the property ISO 32000-1 Table 317 pairs it with: `Title` as `dc:title`, `Author` as `dc:creator`, `Subject` as `dc:description`, `Keywords` as `pdf:Keywords`, `Creator` — the application that made the document — as `xmp:CreatorTool`, and `Producer` — the writer, `tclpdf` unless replaced — as `pdf:Producer`. Without a `Creator` the creating tool in the packet is the producer. `CreationDate` and `ModDate` are mirrored into the packet as `xmp:CreateDate` and `xmp:ModifyDate`; `Trapped` is not — the predefined PDF schema of ISO 19005 has no `pdf:Trapped`, and veraPDF fails a PDF/A file that carries it.

  **A key this list does not name is taken and written all the same, and that is deliberate** — it is the one option list in this package that is not closed. ISO 32000-1, 14.3.3 lets a producer put keys of its own into the information dictionary, real files are full of them (`/Company`, `/SourceModified`), and `::tclpdf::pdf info` hands them back, so refusing to *write* what this package is willing to *read* would be a hole rather than a guard. Such a key goes into the dictionary as a text string under exactly the name it was given, is checked no further, and reaches **no** XMP property: only the eight keys above have one that ISO 32000-1, Table 317 pairs them with (`Trapped`, the ninth, deliberately has none). That is not a PDF/A problem — ISO 19005 asks the two to agree for the properties that *are* paired, and a key with no counterpart pairs with nothing; measured, a PDF/A-3B document carrying `/Company` passes veraPDF with 0 failed checks. Use **xmpSchema** to put a property of your own into the packet where the packet is where it belongs. The price of the openness is the usual one: a mistyped `Titel` is written as `/Titel` and nothing says so.

*doc* **language** ?*tag*?

: The natural language of the document as an RFC 3066 tag — `de`, `de-DE`, `en-GB`. It goes into the catalog and is what lets a screen reader pronounce the text correctly. PDF/A-3a and PDF/UA require it. (PDF 1.4.)

*doc* **metadata** ?*xml*?

: Reads or sets the XMP packet directly. Normally the package writes it: set by the caller it is kept as given — as text, answered back unchanged, and encoded to UTF-8 once when the file is written, so `metadata [$doc metadata]` is a no-op; otherwise the packet is rebuilt on every write from the document's current title, language and declarations, so it never lags behind the Info dictionary. (PDF 1.4.)

*doc* **xmpSchema** *prefix uri tags method*

: Registers a schema that writes an `rdf:Description` of its own into the XMP packet each time the packet is built — the mechanism by which PDF/A and PDF/UA put their identification there, open to an extension with properties of its own to declare. *prefix* and *uri* name the schema and *tags* lists every property name it may write; a property answered but not listed fails the write. *method* names a method of the document object — an extension adds one with `oo::define`, see `doc/PLUGINS.md` in the source distribution — that is called when the packet is built and answers the properties as a list of *{kind tag value}* entries: kind `text` writes the value as the element's text, kind `bag` takes a list of tag-value pair lists and writes an `rdf:Bag` whose items carry them as resources. A method rather than the values themselves, because the values may change after registration — `pdfa -part 3` can follow — and a callback reads the state as it is at write time, so a second **write** stays truthful. An empty answer omits the description. Registration order is packet order, and registering a *prefix* again replaces its entry rather than adding a second one. A tag in *tags* that is not a valid XML name is refused at the call, naming the tag and the schema. Returns nothing.

  Both commands live in the `tclpdf::xmp` module, which every conformance claim loads; without one, `package require tclpdf::xmp` first — it needs tdom, as the packet does.

*doc* **xmpRaw** *xml*

: Adds ready-made XML to the packet, for what does not fit the tag-and-value form of **xmpSchema** — parsed at the call, so that XML which is not well formed is refused there rather than at every later **write**, leaving the document unwritable with no way to take the contribution back: whole `rdf:Description` elements, appended under the packet's `rdf:RDF` as given — a PDF/A extension schema is a page of RDF no accessor could usefully model, and `pdfa extension` rides on this call. The `rdf` prefix may be used without declaring it, since the element around it always has; every other namespace the fragment must declare itself. The XML is parsed when the packet is built, and a malformed contribution fails the **write** naming itself rather than travelling on to a validator. The packet is built once a schema is registered or a raw contribution is added — every conformance claim registers a schema, and a raw contribution alone builds the packet just the same, since a document needs no conformance claim to carry metadata. Returns nothing.

*doc* **catalogEntry** *key* ?*value*?

: An entry of the document catalog, for anything the package does not offer by name.

## Structure and accessibility

*doc* **tagged** ?*0*|*1*?

: Whether the document writes a structure tree. **Off by default**, and it has to be set before anything is drawn — once a page has content, `tagged 1` is refused: the brackets go into the content stream as it is written. (PDF 1.4.)

  A tagged document carries a second, invisible layer saying what the marks on a page *are* — a heading, a paragraph, a table cell — rather than how they look. The drawing does not change. Reading software needs it: without a tree it follows the order the content stream happens to have, which on a two column page runs across both columns. PDF/UA and PDF/A level A require it.

*doc* **structure** *type* ?**-name** *name*? ?**-id** *string*? ?**-alt** *text*? ?**-lang** *tag*? ?**-title** *text*? ?**-actualText** *text*? ?**-expansion** *text*? ?**-scope** *side*? ?**-numbering** *style*? ?**-bbox** {*x y w h*}? ?**-colSpan** *n*? ?**-rowSpan** *n*? **-script** *body*

: Opens a structure element, runs *body* with it open and closes it again — including when the body fails, so a half open tree cannot reach the file. Returns whatever the body returned. *type* is one of the standard types of ISO 32000-1 14.8.4; an unknown one is refused at the call rather than in a validator later.

  Elements nest by nesting the calls. Grouping types — `Sect`, `Div`, `L`, `LI`, `Table`, `TR` and their kin — do not hold content themselves: text drawn inside an open `Sect` becomes a `P` **within** it, which is what the nesting rules ask for.

  **Most documents need few of these.** A table knows it is a table and a paragraph knows it is a paragraph, so those tag themselves; `structure` is for the grouping a writer cannot infer.

  **An empty `-tag` is refused where nothing would come of it.** Inside an open leaf it means the same as no tag at all and the content is marked on that leaf; where no leaf is open there is nothing to mark it with, and content that reaches the page unmarked is invisible to a screen reader while every validator passes the file. Every other unusable value — an unknown type, a type that may not stand there — is refused by name, and the empty string is no different. **An artifact kind behind an ordinary tag** (`-tag {P Pagination}`) is refused too: only `Artifact` and `auto` read a second word, and it used to be dropped in silence.

: **What is derived, and what has to be said.** `text` becomes a `P`, and one call is one element however many lines it breaks into. `table` becomes a `Table` with `TR`, `TH` and `TD`, and the fill and rules of its cells become artifacts. `image` becomes an artifact unless `-alt` describes it, and then a `Figure` carrying that description — or unless it is placed inside an open `Figure` (**structure Figure -alt … -script**), whose content it then is: the Figure's `-alt` covers it, and neither `-alt` nor `-artifact` is needed. Inside a form or a pattern `-alt` on an image, drawing or form placement opens no element; the invocation's `-alt` describes it.

  **-alt** appears on `image place`, `image draw`, `form place` and `svg` for the same reason and with the same effect: with it the drawing becomes a `Figure` carrying that description, without it an artifact. **-artifact 1** on the same four calls says that the artifact is intended — this one is decoration, and no description is missing. The two contradict each other and are refused together. The difference matters because an artifact is the one way real content passes a reader entirely, and PDF/UA allows it only for decoration: a graphic that became an artifact with neither option was never judged either way, and the document keeps a note of it — an artifact by default is what most pictures are, an artifact by intent is what a validator cannot ask for and a caller can say. In an untagged document neither option changes anything. A form or a drawing is one piece of marked content however many operators it contains — bracketing per element would scatter one illustration over dozens of leaves, and the parts of a drawing mean nothing on their own. Nothing inside a form, a pattern or a page-number XObject is marked at all: those are content streams of their own, mark numbers are unique per stream, and it is the *invocation* that carries the marking.

  A hyphen the line breaker inserted at a soft-hyphen offer is bracketed as a break rather than left to look like part of the word: it sits in a `Span` with an empty `ActualText`, so extracted text gives the word back whole (14.8.2.6). A hyphen the text brought with it is untouched — `E-Mail` stays `E-Mail`. Only in a tagged document.

  What no writer can infer is whether a line of text is a heading — neither its size nor its weight says so. That is what **-tag** on `text` is for: `-tag H1`, `-tag Caption`, and so on. Inside an open leaf element `-tag` names a child: `-tag Span` inside a `structure P` is a Span in that P, `-tag Caption` inside a `Figure` its caption; a type the open element may not hold (`-tag H1` inside a `P`) is refused naming both. `-tag P`, the default, marks the text as the open element's own. A grouping type (`Table`, `L`, `Sect`, …) is refused on `-tag`: open it with `structure`. `-tag Artifact` takes the text out of the tree altogether, which is what a running head or a page number needs; under PDF/UA anything left unmarked counts as a defect.

  The numbered headings are one of two forms. `H1` to `Hn` state the level themselves — a *strongly* structured document, and the form to write when the levels are known. The generic `H` leaves the level to the nesting: one `Sect` inside another, each opening with an `H`, and the depth of the element is the level — a *weakly* structured document (ISO 32000-1 14.8.4.4.2), which is what a converter honestly writes when its source nests sections without numbering them. A tree uses one form or the other, never both — mixed, the nesting and the numbers can disagree and no reader can choose. Under a `ua` claim the mixture is refused when the document is written (Matterhorn 14-006; measured, veraPDF fails it against ua1 under clause 7.4.4 and passes either form alone), and part 2 refuses the generic `H` altogether — ISO 32000-2 knows only the numbered headings. Example `05.14` writes a weakly structured document.

  **-expansion** *text* is the expanded form of an abbreviation (ISO 32000 14.9.5) — PDF/UA asks that abbreviations be expanded (7.20), and a reader that is asked reads the expansion out. On `structure` it goes onto the element as `/E`; on `text` — `text "EU" -expansion "European Union"` — the word becomes a `Span` carrying it, inside the paragraph that is open, or inside the one the call would have made. It needs a tagged document and is refused without one, and it cannot go on an artifact.

: **Attributes.** Five options write standard attributes onto the element, each one only where the standard allows it — used elsewhere they would be written and then ignored, so they are refused at the call instead.

  **-scope** takes `Row`, `Column` or `Both` and belongs on a `TH`; it says which way a header cell heads. A table sets it by itself: its head row heads columns. **-bbox** takes four numbers and says so if it does not. **-numbering** belongs on an `L` and takes `None`, `Disc`, `Circle`, `Square`, `Decimal`, `UpperRoman`, `LowerRoman`, `UpperAlpha`, `LowerAlpha` (ISO 32000-1 Table 347); in a 2.0 file also `Unordered`, `Description` and `Ordered` (ISO 32000-2 Table 369), which a 1.7 file refuses; PDF/UA makes it mandatory for an ordered list, and no writer can derive it — the label is drawn text, and `1.` and `-` look the same from here. **-bbox** belongs on a `Figure`, `Formula` or `Table` and takes the same four numbers as a rectangle; it is not required by the letter of the standard, but the reading tools rely on it. A picture, a drawing and a form placement described with **-alt** get one by themselves: `image place` and `form place` name the rectangle the placement covers, **-scale** included, and `svg` names the rectangle the drawing was actually fitted into rather than the one asked for — a 2:1 drawing in a square box is drawn 2:1 with empty bands beside it, and the bands are not the figure, which is why it is the same four numbers `svg` answers with. Under **-rotate** the box is the upright one around the turned placement, wider and higher than the placement itself: ISO 32000-2, 14.8.5.4.3 asks for the rectangle that completely encloses the visible content, and the untouched rectangle would leave part of the figure outside it while covering page that is not the figure. **-colSpan** and **-rowSpan** belong on a cell and are written by the table itself where it spans.

: **Types beyond 1.7.** ISO 32000-2 adds `Title`, `Aside`, `DocumentFragment`, `Sub`, `FENote`, `Em`, `Strong` and the headings `H7` to `H10`. They are accepted only in a 2.0 file — in a 1.7 one they would validate as non-standard types with no role map — and `ua -part 2` is the ordinary way to get one. Each such construct pins the version: after a 2.0-only type, a 2.0 attribute value such as `-numbering Ordered`, or a structure destination from `link -structure` or `bookmark -structure`, `configure -version 1.7` is refused naming what the document already uses. As soon as one of them is used, the tree names the 2.0 structure namespace (`/NS` on every element that lives in it, and `/Namespaces` on the root) whether or not PDF/UA-2 is claimed; the twelve 1.7-only types keep the default. Twelve older types (`Art`, `BlockQuote`, `TOC`, `TOCI`, `Index`, `Private`, `Quote`, `Note`, `Reference`, `BibEntry`, `Code` and the generic `H` — the 2.0 namespace knows only the numbered headings) exist *only* in the 1.7 namespace and keep it even inside a 2.0 tree. Inline types — `Span`, `Quote`, `BibEntry`, `Em`, `Strong`, `Sub`, `Ruby`, `Warichu` — need an element that holds text around them (a `P`, a heading, a cell, a `Figure`); at the top level or straight in a `Sect` they are refused (ISO 32005 Table 5). `Reference` is refused at the top level only. **-lang** takes a language tag as `language` does (RFC 3066) and refuses anything else. **-script** may be an empty body — an element with nothing in it is a legitimate placeholder — but a missing `-script` is refused.

  A leaf type such as `P` or `H1` holds text and **inline** markup — `Span`, `Em`, `Strong`, `Link`, `Figure` and their kin — but no block element: a `P` inside a `P` is the standing example of what Annex L forbids. `Ruby` and `Warichu` hold a fixed sequence, not a set: exactly `RB RT` or `RB RP RT RP`, and `WP WT WP` (ISO 32000-2, Table 369) — a part outside its parent is refused where it opens, a wrong or incomplete sequence when the bracket closes. A `Table` holds at most one `THead` and one `TFoot` (Annex L); the second is refused where it is opened.

: **Naming an element.** **-name** gives the element a name that a link or a bookmark points at with **-structure**. A structure destination names the *element* rather than a place on a page (12.3.2.3), so it still lands on the right thing after the content above it has grown — PDF/UA-2 asks for internal targets to be written that way. It is written as a GoTo action carrying both the structure destination (/SD) and a page destination (/D) to the element's first page — /XYZ at the top of its first content, or /Fit when no position is known — so a reader that does not understand structure destinations still lands on the right page. The name has to be unique and may be used before it is declared, which a link pointing forward at a later section needs. An unknown one is reported when the document is written, naming it.

  **-id** is something else: the element identifier of ISO 32000-1 14.7.2, a string written as `/ID` on the element and entered in the `IDTree` of the structure tree root, by which a reader — not this package — looks the element up. It has to be unique in the document. A `Note` gets one on its own when none is given (`Note` followed by its position in the tree), because PDF/UA-1 asks every note for one (7.9); every other element has one only when asked.

: **Artifacts name their kind.** What is not in the tree is bracketed as an artifact, and the bracket says which sort it is: `Pagination` for a page number, `Layout` for everything else this package produces. PDF/UA-2 requires the naming; earlier versions permit it, so it is written either way and a document does not have to be redrawn when it is upgraded.

  **-tag** takes the kind as well, as a list: `-tag {Artifact Pagination Header}` marks a running head, `{Artifact Pagination Footer}` a running foot, which is what PDF/UA asks for (7.8). The kinds are the four of ISO 32000-1 Table 330 — `Pagination`, `Layout`, `Page` and `Background` — and only `Pagination` takes a subtype: `Header`, `Footer` or `Watermark` (Table 331). Anything else is refused at the call. `pageNumbers` works it out by itself — above the middle of the page it is a head, below it a foot — because that is the one place that knows.

## PDF/UA

*doc* **ua** ?*0*|*1*? | *doc* **ua** ?**-part** *1*|*2*? ?**-revision** *year*? ?**-wtpdf** *levels*?

: Declares PDF/UA conformance — the promise that the document can be used by someone who cannot see the page. The boolean form means part 1 (ISO 14289-1) and is what a letter, an invoice or a briefing needs; part 2 (ISO 14289-2) is a PDF 2.0 format and is asked for by name. Part 1 lifts the file to PDF 1.7 and part 2 to 2.0, through `configure`, and the version cannot be lowered again afterwards. Part 1 does not cap the version — a document may move on to part 2 — but a part-1 claim on a file that was raised to 2.0 is refused when the file is written (ISO 14289-1, 6.1: the header is `%PDF-1.n`), naming part 2 as the way out. `ua 0` withdraws the claim and everything it contributed — the schema, `DisplayDocTitle` where `ua` set it, the namespace demand; the file version stays raised.

  **Off by default and explicit**, as `pdfa` is. The claim is legally meaningful in public procurement, so nothing should acquire it as a side effect — and a document using the standard 14 faces cannot make it at all.

: **What it insists on, checked when the file is written.** A title (`info Title`), a language (`language`), every font embedded — a `Figure` or a `Formula` described with **-alt** or **-actualText**, wherever it was opened and whether the caller opened it or `text -tag` derived it, since an empty **-alt** is not a description and veraPDF says so — the standard 14 included, which is stricter than PDF/A and rules out Symbol and ZapfDingbats entirely, as neither has an embeddable representative. Headings starting at `H1` with no level skipped, and one kind of heading throughout: a tree that mixes the generic `H` with numbered ones is neither weakly nor strongly structured and is refused (ISO 14289-1, 7.4.4; Matterhorn 14-006). Tables whose rows all cover the same number of columns, spans counted — a `colSpan` covers as many columns as it says and a `rowSpan` covers its column in the rows below (Matterhorn 15-003, UA-2 8.2.5.26); a table whose rows still differ is refused. A description on every link, which is what `link -tooltip` writes, and every link annotation inside a `Link` element (part 2: `Link` or `Reference`). A description on every form field, which is what `field -tooltip` writes, and every widget annotation inside a `Form` element — a field declared before `tagged 1` misses that and is named; part 2 adds a label or a description on every widget besides (`-label`, `-contents`, and for a radio set the third word of `-buttons`). Every picture, drawing and form placement either described with **-alt** or declared decoration with **-artifact 1** — one that became an artifact with neither was never judged, and an artifact may carry nothing a reader needs (7.1). Lists whose numbering and labels agree (7.6): an `L` with **-numbering** other than `None` needs a `Lbl` in every `LI`, and items carrying a `Lbl` need the `L` to say what they are — `Decimal`, `Disc`, … or `None`. `DisplayDocTitle` still on: **ua** sets it, and a later `viewerPreferences -displayDocTitle 0` is refused rather than written. Part 2 adds a `Desc` on every attachment and forbids the generic `H` and `Note` (use `FENote`).

  All of them are reported at once rather than one per run, and each message names the call to change. Writing fails; no file is left behind.

: **What it contributes by itself**: the `pdfuaid` schema in the XMP, `ViewerPreferences` with `DisplayDocTitle`, and for part 2 the 2.0 structure namespace and the file version. None of them moves a mark on a page. In a document that also declares PDF/A, the PDF/A extension schema describing `pdfuaid` is written as well — without it PDF/A refuses a schema it does not know.

  **-revision** is the year of the edition claimed, four digits, and goes into the metadata as `pdfuaid:rev` for part 2 (ISO 14289-2 Table 1); the default is 2024, the year part 2 was published. Anything that is not a four-digit year is refused at the call.

  **-wtpdf** adds a Well-Tagged PDF declaration and takes `reuse`, `accessibility` or both. It goes with part 2 only. The identifier is written in two spellings: WTPDF 1.0 gives the URI with a slash before the fragment, veraPDF 1.30 tests for the form without one, and a document that has to satisfy both carries both — which the declaration mechanism expressly allows.

: **PDF/UA-2 and PDF/A-3 cannot be combined.** Part 2 needs PDF 2.0, PDF/A-3 is a 1.7 format, and so an accessible ZUGFeRD invoice is `ua 1` together with `pdfa -part 3`. Declaring both is refused at the call that creates the contradiction.

*doc* **ua state**

: What has been declared, as a dictionary: `part`, `revision`, `wtpdf`, `registered`. Empty before **ua** was called. `ua state` answers `part`, `revision`, `wtpdf` and `registered`; none of them is an option of `ua` — `ua -registered 1` is refused as unknown.

## Viewer preferences

*doc* **viewerPreferences** ?**-key** *value* ...?

: How a reader should present the document (ISO 32000 12.2). Without arguments it answers with what has been set so far. (PDF 1.2; `-direction` 1.3, `-displayDocTitle` 1.4, the value `UseOC` 1.5, `-printScaling` 1.6, `-duplex`, `-pickTrayByPDFSize` and `-numCopies` 1.7.)

  Calls accumulate: each one sets the keys it names and leaves the rest alone, so a document can state its window wishes in one place and its printing wishes in another.

  Booleans: **-hideToolbar**, **-hideMenubar**, **-hideWindowUI**, **-fitWindow**, **-centerWindow**, **-displayDocTitle**, **-pickTrayByPDFSize**. Names: **-nonFullScreenPageMode** (`UseNone`, `UseOutlines`, `UseThumbs`, `UseOC`), **-direction** (`L2R`, `R2L`), **-printScaling** (`None`, `AppDefault`), **-duplex** (`Simplex`, `DuplexFlipShortEdge`, `DuplexFlipLongEdge`). And **-numCopies**, an integer from 2 to 5 — the values ISO 32000-1 Table 150 says a reader supports; anything else is refused rather than written to be ignored.

  A misspelled value is refused at the call. It has to be: a reader that meets one falls back to its default silently and nothing anywhere reports a problem — the document simply prints on one side for the rest of its life.

  `ViewArea`, `ViewClip`, `PrintArea` and `PrintClip` are deliberately absent, and so is `PrintPageRange`. The four are deprecated in PDF 2.0 and no reader tested here acts on them.

## Page labels

*doc* **initialView** ?**-pageMode** *name*? ?**-pageLayout** *name*? ?**-page** *n*? ?**-to** *{x y}*? ?**-zoom** *z*?

: How a reader **opens** the document: which panel it shows beside the page (`/PageMode`), how it arranges the pages (`/PageLayout`), and where it puts the reader first (`/OpenAction`) — the catalogue entries of ISO 32000-2, Table 28. Without arguments it answers what has been set so far, and calls accumulate, as **viewerPreferences** does. **-pageMode** takes `UseNone`, `UseOutlines`, `UseThumbs`, `FullScreen`, `UseOC` (PDF 1.5) or `UseAttachments` (1.6); **-pageLayout** takes `SinglePage`, `OneColumn`, `TwoColumnLeft`, `TwoColumnRight`, `TwoPageLeft` or `TwoPageRight` (the last two 1.5). A value outside those lists is refused rather than written: a misspelt name is not an error a validator reports either — the reader falls back on its default and the document opens the ordinary way for the rest of its life. A caller's **-pageMode** wins over the `/UseOutlines` a **bookmark** sets by itself.

  **A command of its own, not three more keys of viewerPreferences.** A viewer preference is a wish a reader may ignore; these are what it *does* when the file opens. **viewerPreferences** answers with the dictionary that goes into `/ViewerPreferences` — the PDF/UA check reads that answer to find `DisplayDocTitle` in it — and three catalogue keys mixed into it would make one return value mean two things; besides, that dictionary already has a **-nonFullScreenPageMode** whose value list overlaps `/PageMode`'s in four of six words.

  **-page**, **-to** and **-zoom** are the three words **destination** and **link** use for the same three choices, and they go through the same code, so an open action and a bookmark pointing at the same place cannot disagree. **-page** counts from 0, as **page current** counts, and may name a page not added yet — the target is filled in when the document is written, as a forward link's is. Without **-to** the destination is `/Fit`, the whole page. **-to** and **-zoom** without **-page** are refused: they describe a place *on* a page and none was given. A zoom of 0 is a number a reader acts on (12.3.2.2: it means the magnification the reader is already at), below it there is none to mean. **An action is not offered.** Table 28 lets `/OpenAction` be a destination or an action dictionary, and the second is left to **catalogEntry OpenAction**: ISO 19005 forbids most action types outright, so an **-action** option would be a promise half of whose values break the moment **pdfa** is declared. Measured with veraPDF: a `/Launch` and a `/JavaScript` open action each fail clause 6.5.1 test 1, while a destination in `/OpenAction` — and `/PageMode /FullScreen` with it — passes PDF/A-3B.


*doc* **pageLabels** / *doc* **pageLabels -from** *index* ?**-style** *s*? ?**-prefix** *text*? ?**-start** *n*?

: What a reader calls each page (ISO 32000 12.4.2). A page has two numbers — the index the file counts it by and the number printed on it — and a reader shows the first unless the file says otherwise; then "go to page 3" lands on the third sheet while the sheet reading "3" is the sixth. One call is one range: it begins at the page **-from**, an index counted from 0 like everywhere else, and runs until the next range begins. Several calls make several ranges, in whatever order they come; a second call for the same index replaces the first. Without arguments it answers the ranges set so far, as a dictionary from index to `style`, `prefix` and `start`, in page order. (PDF 1.3.)

  **-style** takes `D` (decimal, the default), `R` and `r` (roman), `A` and `a` (letters), or `none` for a range that carries a prefix and no number — a cover called "Cover". **-prefix** is put before the number, **-start** is the number the range begins with, a positive integer, 1 unless said otherwise. A misspelled style is refused at the call, because a reader that meets one ignores it silently.

  The tree has to begin at index 0. A document that labels only its body from page 4 on has said nothing about the pages before, so a range without a style is put in front — the standard's own way of saying "no number here" — rather than writing a tree a reader may refuse.

  Labels and printed numbers have to agree: Matterhorn 15-001 counts a visible page number that differs from the page label as an accessibility failure, and it is one no validator can see, because the printed number is drawn text. Nothing here is automatic — a document that prints its numbers with `pageNumbers -from 3` should say `pageLabels -from 0 -start 3` as well, so that the reader's page field shows what the sheet shows.

## PDF/A and ZUGFeRD

*doc* **pdfa** ?**-part** *n*? ?**-conformance** *level*? ?**-profile** *path*? ?**-identifier** *text*?

: Declares PDF/A conformance, writes the output intent with the given ICC profile and raises the file version to match — through `configure`, so `cget -version` answers 1.7 afterwards, and the version can be neither lowered nor raised to 2.0 again — PDF/A-2 and -3 are PDF 1.7 formats (ISO 19005-2/-3, 6.1.2: the header is `%PDF-1.n`), so `configure -version 2.0` after `pdfa` is refused naming the claim, as `pdfa` after `-version 2.0` is. Parts 2 and 3 are accepted; part 1 is refused because it forbids the transparency this package writes, and part 4 because it needs PDF 2.0. Part 2 admits no embedded file that is not itself PDF/A (ISO 19005-2, 6.8): `pdfa -part 2` on a document with attachments is refused, and so is `attach` under a part-2 claim — use part 3.

  Without **-profile** the sRGB profile shipped with the package is used, so every PDF/A file carries an output intent — this package paints in DeviceRGB, and ISO 19005 requires the intent for that. The output condition identifier is read from the profile's own `desc` tag, falls back to the file name, and **-identifier** overrides it; the shipped profile is named `sRGB IEC61966-2.1`, the colour space it implements, as it always was. A profile that does not exist is refused at the call. So is a file that is no ICC profile (no `acsp` signature) or one describing a space other than grey, RGB or CMYK. The path is normalized on the way in; `pdfa state` answers the normalized path. A second profile ships beside it for print work in process colours: `icc/ISOcoated_v2_bas.ICC` (basICColor's ISO Coated v2, FOGRA39, zlib licence — the file `LICENSE-ZLIB-bICC` next to it is the grant), a CMYK output intent that a document painting in `{cmyk …}` names with **-profile** — and `icc/ISOcoated_v2_grey1c_bas.ICC`, its grey component, for a document that paints in grey alone (example `05.10`); and `icc/sRGB2014.icc`, the ICC's own sRGB profile of 2015, beside the default so that the two can be compared — examples `05.11` and `05.12` write the same page under each and print the profile facts; same primaries and tone curve, D50 white point with a `chad` tag against the older D65 without one, and in the file both compress to much the same size; the intent may describe grey, RGB or CMYK, and the package's own objects — the transparency groups of forms among them — name no colour space that could contradict it. Example `05.09` writes such a file.

  **-conformance** takes `B` (the default), `U` or `A`. Level B promises the document looks the same in fifteen years; level U adds that its text can be extracted and searched reliably, which rests on the ToUnicode map written for every embedded face anyway — so `U` is the stronger claim at no cost and is worth asking for. Level A adds the structure tree, so it needs `tagged 1` before anything is drawn; asked for without it, `pdfa` names the missing call rather than writing a file that claims 3a and fails validation. Level A also asks the question **ua** asks: a picture, a drawing or a form placed with neither **-alt** nor **-artifact 1** would go out as an artifact — content a screen reader never sees, in a file veraPDF declares conformant — so the write is refused naming each graphic's page and both ways out (ISO 19005-2, 6.7.3). Levels B and U promise appearance and extractable text, not reachable content, and stay silent. **Both orders are checked**, as they are for fonts, colours and graphics: `tagged 0` after the claim is refused at the write, since a document declares its conformance and its content in either order and only the write sees both.

  Declaring conformance also turns on a check: every font in the document must be embedded, and writing fails with a message naming the offending face rather than producing a file that a validator rejects later. The same is true of colour: ISO 19005-2, 6.2.4.3 admits DeviceGray under any output intent, DeviceRGB only under an RGB one and DeviceCMYK only under a CMYK one, and the rule reaches every road a colour takes — fills and strokes, text, a picture's colour space (an Indexed PNG counts as RGB, a CMYK JPEG as CMYK), a shading, the content of a tiling pattern or a form, and the alternate of a separation (6.2.4.4). The package keeps a record of which spaces the document uses and where, whether or not `pdfa` was declared, and holds it against the profile when the file is written: a `{cmyk …}` fill under the shipped sRGB profile, or a `steelblue` under `icc/ISOcoated_v2_bas.ICC`, is refused with the calls and pages that used it — `used by rect on page 1, text on page 2` — and the two ways out named: paint in the intent's space or in grey, or give a profile of the space you paint in. Every offending space is listed at once, and the check does not depend on whether `pdfa` came before or after the drawing. Measured against veraPDF: what is refused here is exactly what fails there.

*doc* **pdfa state**

: What has been set, as a dictionary: `part`, `conformance`, `profile`, `identifier`, `extensions` and `registered`. Empty before **pdfa** was called. `registered` and `extensions` are state, not options — `pdfa -registered` is refused as unknown.

*doc* **pdfa extension** *xml*

: Adds an extension schema to the XMP packet — the way a profile such as ZUGFeRD announces its own properties. `zugferd` uses it. It needs a `pdfa` call before it; without one it is refused rather than declaring PDF/A-3B by itself.

*doc* **zugferd** *path* ?**-name** *n*? ?**-profile** *p*? ?**-type** *t*? ?**-icc** *path*? ?**-version** *v*? ?**-relationship** *r*? ?**-description** *d*? ?**-compress** *0*?

: The one call an electronic invoice — or an Order-X order, see below — needs. It reads the profile from the invoice XML (BT-24), declares PDF/A-3B, writes the output intent with the sRGB profile shipped with the package, adds the Factur-X XMP extension schema, and attaches the file as `factur-x.xml` at document level with the `/AFRelationship` the profile prescribes by default — `Data` for MINIMUM and BASIC WL, `Alternative` for every fuller profile; **-relationship** may name `Data`, `Source` or `Alternative` instead, while `Supplement` and `Unspecified` belong to the other attachments and are refused for the invoice as they are for the order — plus an entry in the names tree and a modification date. Returns the detected profile. A UTF-16 encoded XML is refused as such — re-encode it as UTF-8 — instead of being reported as carrying no BT-24 identifier.

  **-name** is the name a reader looks the attachment up by, and the standards allow exactly four: `factur-x.xml`, `zugferd-invoice.xml` and `xrechnung.xml` for an invoice, `order-x.xml` for an order. Without the option the file's own name is kept where it is one the document's family allows, and `factur-x.xml` — `order-x.xml` for an order — is taken otherwise; any other **-name** is refused, and so is a name of the other family: an invoice is never `order-x.xml`, an order nothing else. **-type** goes into `fx:DocumentType`: `INVOICE`, `ORDER`, `ORDER_RESPONSE` or `ORDER_CHANGE`; anything else is refused. Without the option the type follows BT-24 — `ORDER` for an Order-X identifier, `INVOICE` for every other — and a response or change has to be named. **-description** replaces the attachment description, which is "*profile* `invoice data`" — "*profile* `order data`" for an order — unless given. **-compress** Flate-compresses the embedded XML and is **off by default**, so the invoice sits in the file byte for byte as it arrived.

  **Order-X** (Order-X 1.0, 4.1.1 and 4.1.2) rides on the same call: BT-24 `urn:order-x.eu:1p0:basic`, `:comfort` or `:extended` is read as `BASIC`, `COMFORT` or `EXTENDED`, the document becomes an `ORDER` unless **-type** says `ORDER_RESPONSE` or `ORDER_CHANGE`, the XML is embedded as `order-x.xml` — the one name the standard allows — with `/AFRelationship` `Data` by default, and the extension schema is written under the Order-X namespace URI, `urn:factur-x:pdfa:CrossIndustryDocument:1p0#`, without the `:invoice` of Factur-X. **-relationship** may say `Source` or `Alternative` instead, the two other values 4.1.1 permits for `order-x.xml`; `Supplement` and `Unspecified` are for the other attachments and refused here. Levels, types and names are bound to their family — invoice: `MINIMUM`, `BASIC WL`, `BASIC`, `EN 16931`, `EXTENDED`, `XRECHNUNG` under `INVOICE`; order: `BASIC`, `COMFORT`, `EXTENDED` under the three order types — and a **-profile** or a BT-24 identifier of the other family is refused naming both, so that a Factur-X BASIC invoice cannot go out labelled an order at BASIC or the other way round.

  **PDF/A-3 is fixed at this call**, and level B is what it settles on where nothing else has been claimed — the level every invoice reaches without a structure tree, and the least the standards ask for. **A claim already made survives:** `pdfa -conformance A` before `zugferd` keeps its A rather than being taken back to B, which used to happen silently and left the structure tree paid for but not claimed. A document that wants more can also say so afterwards: `pdfa -conformance U` after `zugferd` raises the claim to 3U with the Factur-X extension in place, and `A` needs `tagged 1` besides — measured, the file then validates against the higher profile. **-icc** names another output intent profile in place of the shipped sRGB one — a CMYK profile for an invoice painted in process colours, see `pdfa -profile` — and the colours are held against it at write time like everywhere else. **-profile** overrides the level read from BT-24 (`MINIMUM`, `BASIC WL`, `BASIC`, `EN 16931`, `EXTENDED`, or `XRECHNUNG`, which is what an XRechnung identifier is read as; `BASIC`, `COMFORT` or `EXTENDED` for an order), for an XML whose identifier the reader does not recognise — any other word is refused, because it would go into the XMP as a conformance level nobody validates against; with **-profile** given BT-24 is still read for the family where it names one, so an Order-X XML stays an order, and with **-profile** and **-type** both given it is not read at all; **-version** is the `fx:Version` written to the XMP, `1.0` unless given, and takes digits and dots only. Every option is checked before the document is changed; a refused `zugferd` leaves no PDF/A claim, no schema and no attachment behind.

*doc* **zugferd profile** *xml*

: The conformance level named in BT-24 of an invoice or order XML — the XML itself as a string of bytes, not a path — read without writing anything: `EN 16931` for the EN 16931 identifier, `COMFORT` for `urn:order-x.eu:1p0:comfort`. Refuses XML that carries no such identifier or one the reader does not know.

*doc* **zugferd state**

: What was attached and under which profile: `name`, `profile`, `type`, `version`, `relationship` — the `/AFRelationship` that was written, prescribed by the profile or given — and `bytes`. Empty before the call.

## Encryption

*doc* **encrypt** ?**-user** *text*? ?**-owner** *text*? ?**-permissions** *list*? ?**-metadata** *bool*?

: Encrypts the document with the standard security handler in revision 6: AES-256 for strings, streams and the file as a whole (`/V 5 /R 6`, crypt filter method `/AESV3`). The older revisions — RC4 and AES-128 — are deprecated in PDF 2.0 and are not written: a writer that offers them offers a choice whose wrong half looks exactly like the right one from outside. Returns the state as a dictionary, without the key and without the derived values. **encrypt state** answers the same later.

  **The two passwords.** The *user* password opens the document with the permissions below, the *owner* password opens it with all of them. Without **-owner** the user password serves as both; an empty owner password is not offered on its own, being the first one any reader tries. An empty **-user** password is allowed and is often what is wanted — the document opens for everyone and it is the owner password that lifts the restrictions — but then **-owner** has to say what that password is: **both empty is refused**, since it would hand every reader every right and leave a file that is encrypted and restricts nothing.

  **A password is cut to its first 127 bytes** (ISO 32000-2, 7.6.4.3.2), and to *bytes* rather than characters: the text is encoded to UTF-8 first, so a long password of umlauts reaches the limit sooner than its length suggests, and the cut may fall inside a UTF-8 sequence. That is what the standard prescribes and what a reader truncating the same password does, so the two still agree; it is named here because a caller generating passwords needs to know that two that differ only after the 127th byte are the same password.

  **-permissions** takes a list of names: `print`, `modify` (change the content), `copy` (extract text and graphics), `annotate` (annotations and form fields), `fill` (fill in existing form fields), `assemble` (insert, rotate and delete pages, add bookmarks) and `highres` (print at full quality). The default is `all`; the empty list grants nothing and still leaves a document that can be read on screen, since reading is not a permission. The bits the standard fixes are set by the package (ISO 32000-2, Table 22). Note what permissions are worth: they are a statement of intent that a conforming reader honours, not a lock — the content is decrypted either way once the file opens.

  **-metadata 0** leaves the XMP packet in the clear, so a cataloguing system can index a document it may not open.

  **AESV3 is a PDF 2.0 feature**, so the document needs `-version 2.0`; the version is not raised behind the caller's back, the missing call is named instead.

  **encrypt has to come first** — before the first page, the first line of text, the first pattern, and before **language** and **link**, which build their entries at the call rather than at write time. A stream or a string written before the cipher was installed would stay in the clear inside a file whose `/Encrypt` says otherwise, and a reader decrypting what was never encrypted ends up with an empty value and no complaint from any tool. The call is refused rather than repaired, naming the page or the object in the way.

  **PDF/A forbids encryption** (the `Encrypt` keyword shall not be used in the trailer), and a ZUGFeRD or Order-X invoice is a PDF/A-3 document — both combinations are refused, in whichever order the two calls are made.

  Two things stay readable on purpose: the file identifier `/ID` in the trailer, which a reader compares before it has any key, and the strings of the encryption dictionary itself (ISO 32000-2, 7.6.2).

  **What is not there:** revisions 2 to 4 and RC4; reading someone else's encrypted file (**pdf import** refuses it by name); the public-key handler; encrypting only the attachments; and SASLprep (RFC 4013) on the password — for an ASCII password that is the identity, but a password with combining characters may be spelled differently here than by a reader that normalises. The cipher protects confidentiality and nothing else: it is not a signature, and pure Tcl gives no promise about side channels.

## Signatures

*doc* **sign** ?**-signer** *prefix*? ?**-size** *bytes*? ?**-subfilter** *profile*? ?**-name** *text*? ?**-reason** *text*? ?**-location** *text*? ?**-contact** *text*? ?**-date** *date*? ?**-field** *name*? ?**-page** *n*? ?**-rect** *{x y w h}*? ?**-appearance** *name*?

: Prepares the document for a digital signature (ISO 32000-2, 12.8) — an approval signature over the whole file, invisible unless **-rect** and **-appearance** give it a place on a page — and signs it as it is written where **-signer** says what to sign it with. Returns what was declared as a dictionary; **sign state** answers the same later. The call is made once and before the document is written; nothing is built at the call itself, so it may stand anywhere before the write.

  **How a signature gets into a file.** The signature dictionary goes out with placeholders in it: `/ByteRange`, four numbers of fixed width, `/Contents`, a hexadecimal string of zeros as wide as **-size** asks for, and — where the signing happens elsewhere and later — `/M`, a date of nothing but zeros. They are written over once the file exists, and every one of those edits is length-neutral by construction — the cross-reference offsets are already in the file, and `/ByteRange` describes the very file it sits in, so a byte gained or lost here would invalidate exactly the thing being written. `/ByteRange` covers the whole file except the value of `/Contents`, its `<` and `>` included; those bytes are what gets signed, and the CMS object made over them goes into the place the zeros held. Reserving the space first and padding it with zeros afterwards is not a trick but what 12.8.3.3.1 prescribes.

  **tclpdf prepares, the signature comes from outside.** Nothing in this package holds a private key, computes a digest or speaks CMS. **-signer** is a command prefix, called as `{*}$prefix $bytes` from the global context, and it has to answer a CMS SignedData object in DER — what `openssl cms -sign -outform DER` writes. The reason for the cut is not convenience: in production the private key is rarely in the application. It is on a smart card, in an HSM or with a signing service, and the prefix is where that gets plugged in. A prefix that answers nothing, or that answers text where bytes were asked for, is refused at the write rather than turned into hexadecimal nonsense inside the file.

  ~~~tcl
  proc mySigner {bytes} {
      # hand the bytes to whatever holds the key - a pipe to
      # "openssl cms -sign ... -outform DER", a card reader, a signing
      # service - and return the DER object it answers
  }
  $doc sign -signer mySigner -reason "Approved" -name "E. Mustermann"
  $doc write invoice.pdf
  ~~~

  **-size** is the room reserved for the signature value, in bytes, `16384` unless said otherwise. Measured here: a CMS object with an RSA-2048 certificate and its issuer is 2599 bytes, one with ECDSA P-256 2209 — an RFC 3161 timestamp, a longer chain or embedded revocation data come on top of that, which is why the default is generous rather than tight. Reserving too much costs twice the number in hexadecimal digits in the file and nothing else; reserving too little costs the write, which is refused with `TCLPDF SIGN SPACE` naming both numbers.

  **What the dictionary says about the signature** is the rest of the options, each written only where it was given: **-name** becomes `/Name` — the standard asks for it "only when it is not possible to extract the name from the signature", so a CMS object carrying the signer's certificate makes it redundant — **-reason** `/Reason`, **-location** `/Location` and **-contact** `/ContactInfo`. **-field** is the partial field name of the signature field, `Signature1` unless said otherwise, and it cannot be empty: a field dictionary without one is not a field at all but a widget annotation (12.7.4.2). **-page** is the page the widget sits on, counted from 0 as `page current` counts; it has to exist when the file is written, which is where it is checked.

  **-date is the time of signing**, `/M` in the signature dictionary: a PDF date such as `D:20260820132111+02'00'`, the word `now`, or the empty string for no entry at all. **`/M` is written in either way of signing**, and that is the point of the option's default. `now` means the moment of *signing*, not the moment of preparing, and the two ways reach it differently: where **-signer** signs the document as it is written, the moment is the write and the value is written outright; where there is no **-signer**, the entry goes out as the placeholder `D:00000000000000+00'00'` — a year no clock produces — and **::tclpdf::sign digest** writes the real time over it, into exactly that room, before it hands the bytes out. Either way the time states when the signature was made, and either way it lies inside the bytes the signature covers, because `/M` is written behind the reserved room and everything behind that room falls into the second range. An explicit date is written as it stands in both ways: that one is the caller's own statement about a time it knows, not a guess this package made. **-date {}** keeps `/M` out of the dictionary altogether. The zone offset is spelled as the file version spells it, exactly as `/CreationDate` is (7.9.4) — a 2.0 file drops the trailing apostrophe, and the placeholder is one character shorter there.

  **Why `/M` is written at all**, since Table 255 asks for it "only when the time of signing is not available in the signature" and a CMS object carries a `signingTime` attribute: ETSI EN 319 142-1, Table 1 requires the entry ("shall be present") for every PAdES baseline level while forbidding that attribute, and a shall beats a should. The measurements agree with the shall. Acrobat takes the time from `/M` alone and reports "signing time not available" for a file whose CMS object states the time and whose time `pdfsig` prints out; and every compared signer — pyHanko, the EU DSS library, iText, LibreOffice, PDFBox — writes `/M` without exception. **-date {}** is the way back for whoever reads Table 255 to the letter, and it is the one way to get a signature out of this package with no time in the dictionary.

  **Without -signer the document is written prepared rather than signed**, and `/ByteRange` is filled in all the same. That is not a half-finished file but the first stage of the way a signature is usually made — see **::tclpdf::sign digest** below. Filling in `/ByteRange` at that point is the whole purpose of the stage: without it nobody on the other side knows which bytes to hash. What such a file still carries as a placeholder is `/Contents` and, unless **-date {}** said otherwise, `/M`.

  **-subfilter is the signature profile, and it is the caller's choice** rather than a consequence of the file version. It takes `pkcs7`, which writes `/adbe.pkcs7.detached` and is the default, or `cades`, which writes `/ETSI.CAdES.detached`. Table 255 leaves a writer exactly these two — the three other values it lists are not offered at all: `adbe.x509.rsa_sha1` and `adbe.pkcs7.sha1` are deprecated in PDF 2.0 with "PDF writers shall not use this value for this key", and `ETSI.RFC3161` belongs to a document timestamp (`/Type /DocTimeStamp`), which is a different thing from a signature. Each value brings its own version floor, `1.6` for the one and `2.0` for the other, and a document below it is refused naming the version rather than raised behind the caller's back. The default is the one that claims less, and it is the lower floor as well — deliberately: PDF/A-2 and -3 are profiles of ISO 32000-1 and are written as 1.7 at most, so a package that always demanded 2.0 would refuse every ZUGFeRD invoice by the back door, through a version error.

  **`cades` is a promise about the signature, not a second name for it.** `/ETSI.CAdES.detached` claims a PAdES signature (ETSI EN 319 142-1), and that claim is about the CMS object the **-signer** prefix hands back — which this package does not make. Table 1 of that standard puts the `signing-time` attribute at "shall not be present" for every baseline level, and a signer writes that attribute unless it is told otherwise: `openssl cms -sign` leaves it out with `-no_signing_time`, which OpenSSL 3 has and the LibreSSL shipped as `/usr/bin/openssl` on macOS does not, while pyHanko and the EU DSS library omit it by themselves and BouncyCastle adds it unasked. Measured with OpenSSL 3.6.3: `-cades -no_signing_time` produces an object this package takes under the claim, and the same signer without the switch is refused. So whoever asks for `cades` needs a signer that omits it, and a CMS object that carries it is refused rather than written, with `TCLPDF SIGN SIGNINGTIME`, on both ways in. `pkcs7` claims nothing of the sort and takes the object as it comes. Which value is in force matters to the signer either way, since one promises a CAdES-BES object and the other a plain CMS one, so **sign state** answers it.

  **The signature is invisible unless it is given a place to be seen.** Without **-rect** the widget carries `/Rect [0 0 0 0]` and `/F 4`, which is the standard's own example of an invisible signature (12.8.5.3) and the only spelling of it PDF/A admits — veraPDF's rule 6.3.2-2 requires the `Print` flag set and `Hidden`, `Invisible`, `ToggleNoView` and `NoView` clear, which rules out the two other ways 12.7.5.5 offers. A rectangle of no area is also the one case Table 166 exempts from carrying an appearance stream, and there is none. `/F 4` is written for a visible field just the same: rule 6.3.2-2 asks those flags of *every* annotation, and a visible one is no exception. Field and widget are written as a single object, which 12.5.6.19 permits where a field has exactly one widget and a signature field never has more (12.7.5.5); `/AcroForm` carries the field and `/SigFlags 3`.

  **-rect and -appearance make the field visible, and neither works without the other.** **-rect** is `{x y w h}` — the top left corner of the field and its size, in the unit of the document, y counted from the top of the page the way **link -at** and **rect -at** count it — on the page **-page** names. **-appearance** is the name of a form XObject the caller built with **form create**; it is hung into the widget as `/AP << /N … >>`, which is what an appearance stream is (12.5.5, Table 168). The rectangle is checked for shape at the call, but where it lands on the page and which form the name stands for are worked out at the **write**, exactly as **-page** is, so both the page and the form may be created after the **sign** call as well as before it; a name no form of this document carries is refused there, naming the ones it has.

  **This package does not draw the appearance, and that is deliberate.** A signature appearance is a frame with a name, a reason and a date in it — that is layout, and the caller's document does layout already: it draws form XObjects, breaks lines, embeds faces and knows its own unit. A drawing engine here would be a second place where the font, the wording and the arrangement are decided, and the caller could overrule none of it. So what is built here is the part only a signature module can build — the field, the rectangle and the reference — and what goes into the rectangle is the caller's; `examples/08.02-signature-visible.tcl` draws one and shows both ways of signing it. Give the form the size the **-rect** has: an appearance stream is mapped from its bounding box into the rectangle of its annotation (12.5.5), so a form of 70 by 26 shown in a rectangle of 35 by 13 comes out at half size, lettering and all.

  **Each half without the other is refused** rather than silently completed. **-rect** without **-appearance** is a visible annotation with nothing to show: veraPDF's rule 6.3.3-1 fails it, and a reader draws it as nothing at all. Measured on the same bytes, once with the `/AP` entry and once with it blanked out to the same length: with it, `isCompliant true`, 555 checks passed and none failed; without it, `isCompliant false`, 482 passed and one failed — clause 6.3.3, test 1, "An annotation does not contain an appearance dictionary". **-appearance** without **-rect** is a drawing on a rectangle of no area, which nothing ever paints. A **-rect** that is not four values, that carries something other than a number, or whose width or height is not above zero is refused as well, and so is one lying entirely beside the page it names — that message gives the rectangle and the media box both in points, which is what tells apart a y counted from the bottom and a rectangle given in millimetres on a document set to points. A rectangle merely **sticking out** over the edge is allowed: nothing else in this package takes the page edge for a boundary, a media box need not begin at zero, and a field at the very edge is the caller's business.

  **What a visible appearance cannot show is the time of signing, unless the signing happens at the write.** The form is part of the document and is drawn when the document is written, while the time of signing is only known at that moment where **-signer** signs it there and then. In that one-stage way the time is known, and an appearance may state it — pass the same value to **-date** and the picture and `/M` say the same thing. In the two-stage way it is not: the file is handed on and signed later, possibly days later on another machine, so an appearance naming a time there would be a picture of something that has not happened. What is left is to say in the appearance where the time is to be found — which is what the example does — or to reserve a placeholder of fixed width in the drawing as well and overwrite it in the finished file the way `/M` is overwritten, which binds the layout to one text width and one set of face metrics. That is why the package does it for nobody.

  **A PDF/A or ZUGFeRD document may be signed** — measured, not assumed. The ZUGFeRD example signed comes out of veraPDF with `isCompliant true` and 0 failed checks over 2476 checks performed, where the unsigned file has 2407; Mustangproject calls it valid, and the embedded invoice is byte-identical to the one in the unsigned file. The 69 additional checks are the signature rules of ISO 19005-3, 6.4.3 — the digest covering the entire file, the signer's certificate being present in the CMS object, a single `SignerInfo` in it — which were simply not applicable before.

  **A timestamp needs nothing here, and that is worth knowing.** A PAdES baseline signature at level B-T carries an RFC 3161 timestamp as an **unsigned** attribute of the CMS object, which means the signer puts it there and this package never sees it as anything but bytes. Nothing has to be built for it: reserve enough room and hand the work to a signer that timestamps. Measured on such an object, made by pyHanko against a public timestamp authority: 7108 bytes against the 16384 reserved by default. The check that refuses a `signing-time` under `cades` knows about this — a timestamp token carries a `signingTime` of its own, and one that sits **behind** the token belongs to it rather than to the signature. What this package does not write is a **document** timestamp, the other way the standard offers: that is a dictionary of its own with `/Type /DocTimeStamp` and `/SubFilter /ETSI.RFC3161`, and it would have to be built.

  **What this version does not do.** A second signature is not written by **sign**, which prepares a document written in one piece: a further **sign** call on the same document is refused. It is written by **::tclpdf::sign add** onto the finished file instead, as an incremental update (7.5.6) — see below. No document timestamp and no `/DocMDP` certification. A signed document is **not** byte-identical over two writes: `/M` names the moment of the write, and the CMS object carries its own `signingTime` and a random number besides. A document prepared without **-signer** is, and stays so as long as it is only written: its `/M` is the placeholder, which is a constant, and its `/Contents` is zeros. That is the file the two-stage way hands on, and **::tclpdf::sign digest** is what makes it particular — from there on it names a time. **Encryption and a signature exclude each other** and are refused in whichever order the two calls are made: 7.6.2 exempts exactly one thing from encryption, the hexadecimal string of `/Contents`, and not the other strings of the dictionary around it.

  **A signed document has to be written to a file.** **writeChannel** is refused while a signature is pending, and refused before the bytes go anywhere rather than after: `/ByteRange` is written into the finished file and computed from it, and a socket or a pipe cannot be read back.
