Where a signed document has to reach a channel, the two-stage way is the road — write it, sign it, send the file.

  **What a signature is not.** It shows that the bytes have not changed since they were signed, and that whoever holds a particular private key signed them. Whether that key's certificate means anything is decided by the trust store of whoever opens the document, not by the document. This package builds no certificate chain, checks none, and embeds no revocation information; a signature that is to stay verifiable for years has to carry all of that in the CMS object the signer hands back.

*doc* **sign state**

: What was declared and what the last write made of it, as a dictionary: `date` (what **-date** was given, as it was given — the resolved time stands in the file), `field`, `page`, `rect` and `appearance` (what was given for a visible field, both empty for the invisible one), `size`, `subFilter` (the name that went into the dictionary, `/adbe.pkcs7.detached` or `/ETSI.CAdES.detached`, which is what a signer has to produce for), `signed` (whether a value was actually written), `byteRange` (the four numbers as they went into the file) and `length` (the size of the CMS object in bytes, empty where none was written). Empty before **sign** was called. The signer is deliberately not in it: a command prefix may carry a passphrase or the path to a key file, and a state dictionary is the kind of thing that ends up in a log.

**::tclpdf::sign digest** *path* ?**-date** *date*?

: What is to be signed in a file that **sign** prepared and **write** wrote — the two-stage way, for a signature that is not made in this process. Returns a dictionary: `bytes` (what the CMS object has to be made over), `byteRange` (the four numbers as the file states them), `offset` (where the value will go), `size` (how many bytes are reserved for it), `subFilter` (what the file claims to be — the value **sign -subfilter** put there, read back out of the file, since there is no document object left to ask) and `date` (the time of signing this call wrote, empty where the file already stated one of its own). The file is checked to agree with itself before anything is handed out — a `/ByteRange` that does not describe this very file, because something was appended to it or because the gap is not where `/Contents` is, would produce a digest no reader recomputes and a signature that fails with nothing to point at. A file whose `/ByteRange` is still the placeholder was never written through **write**; that is refused as well, rather than signing four zeros along with the rest.

  **This call writes the file.** It is not the read-only operation its name suggests, and it cannot be: `/M` lies inside the bytes it is about to hand out, so the time of signing has to be in the file *before* they leave — a date written afterwards would be a claim nobody signed, and one written when the document was made would name the moment of preparing instead. So the placeholder is replaced by the current time, in exactly the room it occupies and not a character more, and the file is written back before the bytes are answered. Everything else about it is unchanged, to the byte; that the length did not move is verified rather than assumed.

  **What follows from it: the second call answers the same time as the first.** Once the placeholder is filled it is no longer a placeholder, and a further **digest** of the same file leaves `/M` alone — the bytes it hands out are the same bytes, so a signature made after the second call is as good as one made after the first. What differs is the `date` key, which is empty from the second call on: it reports what *this* call wrote, and this one wrote nothing. The file itself states the time either way. The same holds from the first call for a document written with a **-date** of its own or with **-date {}**: both are the caller's own statement about the time, and neither is overwritten by a call that knows less.

  **-date** names a time other than the current one, for a caller who has to state the moment of signing rather than measure it; `now` is the default and means the moment the bytes are handed out. It takes a PDF date as 7.9.4 spells it, and it is written *over* the placeholder, so it has to be exactly as long as one — which is what `::tclpdf::pdfObj date` produces for the version the file states in its header. A date of the wrong length is refused rather than fitted in, since nothing inside the signed range may change length.

  **These two are package commands rather than document methods**, because the second stage happens in another process — often on another machine, and days later. A script that only embeds a signature never creates a document, so it never loads the module by using one: it needs `package require tclpdf::sign` of its own. Where **sign** was called on a document in the same process, the module is already there.

  ~~~tcl
  $doc sign -reason "Approved"
  $doc write invoice.pdf
  set job [::tclpdf::sign digest invoice.pdf]
  set der [signWithTheCard [dict get $job bytes]]
  ::tclpdf::sign embed invoice.pdf $der
  ~~~

**::tclpdf::sign embed** *path der*

: Puts a DER-encoded CMS object into a file prepared that way, over the reserved zeros and padded back out to the reserved length, and returns the length of the object in bytes. The file keeps the length it had, to the byte, and that is verified rather than assumed. An object that does not fit is refused with `TCLPDF SIGN SPACE` instead of being truncated — a truncated signature yields a file that every reader calls signed and no verifier accepts. Two more refusals, and both were measured on what happens without them: text where bytes were asked for — a character above `0xff` cannot be a byte of a DER object — went in silently under Tcl 8.6 and raised an error about code points under Tcl 9, and a file that was changed after it was written took the object without a word. The second is the same check **digest** makes, and for the same reason: a real signature over the wrong bytes verifies nowhere and passes every structural check on the way. A third refusal applies only where the file claims PAdES — the claim is read out of its `/SubFilter`, since no document object is left to ask: an object stating a signing time is refused with `TCLPDF SIGN SIGNINGTIME`, exactly as it is on the **-signer** way.

**::tclpdf::sign add** *path* ?**-signer** *prefix*? ?**-size** *bytes*? ?**-subfilter** *profile*? ?**-name** *text*? ?**-reason** *text*? ?**-location** *text*? ?**-contact** *text*? ?**-date** *date*? ?**-field** *name*? ?**-page** *n*?

: Adds **another** signature to a file that is already finished — signed or not — by appending an incremental update (7.5.6) to it. Every byte the file already has stays where it is, which is the whole point: an existing signature covers the bytes of its own revision, and those bytes must not move. The options are the ones **sign** takes, and they mean the same; what is different is the subject, a path rather than a document, and that is why this is a package command like **digest** and **embed**. It answers what was written, including `appended` — how many bytes the update added.

  **What it writes is more than the signature dictionary.** A signature needs a field, and a field has to be reachable: the update therefore carries a new copy of the **catalogue**, whose `/AcroForm /Fields` gains the new field and whose `/SigFlags` is set, and a new copy of the **page object**, whose `/Annots` gains the widget. Both old copies stay in the file, as 7.5.6 prescribes — the added cross-reference section decides which one a reader sees. A file that carries no `/AcroForm` at all, because it was never signed, gets one.

  **The older signature stays valid, and `pdfsig` will say something about it that looks like a complaint and is not.** It reports `Not total document signed` for the older one — correctly: that signature covers its own revision, and the file has grown since. The newest signature covers everything, and for it the same tool says `Total document signed`. Measured on a document signed twice this way: ranges `[0 586] [33356 35649]` for the first and `[0 35785] [68555 69333]` for the second, where 69333 is the size of the file. That last number has to be exact — ISO 32000-2, 12.8.1 puts the end of the range at the end of the `%%EOF` that terminates the update, and one byte beside it turns `Total document signed` into its opposite without anything being cryptographically wrong.

  **A PDF/A or ZUGFeRD document survives it** — measured: the ZUGFeRD invoice of `examples/05.02` signed twice this way comes out of veraPDF with `isCompliant true` and 0 failed checks over 2528 checks performed, where the unsigned file has 2407, and Mustangproject calls it valid. Nothing of `/Info` or the metadata stream is rewritten, which is what keeps the claim intact.

  **Refused by name, rather than written wrong:** a **visible** field, because what a visible field shows is a form XObject and drawing one takes a document — a font, a unit, a layout — while this call has a file and no document; **raising the file's version**, because the header lies inside the bytes that stay untouched, so a file below the version floor of the `-subfilter` asked for is refused instead of being lifted behind the caller's back; and a second signature onto an **unfinished** one, a file whose newest signature still holds its reserved zeros — **digest** and **embed** reach the newest signature of a file, so filling the older one afterwards would not be possible, and filling it first would sign bytes this update then changes. Finish that signature, then add the next.

## Writing

Two calls write the document, and neither finishes it.

**PDF 2.0 differences that are written for you.** A 2.0 file spells the zone offset of a date without the trailing apostrophe (7.9.4), and the version decides it — declaring `ua -part 2` is enough. `ProcSet`, `CharSet` and `CIDSet`, all deprecated in 2.0, are written by no version of this package.

*doc* **write** *path*

: Writes the document to a file. Writing does not finish the document: a second **write** of an unchanged document produces a byte-identical file — unless the document is signed, where `/M` and the CMS object carry the moment of the write, or encrypted, where every string and every stream takes a fresh initialisation vector and reusing one would be the mistake; there the second file is a different sequence of bytes carrying the same objects, opened by the same password. Drawing between two writes works either way — the second file carries the additions.

*doc* **writeChannel** *channel*

: Writes to an open channel instead of a file — a CGI response, a socket, a pipe. The caller opens and closes it; the channel is put into binary translation here, because that is what decides whether the bytes arrive unchanged. The cross-reference offsets are counted from the `%PDF-` header as they are written, not asked of the channel, so a pipe or a socket — where `tell` answers −1 — and a channel that already carries a CGI header get a correct table. **write** and **writeChannel** may be combined freely — the same document can go to a file and into a response; the one exception is a document prepared with **sign**, where **writeChannel** is refused by name, because `/ByteRange` and the signature are written into the finished file and a channel cannot be read back.

## Events

*doc* **on** *event script* / *doc* **off** *token* / *doc* **events** / *doc* **subscribers** ?*event*?

: The document publishes events while it is written: `beforeWrite`, `resources`, `catalog`, `info` and `afterWrite`, plus `pageAdded` after each `page add` — the ones **text -paginate** and a breaking **table** add included. This is how attachments, ZUGFeRD and the output intent attach themselves without the core knowing about them, and it is available to callers for the same purpose. **on** returns a token and refuses an event that is not one of these six, naming them; **events** answers the list. **off** takes that token, not the event and script again, and accepts an unknown one silently. Subscribers run in registration order and are called with the emitting object followed by whatever the emitter passes on. The write-time events fire on **every** write, so a subscriber that creates objects must be idempotent: take its object numbers from **reservation** once and write over them on later runs, instead of reserving fresh ones each time. How to build an extension on top of this — the supported methods, the contracts and two worked examples — is the subject of `doc/PLUGINS.md` in the source distribution.

## Error codes

Every refusal is a Tcl error whose message begins with `tclpdf:` and names what went wrong and where. A number that is not one — `NaN`, `Inf` — is turned away where it is read as a measurement rather than where the arithmetic later trips over it, so the message names the option the caller wrote. The wording of a message is **not** a contract — it may be sharpened in any release. Where a script is meant to handle a refusal programmatically, the error also carries a machine-readable `-errorcode`, and that **is** a contract: a Tcl list whose first word is `TCLPDF`, followed by a fixed hierarchy and then the facts of the case. `try` … `trap` matches such a list by prefix, so `trap {TCLPDF FONT GLYPH} {msg opts} {…}` handles the whole class below without reading the message; the full list is `[dict get $opts -errorcode]`. **Every refusal this package makes carries one**, measured over the whole tree and held there by a guard in the suite: on 2026-08-25 five hundred and eleven of them did not, and they had accumulated one module at a time — each of them individually harmless, together a coin toss over whether a given mistake could be handled at all. The **first two words** are the contract: `TCLPDF` and the topic. Where a topic has classes, the **third** word is one of them and is a contract too; what follows are the facts of the case, and those may grow. The codes described one by one below are the ones a script is most likely to want; the table at the end of this section lists every topic and every class in the package. Further codes may be added in later releases, and a class may gain members — a `trap` written against a prefix keeps working when that happens.

**TCLPDF FONT GLYPH** *codepoint position fontname*

: A character cannot be written with the font in use — it lies outside the font's encoding, or the face carries no glyph for it. *codepoint* is the character in `U+XXXX` notation (uppercase hexadecimal, four digits or more), *position* its 0-based index in the string as handed in, and *fontname* the name the document knows the font by: the PostScript name of a standard face, or the alias given to **font embed**. The same code is thrown wherever the refusal happens — drawing, measuring, breaking a paragraph, a table cell — so one handler covers them all; a typical one retries the string with a fallback face, which is what `examples/02.04-missing-glyphs.tcl` does. For text drawn right-to-left the *codepoint* named is the mirrored partner of the character handed in — the glyph the face would have to carry — exactly as the message names it.

**TCLPDF FONT KIND** *kind alias*

: The font *alias* is of a *kind* the writer has no case for — thrown while the document is being written, not while the font is being taken in. The kinds written are `truetype`, `type1` and `type3`. It is reached only by a font entry the package did not build itself, and it exists because the alternative is worse: before it, an unknown kind fell through to the TrueType branch and died inside a `dict get`, naming a key rather than the font.

**TCLPDF FONT OUTLINES** *alias path*

: Every character the face covers has an empty outline, so the face draws nothing — thrown by **font embed**, before the face is taken into the document. *alias* is the name the call asked for and *path* the file as it was handed in. This is what a colour font looks like from the outside: its pictures sit in a `COLR`/`CPAL`, `CBDT`, `sbix` or `SVG` table, none of which this package writes, and a document embedding it would come out blank with nothing anywhere reporting it — not the API, not `pdftotext`, not `pdffonts`, not `qpdf`, not veraPDF. The refusal hangs on the empty outline and not on the colour table, so a colour face carrying real monochrome fallback outlines is embedded as any other; one empty outline among many, which is what the space is in most faces, is not this case. A handler embeds a monochrome face instead, or draws the symbols as a Type 3 font with **font define** and **font glyph**.

**TCLPDF FONT SOURCE** *format*

: What was handed to **font embed** is not a font of the format the road it took expects — thrown by the readers behind the facade, before anything is taken into the document. *format* names the reader that turned it away: `sfnt` for a TrueType or OpenType file, `cff` for a bare CFF table, `type1` for a `.pfb`, `.pfa` or `.t1`. This is the answer that says *wrong format*, and it is worth telling apart from the next one: a script that tries a file through several readers retries on this code and gives up on the others.

**TCLPDF FONT DAMAGED** *part*

: The file is of the right format and is broken — a table directory that ends inside an entry, a `cmap` promising more groups than the file holds, a `PFB` segment reaching past the end, a CFF whose Top DICT is empty. *part* names what was being read: `directory`, `cmap`, `cff`, `charset`, `pfb`, `type1`, `vhea`. Retrying such a file with another reader is pointless, which is the whole reason it does not share a code with `SOURCE`.

**TCLPDF FONT UNSUPPORTED** *format* ?*detail*?

: The file is sound and is a format, or a variant of one, that this package does not read: `collection` (a `.ttc` — extract the single face first), `cff2` (the variable-font CFF, a different charstring interpreter altogether), `cmap` with the format number after it (formats 4 and 12 are read), `charset` and `encoding` for the predefined CFF tables. Nothing is wrong with the file; a handler converts it once, outside, or picks another cut of the face.

**TCLPDF FONT TABLE** *tag*

: The face has no *tag* table and cannot be embedded without it — `head`, `hhea`, `hmtx` and `maxp` for any sfnt, and `CFF ` for one whose signature promises CFF outlines. A `cmap` is deliberately **not** among them: a subset this package built carries none on purpose, and demanding one would make the package unable to re-read its own output.

**TCLPDF FONT SUBSET** *reason*

: A face cannot be reduced to the glyphs a document uses. `outlines` is the case that occurs: CFF outlines are charstrings in a table this package reads no further than its Top DICT, so there is no `loca` and no `glyf` to rewrite, and such a face is embedded whole instead. It is its own class rather than a kind of damage — the face is perfectly sound, and it is only this one operation it does not admit.

**TCLPDF TEXT ANCHOR** *anchor*

: **-anchor** was given a word that is none of the four: `baseline`, `top`, `middle`, `bottom`. Refused rather than read as `baseline` in silence, which is what a misspelt anchor used to do.

**TCLPDF ANNOT LINE** *end* / **TCLPDF ANNOT BOX** *kind* / **TCLPDF ANNOT POINTS** *kind*

: A geometry annotation is missing the geometry it is made of — the named end of a line (`-from` or `-to`), the corner and extent of a square or circle, or at least two points for a polygon or polyline. Raised before anything is written.

**TCLPDF ANNOT WIDTH** *kind width*

: **-width** of a geometry annotation is the thickness of the line it is drawn with and has to be above zero.

**TCLPDF ANNOT ATTACHMENT** *what* …

: A file attachment annotation could not be made. `AT` — no **-at**; `NAME` — no **-name**; `UNKNOWN` with the name after it — nothing was attached under that name, so **attach** has to come first; `ICON` with the value after it — **-icon** is one of the four of Table 184.

**TCLPDF TABLE SPAN** *what* / **TCLPDF TABLE COLUMN** *key* / **TCLPDF TABLE WIDTH** *what* / **TCLPDF TABLE STYLE** *key*

: A table could not be laid out: a `rowSpan` or `colSpan` that reaches past the last row or column or is not a positive integer, a column property that is not a number, fixed column widths that do not fit the table's own width, or a style key with a value outside what it admits. All of them are raised before a cell is measured, so a table with a mistyped value draws nothing at all rather than half of itself.

**TCLPDF TABLE DIRECTION** *direction*

: A table style asked for a writing direction a table cannot set. `ttb` is the case: a table breaks its cells by the column width and steps the lines down the row, which is the direction a vertical line already writes in. There is no vertical table — a page of columns is **textLines -direction ttb** for the breaking and one **text** call per column for the placing.

**TCLPDF GRAPHICS OVERPRINT** *what*

: An **overprint** call is wrong in itself. *what* is `fill` or `stroke` where the value is not a boolean, `mode` where it is neither 0 nor 1, and `NONE` where the call named none of the three switches and would have written a graphics state that changes nothing. A wrong PDF version is **not** this code: asking for `-fill` or `-mode` below PDF 1.3, or `-stroke` below 1.2, raises `TCLPDF VERSION` like every other version floor.

**TCLPDF IMAGE INTERPOLATE PDFA** *alias* …

: A picture was placed with **-interpolate 1** in a document claiming PDF/A. Raised at the placement where the declaration came first, and by **pdfa** where the picture came first — in that direction the code carries every alias already in the file rather than one. Drop **-interpolate**, or drop the declaration; there is no third answer, since the key is written into the picture's dictionary and the dictionary is in the file by the time the profile is claimed.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT TABLES** *alias*

: The face has no `COLR` and `CPAL` tables and therefore no colour glyphs — thrown by **colorFont**, before anything is defined. *alias* is the name the call asked for. An ordinary face goes into the document with **font embed**.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT CHAR** *codepoint alias*

: The face has no glyph for *codepoint*, so there is no colour glyph to draw for it — thrown by **colorFont**, and before the font is defined, so nothing is left half built. *codepoint* is in `U+XXXX` notation.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT BASE** *codepoint glyph alias*

: *codepoint* reaches glyph number *glyph* of the face and that glyph has no entry in the `COLR` table, so it is not a colour base glyph. Most glyphs of a colour font are layers or plain outlines, and drawing one of those would produce a blank glyph rather than an error, which is why this is refused. A handler sets the character with an embedded face instead.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT COMPOSITE** *codepoint glyph alias*

: Layer glyph *glyph* of *codepoint* is a composite — it draws other glyphs rather than an outline of its own — and this package assembles those only when a face is embedded. The layers of a colour glyph are normally drawn shapes rather than letters with accents, so this is unusual; a handler sets the character with an embedded face.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT EMPTY** *codepoint alias*

: Every layer of *codepoint* has an empty outline, so the glyph would draw nothing. Refused for the same reason as `TCLPDF FONT OUTLINES` one level down: the document would be valid, extractable and empty, with nothing anywhere reporting it.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT LIMIT** *count*

: **-chars** names *count* characters and a Type 3 font is addressed by single bytes, so it holds at most 255 glyphs (ISO 32000-2, 9.6.5.3). Checked before the face is read, so the 256th character is a refusal and not a silent truncation. A handler builds a second font for the rest.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT PALETTE** *asked count*

: **-palette** names palette *asked* and the face has *count* of them, numbered from 0. Checked before anything is built — the reader would refuse a wrong number too, but only on the first layer that is not the text-colour sentinel, so a glyph that takes the text colour throughout would get away with it until the next one.

**TCLPDF COLR** *…*

: What the `COLR`/`CPAL` reader itself refuses, one class rather than an entry apiece — these are the codes **colorFont** passes through unchanged, since they say something about the *face* rather than about the call. Eight of them: `MISSING` (the face has a `COLR` table and no `CPAL`, or neither — the word after it is the table that is absent), `VERSION` (a `COLR` other than version 0, or a `CPAL` above version 1; the two words after it are the table and the version found), `EMPTY` (a `COLR` declaring no base glyphs, or a `CPAL` holding no colours; the word after it is the table), `TRUNCATED` (a table that ends before what its own header promises; the word after it is the table), `LAYERS` (a base glyph whose layer run reaches past the last layer record), `RECORDS` (a palette whose colour run reaches past the last colour record), `PALETTE` (**-palette** names a palette the face does not have — the same case `TCLPDF COLORFONT PALETTE` catches at the call, reached here only by a face whose header and body disagree) and `ENTRY` (a layer naming a palette entry that is not there). Each message says what the table holds and what was asked of it; `trap {TCLPDF COLR}` catches the lot, and `trap {TCLPDF COLORFONT}` does *not*, which is why they are written out here.

**TCLPDF TIFF STRIPS**

: A TIFF has more strips than this package will stack. A compression that carries state from one row to the next begins afresh in every strip, so such a file becomes one image XObject per strip; above 256 of them the picture is refused rather than placed, and nothing is written for it. Measured over 194 files: 80 are single-stripped, 13 lie above this line, and the largest has 1922 strips. The message names how many strips the file has and where the line lies. The way out is to re-save it with a `RowsPerStrip` that holds the whole picture, or with no compression or PackBits, whose strips are joined into a single stream.

**TCLPDF TIFF STACKED**

: A TIFF that is several image XObjects was asked to be a mask, or to wear one. `Mask` and `SMask` name a single image XObject (ISO 32000-2, Table 87 and Table 143), and a mask laid over a stack would be drawn over each part in turn instead of over the picture — every part is an image of its own on the unit square (8.9.6.3). A TIFF that reaches the file as ONE image is refused neither way: it may be a soft mask like any greyscale picture, and it may wear one. Thrown when the picture's objects are built, so nothing is written for it; a picture already placed on an earlier page is caught too.

**TCLPDF TIFF** *…*

: What the TIFF reader itself refuses, one class rather than an entry apiece: `SIGNATURE` (not a TIFF at all — measured in a real holding, two of them were mail texts named `.tif`), `BIGTIFF`, `TILED`, `PLANAR`, `ALPHA`, `COMPRESSION`, `PHOTOMETRIC`, `DEPTH`, `SAMPLES`, `SAMPLEFORMAT`, `PREDICTOR`, `FILLORDER`, `BYTEORDER`, `JPEG`, `DIRECTORY`, `CIRCULAR`, `RANGE` and `DAMAGED`. Each names what the file is and what it would have to be re-saved as; `trap {TCLPDF TIFF}` catches the lot.

**TCLPDF IMPORT** *…*

: What the PDF reader refuses in a foreign file, one class rather than an entry apiece: `FILE`, `SYNTAX`, `DEPTH`, `XREF`, `OBJECT`, `OBJSTM`, `RECURSION`, `STREAM`, `FILTER`, `PREDICTOR`, `ROOT`, `PAGES`, `BOX`, `ROTATE`, `ENCRYPTED`, `XFA`, `OPTION` and `SERIALIZE`. `trap {TCLPDF IMPORT}` catches every one of them, and they reach the caller from **pdf import**, from **pdf info**, **pages**, **fonts**, **metadata** and **fields**, and from **::tclpdf::update open** alike — the commands share one reader. A damaged or hostile file is the case they exist for: a cross-reference chain that runs in a circle, an object stream that contains itself, a `/Length` that points at its own object, a page tree that names itself among its children, an array whose bracket never closes. Each of those used to end in a loop that did not return or in a raw Tcl error naming an operand instead of the file; naming the file and what is wrong with it is the whole point of the class.

**TCLPDF HYPHENATE LANGUAGE** *tag*

: No hyphenation patterns are loaded for *tag* — thrown by **::tclpdf::hyphenate word** and **::tclpdf::hyphenate languages** *tag*, and by every block that names an unloaded language with **-hyphenate** — a table whose style carries `hyphenate` among them, where the whole table is refused before its first cell is drawn — before a byte of it reaches the stream. *tag* is the spelling that was asked for, not one it might have resolved to. The refusal is the point: a block whose language is missing could only be set unhyphenated, and that produces a document which looks like the one that was asked for and is not. A handler has three ways on — load the file, fall back to another language, or set the block without **-hyphenate** and know that it did.

**TCLPDF HYPHENATE UNSET**

: **-hyphenate 1** asks for the language the document declares and the document declares none. The code carries no further words: there is one way into it and one way out of it. A handler either calls **language** first or names the language on the block itself, or, from a table, in the style that carries `hyphenate`.

**TCLPDF SIGN SPACE** *needed reserved*

: A CMS object does not fit into the space a signature reserved for it — thrown by **write** where a **sign -signer** prefix handed back one that is too long, and by **::tclpdf::sign embed** for the same reason. *needed* is the length of the DER object in bytes, *reserved* what **sign -size** put aside, and the two are what a handler needs: build the document again from the start with **sign -size** at *needed* or more. It is the *document* that has to be built again, not just written again — **sign** is called once and refuses a second call, so `-size` cannot be raised on a document that already carries a declaration, and the space is reserved before the digest is computed and cannot be grown afterwards without moving every byte behind it. Where the second stage failed instead, **::tclpdf::sign embed** raises the same code against the file's placeholder, and the placeholder is as fixed as the offsets around it: that file has to be produced again too. The refusal is the point — a signature written into too little room would have to be truncated, and a truncated one produces a file that every reader calls signed and no verifier accepts. The space is reserved before the digest is computed and cannot be grown afterwards without moving every byte behind it, which is why this is an error at the write rather than a repair.

**TCLPDF SIGN SIGNINGTIME**

: A CMS object states the time of signing where the document claims to be a PAdES signature — thrown by **write** where a **sign -signer** prefix handed back such an object, and by **::tclpdf::sign embed** for the same reason, in both cases only under **sign -subfilter cades**. The code carries no further words: there is one way into it and one way out of it, and neither needs a number. ETSI EN 319 142-1, Table 1 puts the `signing-time` attribute at "shall not be present" for every PAdES baseline level, while requiring the time in `/M`, which this package writes. The check looks for the attribute's object identifier, 1.2.840.113549.1.9.5, in the DER as a byte pattern; it does not parse ASN.1, and it is the one place this package looks inside the object at all. **A signature that carries an RFC 3161 timestamp token is not caught by it**, which matters because that is what a PAdES B-T signature is: such a token holds a `SignedData` of its own with a `signingTime` inside it, and refusing on that would refuse the very objects the profile asks for. The two are told apart by position — RFC 5652 puts `signedAttrs` before `unsignedAttrs`, so an outer signing time always stands *before* the first token identifier (1.2.840.113549.1.9.16.2.14) and one belonging to a token always after it — and the refusal falls only where the time comes first or where there is no token at all. An object that carries both is refused as before. The price of that coarseness is stated rather than hidden: certificates stand before the `SignerInfo`s in a DER, so this identifier inside a *certificate* would be read as an outer signing time; `-subfilter pkcs7` is the way past it. A handler has two ways on: have the object made by a signer that leaves the attribute out — pyHanko and the EU DSS library do so by themselves, `openssl cms -sign` does it with `-no_signing_time` (OpenSSL 3; the LibreSSL shipped as `/usr/bin/openssl` on macOS has no such switch), while BouncyCastle adds it unasked — or write the document with **-subfilter pkcs7**, which claims no PAdES and takes the object as it is. Note what the coarseness costs: the same identifier inside an embedded RFC 3161 timestamp token would be refused too, and this package produces no such token, so an object carrying one was assembled elsewhere by someone who knows what is in it.

**TCLPDF FIELD** *…*

: What an interactive form field refuses, one class rather than an entry apiece: `trap {TCLPDF FIELD}` catches all of them — a misspelt option *name* excepted, which the shared option parser refuses under `TCLPDF OPTION UNKNOWN` below, here as everywhere in this package — and none of them leaves anything behind — a refused call leaves the document exactly as it was, so a second attempt does not trip over the leavings of the first, and a refused write leaves no file. The word after `FIELD` names the case. About the field itself: `NAME` (no name, or one holding a period), `TAKEN` (a name already used, by another field or by `sign -field`), `UNKNOWN` (**field state** asked about a field that was never declared), `SUBCOMMAND` and `TYPE` (a word that is no field type), `PAGE`, `RECT` (not four numbers a PDF can hold, or no area — and `RECT OUTSIDE`, at the write, for a rectangle lying entirely beside its page), `STRUCTURE` (a widget that cannot go into the structure tree where it stands). About the values of a call: `BOOLEAN`, `ALIGN`, `SIZE`, `BORDERWIDTH`, `COLOUR`, `COLOURSPACE` (a colour with no `/DA` or `/MK` spelling), `FONT`, `MAXLEN`, `LENGTH`, `LINES`, `PASSWORD`, `COMBINATION` (two options that cannot both be given), `OPTIONS`, `INDEX`, `SELECT`, `TOP`. And `FLAG`, `SIGFLAGS`, `REGISTER`, `DECLARE` and `BUILD`, which are reached only by a field type of one's own. The three button types put their own word after `TCLPDF FIELD BUTTON`: `BUTTONS`, `CAPTION`, `ACTION`, `FIELDS`, `EXPORT`, `MARK`, `MARKSIZE`, `VALUE`, `UNISON`, `NOTOGGLE`, `CONTENTS`, `BOOLEAN`, `BORDERWIDTH`, `COLOUR` and the `PDFA` below. Each message names what was asked for and what the standard says about it; the facts of the case follow the class, as everywhere here.

**TCLPDF FIELD BUTTON PDFA** *name*

: The push button *name* carries **-action reset** and the document claims PDF/A — thrown when the document is **written**, and not at the call, because a document declares its profile and its fields in either order and the check can only be made once both are known. Three field refusals fall there rather than at the call, and they are the three that depend on something the call cannot yet know: this one, `TCLPDF FIELD PAGE` for a page that never came, and `TCLPDF FIELD RECT OUTSIDE` for a widget entirely beside its page. The profile admits no action on a widget annotation at all (veraPDF 6.3.3-3) and forbids `ResetForm` by name (6.5.1-1). A handler drops the button, or drops the PDF/A declaration; the refused write leaves no file behind, so the same script may write again to the same name.

**TCLPDF ANNOT** *…*

: What a note, a stamp or a text markup refuses, one class rather than an entry apiece: `trap {TCLPDF ANNOT}` catches all of them — a misspelt option *name* excepted, which the shared option parser refuses under `TCLPDF OPTION UNKNOWN` below — and none of them leaves anything behind, since every refusal is made before a byte reaches the file. The word after `ANNOT` names the case. About the call: `KIND` (an annotation this package does not write; the message names the ones it does), `MODULE` (a registered kind whose module does not provide it, reached only by a module of one's own), `NOTE` with `AT`, `CONTENTS`, `ICON` or `OPEN`, `STAMP` with `AT`, `NAME`, `SIZE` or `BOTH` (both `-name` and `-appearance`), `RECT` with `AT`, `SIZE`, `NUMBER` or `EMPTY`, `APPEARANCE` (a form of that name was never created) and `STRUCTURE` (a tagged document where an `Annot` element may not stand). About a markup's geometry, all under `QUADS`: `NONE` and `BOTH` (no road to the rectangles, or two), `AT`, `SHAPE`, `NUMBER`, `EMPTY`, `LINES` (a line break in `-text`), `EMPTYLINES`, `EMPTYTEXT`, `LEADING`, `ALIGN` (`-align justify`, which the measurement cannot follow) and `TYPE3` (a face with no descender to measure with). About the values: `COLOUR` (`-colour` and `-color` both given), `COLOURSPACE` (a colour `/C` has no spelling for), `OPACITY`, `DATE`. And three about a claim the document makes: `PDFA` *subtype*, for an archived document that would get an annotation with no appearance stream; `UA CONTENTS` *subtype*, for one with no description; `UA STRUCTURE` *subtype*, for one outside the structure tree. The last three are thrown at the call where the claim already stands, and at the **write** where it was made afterwards — a document declares its profile and its annotations in either order, and only the write has seen both. A refused write leaves no file behind, so the same script may write again to the same name.

**TCLPDF OPTION** *…*

: What the shared option parser refuses — every command of this package reads its `-name value` arguments through it, some sixty calls out of thirty-nine modules, so this is the code a mistake in the *call* carries whatever the command was, and `trap {TCLPDF OPTION}` catches the lot. The word after `OPTION` names the case, then come the facts: the option or key that was named, and last the context. `NUMBER` is one of them: a `NaN` or an `Inf` where a measurement was expected — both are doubles as far as Tcl is concerned, and both place nothing. `POINT` covers a coordinate that is not finite as well as one that is not two numbers the message already quotes — `field text`, `text`, `pdf import` — which is empty where the calling command passed none. Six of them: `UNKNOWN` *option context* (no such option; the message lists the ones that exist, which is what a caller who wrote `-colour` needs), `PAIRS` *context* (an odd number of words, so an option was left without its value), `REQUIRED` *option context* (an option that has to be given was not), `POINT` *option context* (a position that is not two numbers — `-at {20 [expr {$y+5}]}` is the usual way in, since braces do not substitute), and `DICT` *what context* and `KEY` *key what context* for the places a caller writes a dictionary rather than options: a table cell, a style, a column description. A misspelt option name was long the one refusal in this package that reached the caller as `NONE`, which made the commonest mistake of all the one a handler could not see: `trap {TCLPDF FIELD}` caught every wrong value and no wrong name. The caller's own spelling is a fact behind the class word rather than the class itself — a code of `TCLPDF OPTION -recht` would read the mistyped word as a class of its own, and nothing below `TCLPDF OPTION` could then be trapped.

**TCLPDF TEXT HYPHENS** *value context*

: **-hyphens** was given a value that is no boolean. *value* is what was written, *context* the call that read it — `textLines` or `textHeight`. Refused by both, although only **textLines** reads the option, so that a caller who builds one option list is told at the first of the two calls and not at the second.

**TCLPDF VERSION** *version*

: A feature was asked for that the document's PDF version does not offer — `opacity` in a 1.3 file, a form field in a 1.1 one, `layer create` below 1.5, `field -label` below 2.0. *version* is the version the document has to be at for the feature, which is what a handler needs: create the document with `-version` at that number, or raise it with `configure -version`, or leave the feature out. Nothing is ever raised behind the caller's back — whoever set `-version 1.3` said what the file may contain — and the code is the same from every module, some forty calls in the package, because they all ask through one place. The two mirror refusals carry no code and are **not** part of this contract: lowering the version below a feature the document already uses, and raising it past a ceiling a claim such as `pdfa -part 3` has fixed.

**TCLPDF FONT METRICS** *afm*

: A Type 1 font program was handed over without the metrics that go with it. The `.pfb` carries the outlines and the `.afm` carries the widths, and a PDF needs both; **font embed -metrics** names the second file. Promised in the description of **font embed** since the day `-data` was built, and part of the contract since 2026-08-25 — it was named in the prose and missing from this list, which is the one place that says which codes are contracts.

**TCLPDF TEXT FIT** *what*

: **text -fit** was given something it cannot fit into a box. *what* says which: `width` for the combination with **-width**, which asks for a paragraph and a fitted line at once; `box` for a box that is not two numbers, that is not finite, or that is too small for anything this package can write into it — below the five decimals a PDF number carries, the size would round to zero and the text would vanish without a word.

**TCLPDF TEXT VERTICAL** *what*

: A vertical line was asked for something that only reads along the horizontal axis. *what* is the option: `anchor` for **-anchor**, `fit` for **-fit** — the latter measures the width of a line and squeezes the letters with `Tz`, and in a column neither means anything.

**TCLPDF FIT ROTATE** *angle command*

: **-fit** and **-rotate** were given together. The box is upright and the picture is not, so the fitted extent and the drawn extent are different rectangles — measured, the rotated picture lands beside its box. *command* names the call, since the same refusal serves **image place**, **form place** and **svg**.

**TCLPDF SHADING PATTERN**

: A shading was asked to paint in a pattern colour space, which ISO 32000-2, 8.7.4.5.1 forbids it — a reader given one draws nothing at all. A shading is what a shading pattern is *made of*: build the gradient in device colours and register it with **shading pattern**.

**TCLPDF LEADER FILL** *fill*

: The fill character of a **leader** does not get longer the more copies it has — a **-spacing** more negative than the character is wide. The row would never reach the width asked for, and the search for the right number of copies would not end.

**TCLPDF FONT EMBED** *alias*

: **font embed** was called wrongly — with a path *and* **-data**, or with neither. Distinct from **TCLPDF FONT SOURCE** on purpose: that one says a reader turned the bytes away and invites a script to try the next reader, and a script that retried on this one would retry for ever, having handed over no font at all.

**TCLPDF FONT VERTICAL** *alias*

: A face was asked to set a vertical line and cannot: only a TrueType or OpenType face carries the vertical metrics and the `vert` feature that vertical writing is made of. Raised by the font module before any resource is registered, so that a refused vertical run leaves the document exactly as it was.

**TCLPDF XMP CHAR** *field codepoint*

: A value bound for the XMP packet carries a character XML does not allow — a C0 control such as `U+0000`, `U+0007` or `U+000B` (XML 1.0, 2.2). It is refused rather than repaired: dropping it would deliver a different title than the one given, and a replacement character would claim something was there. *field* names where it came from, so that the call can be found.

**TCLPDF XMP SURROGATE** *field codepoint*

: A value bound for the XMP packet carries an unpaired surrogate, which is not a character at all and has no UTF-8 form. Under Tcl 8.6 it used to reach the file as three bytes of CESU-8; under Tcl 9 it raised a bare Tcl error out of the encoder. Both roads now answer the same way.

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE TYPE** *vocabulary*

: The word that should have named a structure type is not one that can be used here. *vocabulary* names what it was measured against rather than the word itself: `structure` for the standard types of ISO 32000-1, 14.8.4 and Annex M, `artifact` and `subtype` for the artifact tables 330 and 331, `tag` for what **-tag** takes on a drawing call — a multi-word tag without `Artifact`, a grouping type that holds no text, an empty tag with no element open. A typo in a type name lands here, and the message names the full list.

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE PLACE** *rule*

: A type the standard knows, in a place it does not allow it — checked where the element is opened or closed, so that the message names the line rather than an object number later on. *rule* names the rule: `parent` (this type belongs under other parents), `child` (this parent takes other children), `inline` (markup with no surrounding text), `leaf` (a leaf element holds text and markup, not a block), `duplicate` (a Table holds at most one THead and one TFoot), `caption` (the Caption is not where it belongs), `sequence` (Ruby and Warichu hold a fixed sequence, ISO 32000-2, Table 369).

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE ATTRIBUTE** *option*

: An attribute of **structure** is wrong — wrong value, wrong form, or on a type that does not take it. *option* is the option without its dash: `lang`, `scope`, `numbering`, `bbox`, `colSpan`, `rowSpan`, `expansion`. Passing an option is the caller's doing, so the option and not the kind of mistake is what the code carries.

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE VERSION** *what*

: What was asked for exists only in a newer PDF version than the document's, and raising the version is the whole remedy — which is why this stands beside `TYPE` and `ATTRIBUTE` rather than inside them. *what* names what needs the version: `type` (a structure type from ISO 32000-2, Annex M), `attribute` (an attribute value from Table 369), `destination` (a structure destination, 12.3.2.3). **ua -part 2** raises the version with it.

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE NAME** *trouble namespace*

: An identifier. *trouble* is `duplicate` or `unknown`, and *namespace* says which one: `name` is this package's own handle, under which a link or a bookmark finds an element, `id` is the **/ID** of ISO 32000-1, 14.7.2. `unknown name` is the one refusal here that comes at the write: a link or a bookmark points at a name no element ever carried.

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE STATE** *call*

: Not the arguments refusing but the state of the document, and the remedy is always to do something *earlier*. *call* names the call concerned: `tagged` (**tagged 1** comes too late, content is already on a page), `expansion` (**-expansion** is read out of the structure tree and needs a tagged document), `destination` (a structure destination needs the same tree), `element` (elements were closed out of order).

**TCLPDF STRUCTURE ARGUMENT** *argument*

: The call itself is malformed, and no state of the document would make it right. *argument* names the guilty one: `script` for a missing **-script** on **structure**, `tagged` for **tagged** with more than one word or without a truth value.

**TCLPDF AFM** *what name*

: One of the fourteen standard fonts was asked for by a name it does not have. *what* is `family` for a family nobody carries, `style` for a family that has no such cut, `font` for a base name outside the fourteen; *name* is what was asked for. The message lists what there is — these are a closed set, so the list is short and complete.

**TCLPDF COLORFONT** *what*

: A **colorFont** call is incomplete. *what* is `alias` for a missing name, `source` for neither a path nor **-data**, `chars` for the missing **-chars** — a Type 3 font holds the glyphs it is told to hold, and there is no way to derive that list from a colour font.

# STANDARDS

These are the documents the package is written against, and — where a validator exists for one — the documents every release is measured against. A profile a document claims is checked on every run of `make check`: veraPDF for PDF/A and PDF/UA, Mustangproject for the electronic invoices, qpdf over every document, pdfsig over every signed one.

**The file itself**

: **ISO 32000-1:2008** (PDF 1.7) is what a document declares by default, and **ISO 32000-2:2020** (PDF 2.0) what `tclpdf new -version 2.0` declares. The version is a property of the document rather than a constant of the writer: encryption in the revision PDF 2.0 prescribes and a CAdES signature need 2.0, PDF/A-3 and therefore ZUGFeRD need 1.7.

**Archiving and accessibility**

: **ISO 19005-2** and **ISO 19005-3** for PDF/A parts 2 and 3, conformance levels B, U and A. **ISO 14289-1** (PDF/UA-1) and **ISO 14289-2** (PDF/UA-2), with **ISO/TS 32005** for the structure namespaces PDF/UA-2 requires, and the **WTPDF 1.0** profile of the PDF Association for well-tagged documents. The XMP packet follows **ISO 16684-1** (XMP part 1).

**Electronic invoices**

: **EN 16931** as the semantic model, in the syntax of **ZUGFeRD 2.3 / Factur-X 1.07** and **Order-X 1.0**, carried in a PDF/A-3 document as **ISO 19005-3** and the ZUGFeRD technical specification require. The attachment relationship follows Table 43 of ISO 32000-2.

**Signatures**

: **ETSI EN 319 142-1** (PAdES baseline profiles) and **ETSI EN 319 122-1** (CAdES), over **RFC 5652** (CMS) and **RFC 3161** (timestamp tokens). The signature dictionary itself is ISO 32000-2, 12.8.

**Fonts**

: **ISO/IEC 14496-22:2019** (Open Font Format, the ISO edition of OpenType) for the sfnt tables, the character map, GSUB and GPOS, the variable-font tables and `COLR`/`CPAL`. Beside it Adobe's own specifications for what that standard does not cover: the **Type 1 Font Format**, **Technical Note #5176** (the Compact Font Format), **Technical Note #5902** (PostScript name generation), the **Adobe Font Metrics** of the fourteen standard faces, and the **Adobe Glyph List**. Right-to-left text follows **UAX #9**, hyphenation Liang's algorithm in the pattern format of libhyphen.

**Pictures**

: **ISO/IEC 10918-1** (JPEG) with the **JFIF** and **Exif 2.32** application segments, the **PNG specification** (ISO/IEC 15948) including its predictors, and **TIFF 6.0** with **Technote 2** for JPEG-compressed strips. Colour follows the **ICC** specification for embedded profiles, and CIE **L\*a\*b\*** as ISO 32000-2, 8.6.5.4 states it.

**Language and country codes** follow **RFC 3066** where a document, a block or a hyphenation pattern set names a language.

# SEE ALSO

tdom, qpdf(1), veraPDF, pdffonts(1), pdftotext(1), tzint

tdom builds the XMP metadata packet — every document that declares PDF/A, PDF/UA or ZUGFeRD needs it — and, where present, parses SVG in place of the built-in parser; see "Optional packages" at the top. Its home is <https://tdom.org>.

tzint is a Tcl binding to the Zint barcode library, at <https://fossil.sowaswie.de/tzint>. It produces SVG, which `svg -data` draws — so barcodes need no code in this package and are not a dependency of it, but drawing one does need tzint installed. See the `Barcodes` section above.

### Every topic and its classes

Read as `TCLPDF <topic> <class> <facts…>`. A topic without classes puts the facts straight after its name. This list is generated from the source rather than kept by hand: `tests/errorcode.test` fails if a refusal appears without a code, and if a code appears whose first word is neither `TCLPDF` nor Tcl's own.

| `ATTACH` | `ARGUMENT` `NAME` `STATE` |

| `ZUGFERD` | `ARGUMENT` `NAME` `PROFILE` `RELATIONSHIP` `STATE` `TYPE` `XML` |

One refusal deliberately carries **no** `TCLPDF` code: an unknown subcommand of `tclpdf` itself answers with Tcl's own `TCL LOOKUP COMMAND`, in Tcl's own wording, so that a mistyped command behaves exactly as a mistyped command does everywhere else.
